<meta>This is the title page for a 1548 edition of alchemical dialogues. It introduces two major figures in the Western alchemical tradition: Geber and Ramon Llull.</meta>

### CONCERNING
# ALCHEMY:
## TWO
### DIALOGUES.

-> * <-

> *The first of these reveals and proves with reliable arguments the genuine meaning of the books of <term>Geber</term><note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–c. 815), a foundational figure in chemistry and alchemy. His works were often written in "decknamen" or "cover-names" to hide secrets from the uninitiated.</note>, which the author intentionally concealed and wrapped in metaphorical language. The second brings to light the mysteries of <term>Ramon Llull</term><note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316) was a Majorcan philosopher and mystic. While he likely didn't practice alchemy himself, a massive body of alchemical literature was attributed to him in the centuries following his death.</note> of Mallorca.*

These are preceded by one hundred and twenty-nine propositions that embrace the same subject with concise brevity, translated from the <note>original: "ex Tuscanico idiomate"</note> Tuscan dialect.

<image-desc>An elaborate printer's device within an ornamental cartouche. At the top is a figure with a radiant halo. In the center, two hands are clasped together (symbolizing a formal agreement or "the joining of right hands") holding a caduceus, a staff entwined with two serpents representing commerce and communication. Beneath the hands is a circular medallion containing the Latin motto "IN GOOD FAITH."</image-desc>

-> Printed at <term>Lyon</term><note>original: "Lugduni"</note> by the brothers Godefridus and Marcellus Bering, 1548. <-

<summary>This is the title page of a 1548 alchemical treatise printed in Lyon, featuring dialogues interpreting the cryptic works of Geber and Ramon Llull. It highlights the transition of alchemical knowledge from Italian (Tuscan) sources into the international scholarly language of the time.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Geber, Ramon Llull, Lyon printing, Renaissance science, occult philosophy, Jabir ibn Hayyan</keywords>

<meta>Catchword: Vitrio. This page begins the introductory propositions attributed to an anonymous author, summarizing the alchemical philosophy of Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan) and Raymond Lull.</meta>

# EXPOSITION

## OF THE BOOKS OF GEBER AND RAYMOND LULL.

->*One hundred and twenty-nine propositions of Geber and Lull, conducive to the correct understanding of their books, by an unknown author.*<-

->I.<-

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'G' featuring floral patterns and a bird perched among twisting vines and foliage within a square border.</image-desc>GEBER wrote his books ingeniously, in such a language that would by no means be hidden from the wise, yet would be most profound to those of average ability; for the ignorant and unskilled, however, he would lock both ends with an unopenable seal through one and the same tradition. <note>Alchemical writers often used "hermetic" or coded language to ensure their secrets only reached those they deemed worthy or intellectually prepared.</note>

2 ¶ This operation is brought to its conclusion at a low cost.

3 ¶ The principles of nature are also the principles of this <term>magistery|The 'Magistery' refers to the highest practice of alchemy: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone or the Great Work</term>.

4 ¶ He who does not possess a natural talent and a soul that subtly scrutinizes natural principles, the foundations of nature, and the crafts that can follow nature in the properties of its actions, will not find the true root of this most precious science.

5 ¶ There are three levels of difference among the natural principles of metals. For some are most remote, some are remote (or intermediate minerals), while others are immediate.

6 ¶ All metals are generated from sulfur and <term>vitriol|Vitriol refers to metallic sulfates, often characterized by their glassy appearance and acidic properties; it was considered a foundational substance in transmutation</term>.

7 ¶ <term>Quicksilver|Argentum vivum, or mercury</term> and common sulfur are not the principles of metals. <note>Alchemists often distinguished between the "common" materials found in shops and the "philosophical" or "sophic" versions of sulfur and mercury required for the Work.</note>

8 ¶ <term>First matter|Materia prima</term>, in this art, is said to be that which most closely approaches the metallic nature.

<summary>This page introduces a series of alchemical propositions intended to clarify the works of Geber and Raymond Lull, establishing that the "Magistery" follows natural laws and requires both intellectual depth and specific mineral principles like sulfur and vitriol.</summary>

<keywords>Geber, Raymond Lull, alchemy, Magistery, sulfur, vitriol, quicksilver, first matter, natural principles</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered propositions summarizing the alchemical theories of Geber and Raymond Lull. The previous page established that metals are generated from sulfur and vitriol.</meta>

9 ¶ Vitriol comes closest to the nature of metals because it is sulfurous and possesses an internal mineral power that allows it to be transformed into metal.

10 ¶ The mineral power for generating metals resides in washed sulfur; without this, metals can never be produced.

11 ¶ Those substances that possess the nature of salt and alum are merely soluble.

12 ¶ From dissolved vitriol, a twofold vapor is released; these two vapors are called Sulfur and Mercury by the Philosophers. <note>original: "Sulphur & Mercurius." In alchemical theory, these are not the common chemical elements, but rather the "philosophical" principles of stability and volatility that exist within all matter.</note>

13 ¶ Varieties of sulfur in metals acquire a power that is most direct for their transformation into metal.

14 ¶ We obtain various types of vitriol from "imperfect" <note>Base metals like lead, tin, or copper were considered "imperfect" because they were seen as being in a state of incomplete maturation compared to "perfect" gold.</note> metals, which are both useful and necessary for our work.

15 ¶ Prepared varieties of sulfur are the most suitable agents for converting their quicksilver into Mercury, gold, or silver.

16 ¶ When exhaling vapors fall upon a specific red earth, they are transformed into common <term>quicksilver|argentum uiuum; liquid mercury</term>.

17 ¶ When the twofold vapor penetrates through rocks and encounters sulfur, it dissolves the sulfur and bonds with it firmly. Through a continuous <term>decoction|decoctionem; a process of refining or "ripening" a substance through steady heat</term> within the mineral vein, it is transformed into metal.

18 ¶ All metals are generated from the same fundamental matter. The differences between them arise from the variety of the mineral location, different <term>accidents|accidentibus; non-essential properties like color or smell that do not change the core substance</term>, the qualities of the washed sulfur, and variations in their "digestion" or maturation process.

19 ¶ All superficial qualities that affect the root matter can be removed. To create the true <term>elixir|elixir; the agent of transmutation, often synonymous with the Philosopher's Stone</term>—which is a substance even more than perfect—it must necessarily be produced from nature's own seed.

20 ¶ The elements of the <term>Philosopher’s Stone|lapis Philoſophorum</term> have a nature that is close...

<summary>This page outlines the alchemical mechanism of metal formation, explaining how vapors, sulfur, and vitriol combine within the earth to create different metals. It emphasizes that while all metals share a common root, their differences result from the specific conditions of their "cooking" or maturation in the earth.</summary>
<keywords>vitriol, sulfur, philosophical mercury, quicksilver, decoction, elixir, Philosopher's Stone, metallic generation, imperfect metals</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered propositions regarding the composition and transformation of metals. It concludes the thought from the previous page concerning the proximity of certain substances to a metallic state before moving into the specific "Principles of the Art."</meta>

...nearness to being converted into metal, which by no means belongs to the elements of other things.

21 ¶ Common vitriol <note>original: "Vitriolum commune." Vitriol refers to sulfate minerals, often used as a starting material in alchemy.</note> is a remote principle from the Art <note>The "Art" is a standard term for alchemy itself.</note>.

22 ¶ The principles of the Art are not found in vegetable or living things, nor in those things derived from them, because they are foreign to the nature of metals.

23 ¶ Common sulfur and common <term>quicksilver|argentum uiuum; liquid mercury</term> are not the principles of nature; therefore, they shall not be the principles of the Art. <note>The author distinguishes between "common" minerals found in shops and the "philosophical" or "pure" principles required for transmutation.</note>

24 ¶ Sulfur alone can generate neither metal nor the <term>elixir|elixir; the "philosopher's stone" in liquid or powder form used to perfect base metals</term>.

25 ¶ He who knows how to prepare sulfur in such a way that it can be mixed and united with metals will hold the greatest secret of nature and has entered the short path to perfection.

26 ¶ The Elixir is made in two ways: either through <term>calcination|calcinationem; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder</term> and solution, or without "lime" <note>original: "calce." In this context, "calce" refers to the powder produced by calcination.</note> and distillation.

27 ¶ Quicksilver, when joined with its own sulfurs, can both be coagulated and made <term>fixed|fixum; a state where a substance can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating</term>.

28 ¶ Quicksilver is not made fixed by leaves or herbs alone.

29 ¶ Our quicksilver—or our Mercury—is a salt, possessing the power of true lime.

30 ¶ Mercury, or the quicksilver of metals, is converted into salt through the process of calcination.

31 ¶ Within metallic bodies, there are two sulfurous qualities.

32 ¶ Our arsenic partakes of both sulfur and quicksilver, which is why they call it the <term>Hermaphrodite|Hermaphroditon</term>; however, it cannot generate metal or the Elixir by itself. <note>In alchemical symbolic language, "Hermaphrodite" describes a substance containing both the active/masculine principle (Sulfur) and the passive/feminine principle (Mercury).</note>

33 ¶ <term>Tutia|Tutia; zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</term> is the white smoke of our <term>Jupiter|Iouis; the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term>; for this reason, it imparts a lemon-yellow color to copper. <note>The addition of zinc (from tutty/calamine) to copper creates brass, which mimics the color of gold.</note>

34 ¶ Sulfur and quicksilver are made fixed through conversion into earth, and also without this conversion.

<summary>This page lists specific alchemical axioms (propositions 21-34) regarding the "Principles of the Art," emphasizing that true alchemy requires mineral-based substances rather than organic ones. It identifies the "philosophical" versions of mercury, sulfur, and arsenic as the true components for creating the Elixir.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, sulfur, quicksilver, mercury, vitriol, elixir, calcination, tutia, tin, copper, arsenic, hermaphrodite, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered list of alchemical axioms or "principles" regarding the nature of sulfur, the relationship between metals, and the composition of the philosopher's stone. It transitions from the physical preparation of substances to the theoretical foundations of transmutation.</meta>

35 ¶ Sulfur is <term>calcined|calcinatur; heated until it is reduced to a powder</term> and washed with its own salt and vinegar.

36 ¶ Our <term>Jupiter|Iupiter; the alchemical name for tin</term> and <term>Saturn|Saturnus; the alchemical name for lead</term> are salts joined together before distillation. <note>Alchemists used planetary names as code for metals; tin was associated with Jupiter and lead with Saturn.</note>

37 ¶ In its preparation, sulfur creates several skins or films upon itself.

38 ¶ Under the name of <term>Marchasite|Marchaſitæ; a term used for various metallic minerals, often iron pyrite</term>, Geber speaks of the <term>philosopher’s stone|lapis Philoſophorum</term>. <note>original: "Geber." Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century scholar whose writings formed the foundation of medieval alchemy.</note>

39 ¶ The vessels used for <term>putrefaction|putrefactionis; the "blackening" or breaking down of matter</term> and the preparation of sulfur are similar and the same; that is, they are flat at the bottom.

40 ¶ The principles of the Art <note>The "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy.</note> are found within metals.

41 ¶ The Elixir and the philosopher's stone are vegetable, animal, and mineral. <note>Alchemists often claimed the Stone was "universal," containing the essence of all three kingdoms of nature.</note>

42 ¶ It is necessary that a metallic medicine be found which can <term>transmute|tranſmutare; to change one substance into another</term> imperfect metals into true gold and silver.

43 ¶ Metals can be most perfectly transmuted, one into another.

44 ¶ Art for the most part surpasses the operation of nature. <note>A common alchemical claim: while nature takes centuries to "ripen" metals in the earth, the alchemist can accelerate the process in the lab.</note>

45 ¶ The entire mastery of this Art consists in one single stone.

46 ¶ In the generation of metals and the elixir, sulfur is like the paternal seed, while <term>quicksilver|Argentum uiuum; mercury</term> is like the feminine menstrual fluid. <note>This describes the "Sulfur-Mercury theory," which posited that all metals were born from the metaphorical marriage of these two principles.</note>

47 ¶ The philosopher's stone contains within itself every natural preparation and everything that is required for perfection.

48 ¶ The Elixir, <term>potable gold|aurum potabile; a liquid form of gold intended for medicinal use</term>, the <term>quintessence|quinta eſſentia; the "fifth essence" or purest spirit of a substance</term>, and the seed are all made from the same material.

49 ¶ The opinion of some was that they wished the elixir to be made from common gold.

50 ¶ The elixir cannot be made from common gold, because <meta>catchword: its/his [ipſius]</meta>

<summary>This page outlines core alchemical theories, emphasizing the "Sulfur-Mercury" theory of metallic generation and the belief that alchemy can surpass natural processes. It identifies the philosopher's stone as a universal "metallic medicine" derived from a single material.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur, Jupiter, Saturn, Geber, Marchasite, transmutation, Sulfur-Mercury theory, potable gold, quintessence, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered aphorisms or principles of alchemy, following the previous discussion on the nature of metals and their composition.</meta>

Its own <term>sulfur</term><note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of a substance.</note> has now reached the final and permanent <term>tincture</term><note>A "tincture" is a potent substance capable of infusing its color and properties into another body.</note>, and its tincture does not exceed what was necessary for itself. For this reason, gold is said to be both complete and determined, and it cannot be properly reduced back to its <term>prime matter</term><note>The original, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made.</note> (even though that would be most necessary).

51 ¶ The <term>radical moisture</term><note>The essential, life-giving fluid believed to sustain the existence of metals and living beings.</note> in other metals is just as virtuous, incorruptible, and incombustible as that of gold.

52 ¶ In the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term>, gold and silver exist in potential and in power.

53 ¶ By "perfect bodies," <term>Geber</term><note>Referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most famous authority of early Arabic alchemy.</note> understands species of prepared sulfur.

54 ¶ The "Gold of the Philosophers" and "potable gold" are the most subtle radical sulfur, which is called the **soul**.

55 ¶ The white smoke, carrying the red smoke in its belly, is the true <term>quintessence</term><note>The "fifth essence," a purified state of matter beyond the four elements of earth, air, fire, and water.</note>.

56 ¶ The quintessence possesses no elemental quality in its actual state.

57 ¶ The Philosophers' "water of life" descends from the Philosophers' Stone.

58 ¶ Sulfur, by means of fire, sweetens bitter water.

59 ¶ Our **Heaven** must be adorned with our **Sun** and **stars**.

60 ¶ Our quintessence is called by various names of destructive and ferocious animals, such as the bear, the lion, and the like.

61 ¶ Coagulating and fixing waters are two species of sulfur, designated by the names of **Venus** <note>Copper</note> and **Mars** <note>Iron</note>.

62 ¶ By the name of "glass," sulfur is often understood in this art.

63 ¶ There is a difference between the coagulation of the quintessence and the fixed <term>elixir</term>; for they produce different effects.

<summary>This page lists a series of alchemical axioms (numbered 51-63) defining the nature of sulfur, the quintessence, and the symbolic names used for metallic principles. It emphasizes that gold is a "complete" body and explains the metaphorical language used by authorities like Geber.</summary>

<keywords>sulfur, tincture, gold, radical moisture, Philosophers' Stone, Geber, quintessence, water of life, Venus, Mars, elixir, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
64 ¶ In the drinkable medicine <note>original: "medicina potabili"; refers to the legendary universal cure or liquid gold</note>, the animal part is contained.
65 ¶ In every metal, the individual metals of the Philosophers <note>the "Philosophers" refers to the ancient alchemists</note> are contained.
66 ¶ In this art, it is necessary for the hidden to be made manifest, and the manifest to be hidden.
67 ¶ When it is commanded that bodies are to be dissolved into water, the species of sulfur are understood.
68 ¶ Besides sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum"; mercury</note>, there are no other metals of the Philosophers.
69 ¶ Art imitates nature in many things.
70 ¶ Red sulfur, like wine, when it turns black through calcination <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile substances</note>, is said to be blacker than black.
71 ¶ From the color black to whiteness, many and varied colors appear in the sulfur.
72 ¶ The Elixir first becomes black, secondly it is whitened, afterwards it assumes a lemon-yellow color, and truly at the last, red.
73 ¶ The Elixir is coagulated in the form of an egg.
74 ¶ The blackness lasts for forty days.
75 ¶ The ancients, under the veil of poetic fables, hid this art.
76 ¶ Under the fable of Hercules and Antaeus <note>a myth where Hercules defeats a giant who gains strength from touching the earth</note>, they hid the preparation of sulfur.
77 ¶ The ancients said that Jupiter turned himself into a golden rain; by this fable, they were hiding the distillation of philosophical gold.
78 ¶ By the eyes of Argus <note>the hundred-eyed giant of Greek myth</note> turned into the tail of a Peacock, they signified our sulfur, which changes from color to color.
79 ¶ Under the fable of Orpheus, they hid the sweetness of the quintessence <note>the "fifth element" or purest essence of a substance</note> and of drinkable gold.
80 ¶ If we believe Empedocles <note>a Greek pre-Socratic philosopher</note>, the ancients hid the entire practice of this art under the fable of Pyrrha and Deucalion <note>the Greek myth of the Great Flood, where the survivors created a new race of humans by throwing stones</note>.

<meta>The catchword "Sub" indicates the next page begins with this word, continuing the list of mythological allegories.</meta>

<summary>This page lists twenty aphorisms (64-80) defining alchemical principles and explaining how ancient Greek myths serve as secret allegories for chemical processes. It emphasizes the "Great Work" through the stages of color changes and the use of sulfur and mercury.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, sulfur, mercury, Elixir, calcination, Hercules, Jupiter, Argus, Orpheus, quintessence, drinkable gold, allegory, mythology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of alchemical allegories, specifically linking classical mythology to chemical processes. The previous page likely began this list of "hidden" meanings.</meta>

<margin>81</margin> ¶ Under the fable of the Gorgon <note>Medusa, whose gaze turned men to stone</note>, who turned all who looked upon her into stone, they hid the secret of the fixation <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to fire</term> of the elixir.

<margin>82</margin> ¶ Hiding the process of distillation, they said that Jupiter, having turned into an Eagle, flew up into the heavens with Ganymede.

<margin>83</margin> ¶ Under the fable of Daedalus and Icarus, they concealed the processes of putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the breakdown or "death" of a substance, often turning it black, before its purification</term> and distillation.

<margin>84</margin> ¶ They hid the distillation of the Gold of the Philosophers <note>original: "auri Philosophorum"; not common gold, but the perfected substance of the Great Work</note> by saying that when a golden branch is cut off, another immediately grows in its place, likewise made of gold.

<margin>85</margin> ¶ They also concealed this distillation by saying that Jupiter cut off the genitals of his father, Saturn.

<margin>86</margin> ¶ The Mercurial Water <note>the liquid solvent used in alchemy</note> is the chariot of Phaëthon.

<margin>87</margin> ¶ By the armored Minerva, they understood that distilled water which contains a portion of the most subtle sulfur, which sulfur is called "iron."

<margin>88</margin> ¶ By Vulcan, who follows Minerva, is signified the sulfur that follows this water, and its own salt during the process of putrefaction.

<margin>89</margin> ¶ By the thick cloud with which Jupiter surrounded Io, is signified the thin skin or film appearing during the coagulation <term>coagulation: the thickening of a liquid into a solid</term> of the elixir.

<margin>90</margin> ¶ The black films appearing during the calcination <term>calcination: heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to powder</term> of sulfur are the black sails with which Theseus returned to Athens.

<margin>91</margin> ¶ Under the name of the flood and the generation of animals, they described distillation and the generation of sulfur.

<margin>92</margin> ¶ By Mars they signified our sulfur, and by Juno the element of air, and sometimes the element of earth.

<margin>93</margin> ¶ By Latona, compressed <note>embraced or impregnated</note> by Jupiter on the island of Delos,

<summary>This page provides a systematic decoding of Greek and Roman myths as metaphors for specific alchemical laboratory procedures like distillation, fixation, and calcination. It identifies gods and heroes—such as Minerva, Vulcan, and Theseus—as symbols for chemical elements and physical reactions.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, mythology, distillation, sulfur, elixir, fixation, Minerva, Jupiter, symbolic interpretation</keywords>

<meta>The catchword "Ex" from the previous page leads into the discussion of chemical symbols and the preparation of the Elixir.</meta>

They signified our copper, which, when placed in the glass vessel <note>original: "boccia"; a round-bodied chemical flask</note>, generates the Sun and the Moon <note>Gold and Silver</note>; they hid the preparation of the sulfur, saying that Vulcan <note>The god of fire, representing the heat of the furnace</note> was cast down onto the island of Lemnos because of his deformity.

94 ¶ Atalanta <note>original: "Atalantos"</note>, that is our swiftest and lightest water, is both coagulated and made firm with sulfurs.
95 ¶ The lumps <note>original: "Boli"</note> with which Theseus ensnared the mouth of the Minotaur are species of sulfur. In the Labyrinth—that is, in our vessel or flask—they ensnare the mercurial water.
96 ¶ By the Phoenix, which is always reborn, they understood the multiplication of the elixir.
97 ¶ Under the name and fable of Demogorgon <note>A primordial deity associated with the underworld and the origin of the gods</note>, they hid the matter and the practice of this art.
98 ¶ The Chaos of the ancients is our Saturn <note>Lead, or the raw, dark prime matter of the work</note>.
99 ¶ The elixir cannot be made from silver.
100 ¶ The elixir is made from imperfect metals.
101 ¶ Imperfect metals are intermediate minerals.
102 ¶ The opinion of those who made the elixir from common lead.
103 ¶ The opinion of certain people affirming that the elixir must be made from tin.
104 ¶ The elixir can be made neither from tin nor from common lead, since they are unclean at their root.
105 ¶ The first order is of preparation, the second is of fixation, and the third is of the multiplication of the elixir.
106 ¶ From the burnt dregs <note>original: "fecibus adustis"</note> our Mercury is extracted, with which the multiplication is performed.
107 ¶ Copper and common iron are pure and clean in their root.
108 ¶ The elixir ought not to be made from common copper.
109 ¶ The ore of our copper is a salt, called Saturn.
110 ¶ The opinion that the elixir cannot be made from common iron.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical materials, specifically focusing on the properties of iron as a primary ingredient for the Elixir. Catchword from previous page: "turam" (likely part of "naturam").</meta>

# Page 11

<margin>111</margin> ¶ From the tradition of Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemy</note> and all the Philosophers, it is further proven that the elixir is made from common iron.

<margin>112</margin> ¶ The vessel for the distillation of Mercury <note>In alchemy, Mercury often refers to the "spirit" or "quicksilver" of a metal</note> ought to be similar to a flat table or have a small concavity.

<margin>113</margin> ¶ The elixir is made from common iron because it possesses more fixed sulfur than other metals, and because its spirits are more virtuous, and because its earthiness is easily separated from it, and it is easily reduced to the "first matter" <note>materia prima: the primitive, formless base of all substance</note>. Furthermore, the prepared Mercury of this iron defends its sulfur better from combustion; it is an incomplete and middle mineral in which the extremes are contained in virtue, and it is the ore of "philosophical gold."

<margin>114</margin> ¶ The ancient Philosophers signified through various riddles and metaphors that the elixir is composed of iron.

<margin>115</margin> ¶ The ancients called iron "Man," because it possesses a soul, a body, and a spirit.

<margin>116</margin> ¶ Iron is generated in the earth specifically through the power of the Pole Star <note>stella polaris: the North Star, often associated with the magnetic properties of iron</note>.

<margin>117</margin> ¶ That stone which is sold at a cheap price is iron, if you compare it to the other metals.

<margin>118</margin> ¶ Iron is called "vegetable" <note>In this context, "vegetable" refers to the power of growth or transformation, not a plant</note>.

<margin>119</margin> ¶ The elixir is made from the cheapest stone.

<margin>120</margin> ¶ The cheapest stone of the Philosophers is vegetable, animal, and mineral.

<margin>121</margin> ¶ The separation of the elements.

<margin>122</margin> ¶ The iron dross <note>original: "spuma," literally foam or scum</note> which is cast aside by blacksmiths is the cheapest stone of the Philosophers.

<margin>123</margin> ¶ The radical moisture <note>the essential life-fluid of a substance</note> of metals is not consumed by fire.

<margin>124</margin> ¶ From the writings of Geber, it is proven that the spirits must be sublimated from the calx <note>the powdery residue left after heating a metal</note> of iron.

<margin>125</margin> ¶ The calx of iron, converted by the vehemence of fire into na— <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely "naturam" or nature</note>

<summary>This page argues that common iron is the true "Philosopher's Stone" due to its unique spiritual and physical properties, citing the authority of Geber and ancient allegories. It describes iron as a "middle mineral" containing the potential for gold and linked to the influence of the North Star.</summary>

<keywords>iron, Geber, elixir, mercury, sulfur, first matter, Pole Star, Philosopher's Stone, calx, radical moisture</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical transmutations, specifically focusing on the relationship between iron and the nature of glass as a stage in the Great Work.</meta>

...the nature of glass, colored as if green, or a dark sky-blue, or a dark sapphire, is the most virtuous Stone of the Philosophers. <note>original: "lapis philosophorum." The "Stone" refers to the goal of alchemy, a substance capable of perfecting matter.</note>

126 ¶ This glass is the radical moisture <term>radical moisture: the essential, life-sustaining fluid believed to exist within all matter</term> of iron; it is proven by various opinions of the Philosophers that the Stone of the Philosophers is not consumed by fire.

127 ¶ Iron is cleansed of much earthiness when it is reduced into the nature of glass.

128 ¶ The ancient Philosophers used to convert glass back into a metallic nature.

129 ¶ Glass is the "greater cheap stone," well-known and famous, etc. <note>The "vile" or "cheap" stone is a common alchemical paradox, suggesting the material for the Great Work is common and overlooked by the ignorant.</note>

->T H E  E N D.<-

<summary>This concluding section describes the transformation of iron into a sapphire-colored glass, identifying this "radical moisture" as a key stage in creating the non-combustible Philosopher's Stone. It emphasizes the purification of metal through vitrification, a process the author claims was reversible by ancient masters.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, vitrification, iron, radical moisture, purification, sapphire, ancient philosophy</keywords>

# DIALOGUE
## THE FIRST, EXPLAINING
### THE TRUE AND GENUINE
### MEANING OF THE BOOKS
### OF GEBER.

---

->DEMOGORGON. GEBER.<-

DEMOGORGON. Greetings, most wise descendant of the great Muhammad <note>original: "magni Machumetis... nepos." During my travels, I heard countless craftsmen <note>The Latin "artifices" refers here to alchemists or practical laboratory workers.</note> asserting with rash daring that they had understood your intention and the truest meaning of your writings to a tee <note>original: "ad unguem," literally "to the fingernail," a Roman idiom for perfect precision.</note>. However, having achieved absolutely nothing except labor and heavy expense, they cast all the blame upon you. But I, out of the singular love I have always reverently felt for you and the great esteem in which I have held you, did not hesitate to defend your reputation and honor against the futile and empty slanders and bites of those most impudent detractors. For I knew that you are preferred by all students of true Philosophy over all others who have attempted this most subtle part of the secret Philosophy <note>The "secret philosophy" refers to Alchemy.</note>, both among the ancients and the more recent authors. Then, seized by a supreme desire to see you and to discuss these matters in person, I diverted my journey from Italy here to Persia, chiefly so that I might obtain from you the favor of not refusing to receive me into the fellowship and number of your disciples. For the love and zeal for learning so prevailed within me that neither the length of the journey nor the many dangers to be undergone seemed able to deter me; rather, I thought these things should be bravely despised.

**GEBER:** You ask for honorable things indeed, and your desire is a most certain sign of a noble mind. Therefore, I shall not find it burdensome to receive you into the number of both my disciples and my friends. However, you would do me a far from ungrateful service if you would point out the arguments of those who strive to detract from my reputation with vain slanders.

**DEMOGORGON:** <note>The speaker "DEM." is likely Demogorgon, a figure often associated with primordial knowledge in alchemical dialogues.</note> A heavy dispute of words has often arisen between us and those wicked detractors of your fame and name, who bitterly accused and vituperated you, proclaiming that they had been deluded, deceived, and tripped up by your writings, because you wrote to this effect in the preface of the *Book of the Sum of Perfection*: <note>The "Summa perfectionis" is the most famous work attributed to Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan) in the Latin West.</note> "By God, he who shall have worked according to this book..."

...[if they had followed the instructions], they would rejoice to have found the true end of this art. For this reason, they curse, speak ill, find fault, and make no end of spreading insulting reproaches, shouting that you are a forger, an impostor, and a crafty, deceitful man, whose only heart's desire was to make as many people as possible "rationally insane." <note>original: "cum ratione insanos" — a paradoxical phrase suggesting a madness that follows a logical but false path.</note>

**GEBER:** Who, I ask, has ever lived among men so divine and wise that he did not, in some part, offend the wicked and the malicious?

**DEMOCRITUS:** But what do you say about the many very wise men, of almost blameless life, who are involved in the same error?

**GEBER:** We wrote in the first book, chapter 7 of our *Summa* <note>Referring to the "Summa Perfectionis Magisterii," the most influential medieval Latin work on alchemy.</note>: "This art is kept in the divine power of God, who withholds it or bestows it upon whom He wills, He who is glorious, and sublime, and filled with all justice and goodness." To this we also added at the end of this book, that we have handed down this art with such craftsmanship <term>artificio: the deliberate use of coded language or "Decknamen"</term> that it can be understood by no one, unless it be granted to him by divine gift, or he has been instructed in it by us, who handed it down.

**DEMOCRITUS:** What was the need to write or publish those things which would be understood by no one?

# DIALOGUE

<margin>Authorities on minerals</margin>
...opinion, they follow Aristotle, Avicenna, Albertus Magnus, and Saint Thomas Aquinas, in their books on minerals and meteorology. <note>Referring to the *Meteorologica* and various medieval commentaries on the formation of the earth.</note>

**DEMOGORGON.** I greatly desire to understand and learn now in what way earth, mixed with water, can be transmuted or converted into metal.

**GEBER.** The Sun and all the stars—both the fixed stars and the wandering planets, <note>original: "erraticae"</note> borrowing their light from the Sun—constantly heat the earth above, below, inside, and out, by their circular motion, the reflection of their rays, and their subtle, most powerful force of penetration. Since every burned or cooked thing is easily corrupted and changed, and the corruption of one thing is the generation of another, <note>A fundamental principle of Aristotelian physics: *corruptio unius est generatio alterius*.</note> it is necessary that earth mixed with water, having been scorched in this way for a long time and thereby transmuted, is finally converted into another earthy nature. Thus we see wood and stones, when strongly burned, are converted into ash and lime.

**DEMOGORGON.** Into what earthy nature is the earth transmuted when it is scorched in this way?

**GEBER.** It is converted into a type of earth that has something of the substance of salt or alum within it. For we are taught this by experience. We perceive that ashes, lime, sweat, urine, and seawater cooked by the Sun contain saltiness within them. Therefore, we said at the beginning of the testament that salt can be made from every burned thing.

**DEMOGORGON.** By what name is that salt of yours commonly called, which is hidden in this scorched earth?

**GEBER.** It is commonly called **Vitriol**. <note>term: Vitriol. A sulfate mineral, often green or blue, considered by alchemists to be a primary ingredient in the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

**DEMOGORGON.** Oho! Are metals therefore generated from vitriol?

**GEBER.** Indeed they are; the generation of all metals comes from vitriol and sulfur.

**DEMOGORGON.** How do you prove this, so that I may have faith in your words?

**GEBER.** It is certain that every thing is resolved back into that from which it was composed. Therefore, if you know how to resolve metals into their own primary matter, they will undoubtedly be reduced back into vitriol, which we have also called **"dry water."** <note>original: "aquam siccam"</note>

Therefore, in the *Book of Investigation*, in the chapter on vitriol <note>original: "vitriolo"; iron or copper sulfates used as a primary reagent in alchemy</note>, we have asserted with certainty that different species of vitriol can be separated from different metals. These will be of the greatest use to us and are highly necessary in the operation of this highest art. We confirmed that same point in Book 3, chapter 7.

**DEMOGORGON.** It seems to me (to frankly confess what I feel) that there is no small contradiction in your words.

**GEBER.** What contradiction are you telling me of?

**DEMOGORGON.** In many places you have taught, and in your writings you have affirmed, that sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note> are the true natural principles of all metals. Because you now attribute this to vitriol, it makes me not a little suspicious.

**GEBER.** We have sufficiently proved in Chapter 12 of Book 1 that quicksilver and sulfur are not the primary principles of metals. If you understand that passage correctly, you will recognize that I clearly taught that these are by no means the true principles of metals in their own nature, but rather something else, which follows from the alteration of their substance in the natural root into an earthy substance.

**DEMOGORGON.** This speech is obscure; I do not understand.

**GEBER.** You remember, no doubt, that I have already said that earth mixed with water is the root and foundation of all metals?

**DEMOGORGON.** I remember.

**GEBER.** However, this alteration of theirs into an earthy substance occurs by no means in the essence or substance of common sulfur or common quicksilver. Instead, it happens in their root—namely, in the earth mixed with water—which is cooked <note>original: "decocta"; the alchemical process of 'digestion' or ripening through heat</note> and transmuted by mineral and celestial heat, as we have shown openly enough.

**DEMOGORGON.** Now, if you please, speak a few words also concerning the "mineral mediums" <term>mineral mediums: substances that sit between raw elements and finished metals in the alchemical hierarchy</term> and the remote principles.

**GEBER.** The mineral medium, from which all metals, and quicksilver, and Marcasite <note>likely iron pyrites</note> and Antimony are generated, is

-># DIALOGUE<-

...that very same vitriol <note>original: "uitriolum"; a term for sulfate minerals, often green or blue, considered essential in alchemy</note> which we said was hidden in the earth, mixed with water, or boiled and burnt away. And when metals must be reduced into their own first matter <note>original: "materiam primam"; the primal, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made</note>—that is, as close as they can possibly approach the metallic nature—they must be reduced to the nature of vitriol and sulfur.

**DE M.** <note>Likely "Democritus," a common persona in alchemical dialogues</note> Why are metals generated more from this vitriol than from any other sulfur or alum?

**GE B.** <note>Likely "Geber," the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the famous alchemist</note> Because it is more sulfurous and approaches closer to the nature of metal. From celestial influence <note>original: "cœlesti influxu"; the belief that stars and planets direct the growth of minerals underground</note>, its virtue is also more capable and ready to be converted into metal, provided the mineral site is consenting and promoting the process with sufficient sulfur and heat.

**DE M.** What "mineral site" are you telling me about?

**GE B.** The rock, in which the mineral virtue of hardening and fixing is contained.

**DE M.** What is this mineral virtue?

**GE B.** This virtue is celestial, having the power of procreating metals, hidden in a certain washed sulfur that is bright like silver.

**DE M.** I have known no sulfur that is washed and bright.

**GE B.** Have you not noticed at some time, mixed among the sand and rocks dug out of the earth, certain shining scales similar to silver?

**DE M.** I have noticed them often, but I believed the substance to be silver itself.

**GE B.** You are mistaken. It is sulfur, washed by nature, boiled down, and partially fixed <note>original: "fixum"; rendered solid or non-volatile by chemical process</note>, possessing in itself the nature, splendor, and metallic fusion. Without this, metals can in no way be generated. Wherever it abounds in plenty, there metals are generated, as Albertus de Metheo <note>likely a reference to Albertus Magnus and his work "De Mineralibus"</note> testifies to the same.

**DE M.** Is vitriol therefore born among the rocks?

**GE B.** Not at all, but in the earth.

**DE M.** How then shall it enter the rocks, so that it may find the aforementioned "washed sulfur" in that same place?

**GE B.** Metal-bearing mountains are not stony in their depths, just as they are on the surface...

# BOOK ONE

<margin>+</margin> Therefore, miners, when they find earth and alum <note>original: "alumen"; likely referring to a group of sulfate minerals</note> while digging—specifically vitriol <note>original: "uitriolum"; iron or copper sulfates, often called "copperas"</note>—do not descend any deeper, but rather abandon those mines or metallic veins. For they know that metals are lacking there. Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist</note> asserts the same in his 33rd book. It happens, however, that sometimes not far from such failing veins or mines, others may be found that do contain metal. Since we can sufficiently prove from the operations of nature (as we clearly demonstrated in Book 2, Chapter 15) that only those things which have the nature of salt, alum, and the like are soluble, it will be manifest that even this vitriol of ours can be dissolved by the power of subterranean heat. Once dissolved, it exhales a double smoke (as I said in Book 1, Chapter 12). Because heat is capable of mixing and most strongly unites, binds, and fastens together the subtle earthy part and the watery moisture when they are digested <note>term: "digesta"; a process of slow heating or maturation in alchemy</note> together. Heavenly heat, however, by its nature attracting the subtle watery moisture, also draws away something of the subtle earthy part. Aristotle affirmed this in his 3rd book of *Meteorology* <note>original: "Metheororum"</note> with these words, when he says:

> that the enclosing moist vapor and the enclosed dry vapor are raised upward together.

For this happens, as Galen and Avicenna <note>referring to the famous Greek physician and the Persian polymath</note> affirm, through heat elevating both vapors. Because heat is always capable of mixing and causes the movement of one essence into the essence of another. These two smokes or vapors are called **sulfur** and **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum uiuum"; mercury</note> by the Philosophers. Since the earthy, subtle, oily, and somewhat digested vapor is the essential matter of sulfur—although we have called it by the name of **Arsenic**. The watery, viscous moisture, mixed with the subtle earthy part, is the immediate matter of quicksilver; Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus, 13th-century scholar</note> affirms this in Book 3 of *On Minerals*, Chapter 4. Therefore, in the generation of metals, two [types] of sulfur flow together...

types of sulfur, namely, sulfur released from vitriol <note>Vitriol refers to sulfate minerals, often used here to describe metallic salts</note>, and that which you call "washed sulfur," found in rocks and <margin>GEBER Chapter 12, Book 1</margin> mineral deposits. Therefore, you will not err if you call them either vitriols or atraments. Sulfur that is generated in stone is composed of sulfur and stone; but that which is released from vitriol is composed of salt and sulfur. In the latter lies the mineral power of certain liquefiable bodies which are generated from sulfur of this kind. However, sulfur generated in stone does not dissolve because it does not have the nature of salt. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the two types of sulfur and their role in the formation of metals and atmospheric phenomena, following the previous page's introduction to mineral vapors.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

<margin>The nature of sulfur before preparation</margin>
is, before preparation, green in color, like common vitriol <note>original: "vitriolum commune"; a sulfate mineral often used in alchemy and dyeing</note>. And within the metal, it has acquired a copper-like virtue and the properties of bronze, which is why it is called "Bronze," and "Venus," and "Verdigris" <note>original: "viride aeris"</note>. These two species of sulfur can be extracted from metals by artifice. However, since that sulfur called "iron" is whitened during preparation—just as silver is—and is deprived of all combustible oiliness, **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose medical and alchemical works were foundational in Europe</note> considered this the most suitable substance to be converted into its own quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note>. On the other hand, when the "green sulfur" is rendered pure and clear through preparation, taking on a reddish hue and containing a fiery (yet non-burning) virtue, he considered this most suitable for chemists <note>original: "Chymistis"</note> in the making of gold; thus, it is called "Gold."

**THE DISCIPLE:** I am eager to hear in what manner this double vapor is able to penetrate even stones and rocks.

**GEBER:** <note>The speaker is likely the Latinized persona of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the legendary father of chemistry</note> These two vaporous exhalations, drawn upward by celestial heat, behave differently depending on their location. If they find an open terrestrial space from which they can exhale their dark mist, they are drawn into the air. There, they are converted into comets or falling stars, or the circles appearing around the Sun and Moon <note>halos</note>, or into the rainbow, or into winds, thunder, lightning, clouds, mists, hail, snow, frost, blight, and similar appearances in the air, just as **Aristotle** taught in his *Meteorology* and all other philosophers have attested.

However, if the location is narrow and so constricted that no exit is open for the natural heat or the double smoke, then these two vaporous exhalations (or double smoke) begin to thicken and multiply. They are dispersed through the mineral site, seeking an exit everywhere through small cracks, as **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and bishop who wrote extensively on the natural sciences</note> taught in his *Book of Minerals*. These vapors, thus enclosed in stony places, are called "intermediate minerals" or the "remote matter of metals," as **Saint Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), who commented on Aristotle's works</note> taught at the end of the third book of *Meteorology*.

**THE DISCIPLE:** It remains now for you to reveal your opinion to me regarding the immediate principles: how these two vapo-

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the subterranean formation of metals, specifically discussing the role of vapors and the influence of the environment on the final substance. The catchword from the previous page was "books."</meta>

->DIALOGUE<-

things, penetrating into the rock, are converted into metal?

GEBER. When those vapors, in penetrating the stone, do not find <term>washed sulfur<note>original: "sulphur lotum"; in alchemy, this refers to sulfur that has been purified or "cleansed" of its combustible, foul-smelling impurities to serve as a pure principle of matter.</note></term>, the stone is merely stained with various colors, and no metal is generated there. However, if this smoke, or vapor, should fall into some part of the rock and cannot descend deeper into another place, it settles there and creates a well of water which will never be dried up. But if this vapor or smoke falls into red earth, it is converted there into common <term>quicksilver<note>original: "argentum uiuum"; mercury.</note></term>. Thus, by penetrating the rocks, if it finds washed sulfur, it dissolves that which was almost fixed and mixes itself with it. For this reason, I said in Book 1, Chapter 12, that the water which flows through the passages of the earth finds a substance dissolvable by the earthy substance—namely, washed sulfur, which is the fatness of the earth—and dissolves it, joining itself uniformly to it so that from both one natural substance is made. This mixture, through successive <term>decoction<note>original: "decoctionem"; a process of heating or "cooking" within the earth over long periods.</note></term> in the <term>ore-vein<note>original: "minera"; the mineral bed or mine where metals grow.</note></term>, is thickened and hardened, and becomes metal. If, however, it happens at times that I rebuke the opinions of others, I rebuke them with a simple letter. For we have often placed first that which ought to have been placed last.

DEMOCRITUS. If all metals are generated from sulfur and <term>vitriol<note>original: "uitriolo"; metallic sulfates, often associated here with the acidic or transformative principle.</note></term>, transformed by long decoction into another substance of sulfur and quicksilver, from where then does such a diverse variety of them arise? GEBER. From the diversity of the mineral site, and from the <term>accidents<note>original: "accidentia"; in Aristotelian philosophy, these are non-essential qualities (like color or hardness) that happen to a substance without changing its core nature.</note></term> which come upon the primary mineral matter; and furthermore, from the diversity of the washed sulfur and the different heat which digests the metallic matter in various ways. DEMOCRITUS. Can these intervening accidents be removed? GEBER. All accidents coming upon the primary metallic matter can be separated by artifice, as I clearly showed in the preface of the *Book of Investigation*, in the chapter on the preparation of <term>sharpest vinegar<note>original: "acetum acerrimum"; a potent alchemical solvent, often distilled vinegar used for purifying ores.</note></term>, and at the end of the aforementioned

<summary>Geber explains to Democritus how subterranean vapors combine with "washed sulfur" to form metals through heat and pressure, attributing the variety of metals to environmental "accidents." He asserts that these impurities can be removed through alchemical artifice, such as the use of strong acids.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Democritus, alchemy, sulfur-mercury theory, mineral formation, quicksilver, decoction, accidents of matter, sharpest vinegar</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Democritus and Geber regarding the fundamental principles of alchemy and the imitation of nature through art.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

books. I say furthermore, that even undigested parts can be digested. <note>In alchemy, "digestion" refers to the slow heating of a substance to mature or purify it.</note>

DEMOCRITUS: These things are sufficient for me regarding the declaration of natural principles. Now we must discuss the principles of art: how the principles of nature are also the principles of art, and in what way art is able to imitate nature.

GEBER: In Book 1, chapter 5, I said that the craftsman must know the principles of this art and the primary roots, which are part of the substance of the work. For he who does not know the principles will not reach the end. Therefore, you should know that those transformations and generations which nature performs by means of some seed, can also be performed by art through the intervention of some seed. Saint Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), a major theologian who also wrote on natural philosophy.</note> also affirms this in the 3rd book of *Meteorology*, saying that Alchemists, through the aforementioned natural principles—that is, through sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum"</note>—are able to achieve the true generation of metals. Since, therefore, nature has its own proper natural seed mentioned before, which approaches nature aptly and closely in the generation of metals so that it may be converted into the nature of a metal, it will be necessary for the preparation of the elixir <note>The "elixir" or "philosopher's stone," the goal of the alchemical work.</note>—which is a metal surpassing all others in perfection—that this be done by means of some proper natural seed. Without such a seed, only God, the creator of nature, can generate anything, such as bread from a stone, or a woman from the rib of a man.

DEMOCRITUS: It is the opinion of all Philosophers that the four elements are the principles of all mixed things. You also agree with this opinion in Book 1, chapter 6, by saying that the diverse preparation of metals is the cause of the diversity of species. Therefore, are the principles of both art and nature present in every elemental thing, and can they be taken from every thing?

GEBER: If you understand me correctly, I say the elements of the Stone of the Philosophers are not the most remote elements, nor are they the same as the elements of other things. For these elements of the Stone of the Philosophers are of another...

<summary>Geber explains that alchemy imitates nature by using specific "seeds" (sulfur and mercury) to generate metals, arguing that the Elixir must be produced from its own proper natural substance rather than common elements.</summary>

prepared and transmuted from their primary nature, and have received a proximate nature and property, so that they can now be transmuted into metal—which is not at all suitable for the elements of other things. **DEMOGORGON.** I shall therefore take common <term>vitriol<note>A sulfate mineral, often green or blue, considered by alchemists to be a "seed" or foundational salt for metallic work.</note></term>, which is the natural seed of metals, and through distillation I will extract that double vapor, which I shall convert by a temperate fire into the substance of sulfur and <term>quicksilver<note>original: "argenti uiui"; also known as mercury.</note></term>, and in this way I will imitate nature.

**GEBER.** This vitriol is a beginning point that is remote from the Art. That is to say, we can by no means escape or even disguise those things which are generated by nature, nor can we lead them to perfection in that manner. For since these vapors are most subtle by nature, they are led to perfection by a temperate heat over a thousand years. We, however, desiring to shorten such a long span of time with vehement heat, find that all things are instead resolved into smoke.

**DEMOGORGON.** You render me doubtful and plainly uncertain. **GEBER.** For what reason? **DEMOGORGON.** Previously you advised that for the composition of the <term>elixir<note>The "philosopher's stone" or medicinal catalyst capable of perfecting metals.</note></term> we should take the seed of nature. Therefore, if by chance there is another seed or vitriol more proximate to the Art, through which we might be able to imitate nature artificially in the composition, show us that elixir.

**GEBER.** Without doubt, there is another. **DEMOGORGON.** Where shall I find this? **GEBER.** Where it has been placed by nature. **DEMOGORGON.** I knew that. But where has nature located it? Shall I find it perhaps in vegetable matter? **GEBER.** In Chapter 11 of the first book, I spoke among the diverse opinions of those who suppose the Art to exist, noting that some have affirmed this Art is found in all vegetable things

# BOOK ONE.

and that it is possible for others, but by no means for themselves. Therefore, they will sooner fail from the labor than it be possible to complete the work. 

**DEMOGORGON.** Are these materials then found in animate things <note>Living organisms, such as plants or animals.</note>?

**GEBER.** Arnold <note>referring to Arnold of Villanova, a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note> in his *Book of the Perfect Mastery*, when discussing vegetable and animate things, says: "Since these things are totally and entirely removed from the nature of metals, it is impossible for metals to be generated from them." When the Philosophers proposed this art in some vegetable or animate thing, or in those things taken from them, they spoke through the likeness of things <note>original: "per rerum similitudines"; meaning they were using metaphors or allegories rather than literal ingredients.</note>. Since these are not the principles of nature, they shall by no means be the principles of the art. This error has deceived many. For since the Elixir <term>Elixir: the medicinal substance or "Stone" capable of transmuting base metals into gold</term> is of a metallic nature, it is without doubt procreated from the seed of metal. Furthermore, since it is especially required that it be joined to metals, it is necessary that it be similar to metals in substance, since only similar things are firmly united and strongly joined together.

**DEMOGORGON.** Shall I then take sulfur and quicksilver <term>Quicksilver: Mercury</term> as they are generated by nature?

**GEBER.** I warned above that these are not the principles of nature: therefore they will not be the principles of the art. And what is more, craftsmanship will in no way be able to make these fixed <note>Fixed: rendered non-volatile so they do not evaporate when heated</note> so that they are united into the single substance of gold or silver. This is because it requires a most certain and infallible measure, a proper proportion of the thickening agent, and an exquisite balancing of heat—all of which are unknown to us, as we clearly showed in Book 1, Chapter 8.

**DEMOGORGON.** Shall I then take sulfur alone? For in Book 1, Chapter 13, you openly say that he who knows how to mix and befriend this with bodies in the preparation holds the greatest secret of nature; and that he has already entered upon a certain path of perfection, though there are many paths which nevertheless all lead to the same intention. In that same place, you call God to witness that by this all things are illuminated: the body, and the light, and

You will note, therefore, that the elixir can be made in two ways: namely, by distillation, solution, and coagulation of the matter; and without distillation, solution, and coagulation, as Saint Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas, to whom several alchemical treatises were falsely attributed</note> affirms at the end of the third book of *Meteorology*. Furthermore, in a certain "short-cut" chapter <note>original: "cap. accurtatorio"; likely referring to the *Liber Accurtationis* attributed to Geber</note>, I said that this shortened work is completed in twenty days, and that in this work two stones would be necessary (that is, two metals: one from which the sulphur is extracted, as if fixed and altered, and another to which the said prepared sulphur is mixed by fusion). Therefore, in the already mentioned short-cut chapter, we placed all the efficacy of this work in the purging of the stone from its oiliness and impurity. Indeed, we have advised in the place already cited how you may detect the sulphur when it is almost prepared, saying: When you see the oil of iron above the water, shining with such whiteness that it dazzles the eyes, then [gather] it...

I remember mentioning this **sulfur** in the *Book of the Testament* under the name of **Mars** <note>original: "Martis nomine"; in alchemy, Mars is the symbol and name for Iron</note>, saying: if you know how to lead this to the whiteness and softness of silver, it will be strengthened and fixed in every judgment or examination <note>referring to the "trial by fire" or assaying of metals</note>; and when Mars is melted lightly and without the aid of any other thing, you have what is desired. Speaking of this Mars in the first book, the last chapter, I said: When it is melted without the medicine <note>the "elixir" or transformative agent</note> which can mature its nature (that is, when it is melted by itself without **quicksilver** <note>original: "argento uiuo"; Mercury</note>), it is joined to the **Sun** and **Moon** <note>Gold and Silver</note>, nor is it easily or without great industry separated from them. 

If, however, it should happen to be separated by the violence of fire, this occurs because it was not yet fixed by the final fixation. But if it is joined to them for the space of twenty days, it is rendered fixed with them, and thereafter it can be separated by no artifice, unless the nature of the fixation is changed; however, it acquired this imperfect fixation through **calcination** <note>the process of reducing a metal to powder by heat</note> and preparation. Truly, the nature of the fixation itself is changed through solution in **Mercurial water**, when the **elixir** is completed in nine months. 

But when it is joined to them unaltered (that is, when it is joined to the Sun or Moon at the beginning) without being boiled or transformed in its nature, it neither changes color nor tints the metal joined to it, but increases it in quantity. Furthermore, in Book 3, Chapter 1, I said that the cause of the perfection of bodies or of quicksilver is the substance of **sulfur** and **arsenic**, because on account of their earthiness and good substance they are not hindered from entering into the bodies, which is perfected by a good melting; and through their subtlety, the impression is not easily removed by flight <note>evaporation or sublimation</note>. Therefore, their mediocre substance is not the cause of the perfection of bodies, or of quicksilver, unless they are rendered more fixed. # BOOK ONE

lest they be struck with amazement, they claimed the art was vain and not at all true. **DEMOGORGON.** I desire to hear the cause of this error. **GEBER.** Since quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum"</note> is that double smoke, or rather an exhaling vapor, which nature could neither coagulate nor render fixed because it lacked that washed, non-burning sulfur—which is the proper medicine for the coagulation and fixation of Mercury itself—it will be rendered fixed by artifice much less by itself, without that or a similar sulfur and medicine. This same opinion is held by Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a famous scholar and bishop who wrote extensively on alchemy and nature.</note> in Book 4 of the *Meteorology*, treatise 3, chapter 2, who says that in the works of Alchemy, quicksilver is dried out through much burning and mixing with sulfur, though not entirely in color; this is to be understood in the operation of the elixir. **DEMOGORGON.** What should be thought of those who have learned to render Mercury fixed using certain herbs?
**GEBER.** I wish to answer them with what I said in Book 2, chapter 17: namely, that the medicine which can coagulate Mercury and be mixed with it in its depths must be taken from things compatible with it. These are all bodies, namely, sulfur and arsenic. For these are called the "bodies and spirits of the Philosophers." And a little further down I said, from whatever thing its medicine is taken, it must be of the most subtle and purest substance, which easily adheres to Mercury and is of easy liquefaction, like "fixed water" in the battle of the fire. For this will coagulate it, so that it converts into a solar <note>gold-like</note> or lunar <note>silver-like</note> nature. You understand, therefore, from the reasons stated above, that our quicksilver is not common quicksilver, but a salt in the likeness of true common lime <note>original: "calcis," referring to the powder produced by heating a substance to high temperatures.</note>, because quicksilver, or the Mercury of the metals, is converted into salt through calcination <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical state</note> and reduction, as clearly appears throughout the whole book of the *Testament*. Indeed, this common salt has the properties of our Mercury, which we have attributed to it in many places.

# DIALOGUE

By means of common Mercury, or common quicksilver <note>Latin: *argentum uiuum*</note>, namely, so that it possesses much viscosity, a great deal of dryness, and a foul earthiness, so that it may be a "dry water," and many other things which we shall pursue at greater length in their proper place. Concerning this Mercury of ours, I spoke in Book 2, Chapter 16, saying that it has its own parts of sulfur naturally mixed within it to a greater or lesser degree, which can be removed from it by artifice. Furthermore, I said in Book 3, Chapter 7, that in metallic bodies there are two sulfurous qualities <note>Latin: *sulphureitates*</note>, one of which is enclosed in the depths of the Mercury from the beginning of its mixing, while the other is accidental or supervening <note>Added from the outside during the mineral's formation</note>. It possesses a good quantity of this supervening or accidental sulfurousness, and this is almost fixed; even with diligent labor, it cannot be easily removed from it. However, of that sulfur which is enclosed in its depths, it possesses only a small quantity, and this cannot be removed unless the primary substance of the quicksilver is first destroyed through distillation. By these two sulfurous qualities, the already prepared Mercury is coagulated and rendered fixed. Therefore, we say that the Elixir can be made from Mercury alone—that is, mixed with its own sulfurs. These things are also understood regarding the multiplication of the elixir, which is done with Mercury alone. 

**DEMOGORGON:** <note>The interlocutor in this dialogue</note> If the elixir can be made from Mercury alone because it contains its own sulfur within itself, then by the same reasoning I shall prepare it from arsenic alone, which is composed of sulfur and Mercury, as Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant medieval authority on natural philosophy</note> says in Book 4 of *On Minerals*, Chapter 1: We ought to observe that a certain heat and dryness are joined to a cold moisture in the same temperament <note>Latin: *complexione*</note>, which is called "Hermaphrodite," as can be seen in plants, which are impregnated in every part. He is speaking, however, of our sulfur, which has the nature of a Hermaphrodite and is composed of two names: of Mercury...

<summary>The text explains that Mercury contains two types of sulfur—one internal and one accidental—which allow it to be transformed into the Elixir. The character Demogorgon argues that if Mercury suffices, then arsenic (as a "hermaphroditic" blend of sulfur and mercury) should also work, citing Albertus Magnus.</summary>

signifying the son with Venus <note>In alchemy, Venus represents the metal copper</note>. GEBER <note>Referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational Arab alchemist</note> says: Speaking generally of Hermaphrodites, I say: even they cannot generate by themselves without a seed or external matter. In this, however, it differs from sulfur, and is most diverse, because arsenic is an easy tincture <term>tincture: a substance capable of changing the color and quality of a metal</term> for both whiteness <note>original: "albedini"; the silver-making stage</note> and redness <note>original: "rubedini"; the gold-making stage</note>. And I wished to show in that place that it is composed of sulfur and Mercury. For although this power of dyeing is easy, it is nevertheless useless and not very firm. --- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue on alchemical principles, specifically focusing on the relationship between sulfur, arsenic, and the transformation of colors. It follows the previous discussion on the composition of metals.</meta>

# DIALOGUE

It participates with one and the other. Raymond <note>Likely referring to Ramon Llull, a major figure in medieval alchemy</note> says in the book titled *The Opener* <note>original: "Apertorij"</note>, while speaking of arsenic, that the soul participates with the nature of the body and the spirit—that is, with sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argento uiuo," or mercury</note>. Through this participation, it acquires the power of binding these together. 

Sulfur, however, dyes easily into redness, but into whiteness only with difficulty. Regarding this sulfur, we likewise spoke under the name of Mars <note>The alchemical symbol for Iron</note> in Book 1, Chapter 10, calling it an easy tincture of redness, but a difficult one for a good whiteness. We did this to show openly that the red tincture which appears easily in sulfur is not useful in the process of putrefaction <note>The stage of breaking down matter</note>, but rather harmful. Because whiteness lies hidden beneath the redness—as we advised in the *Book of the Testament*—and because this whiteness is only reached through long sublimation and washing, we have therefore called it a "difficult tincture." 

Moricinus <note>An alchemical authority, possibly a corruption of Morienus</note> says that our Latona <note>A term for the base metal or "impure body" awaiting transformation</note>, however red she may be, is useless unless whiteness follows the redness; that is, unless the redness is brought into whiteness. We also wrote in the aforementioned chapter on arsenic that there are two kinds of arsenic and sulfur: namely, the lemon-yellow <note>original: "citrinum"</note> and the red. Therefore, when arsenic is distilled by the expression of fire with mercurial water, it is red. But when white water is joined to this, both together produce a lemon-yellow color. 

All these things are confirmed by the Elder Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Senior Zadith, an influential Arabic alchemist</note> when he says that the ancient Philosophers called the soul and the spirit which arise from this mixture "red and lemon-yellow arsenic." By "red," they understood the dyeing soul. Similarly, they called this water "red sulfur" and the spirit "lemon-yellow arsenic." This is because the spirit whitens the soul and stains it with its own color. And they will call this soul "bronze" <note>original: "æs"</note>, and this is the "heavy smoke." And we said in Book 1, Chapter 21, that Venus <note>The alchemical symbol for Copper</note> is dyed with a lemon-yellow color by tutia <note>A term for zinc oxide or "philosopher's wool"</note>, which is that dross...

It cites authorities like Ramon Llull and Senior Zadith to explain how colors indicate the internal state of the chemical soul and spirit.</summary>

white. Similarly, there is both red and yellow sulfur. The red sulfur appears after the <term>putrefaction</term><note>The stage of "blackness" or decay where the matter is broken down.</note> of the material. However, when it is joined under the "white Mercury," it becomes yellow. For the color yellow is a specific proportion of red and white. For this reason, <term>tutia</term><note>Zinc oxide, often found as a powdery deposit in furnaces.</note>, which is the white smoke of our white Mercury, stains the red body itself with a yellow color—namely, our "bronze"—as we have shown clearly enough in Book 3, Chapter 3. And in Chapter 4 of that same book, I said that whatever is sublimated from metallic bodies by strong ignition, so that the most subtle part of them ascends, is a white smoke with sulfur (called "gold"). This <term>arsenic</term><note>In alchemy, this often refers to a volatile spirit or "philosopher's arsenic" rather than just the modern element.</note> is of a very bright yellow color; and when the arsenic is made "fixed" <note>Rendered non-volatile so it does not evaporate when heated.</note> together with the sulfur (that is, by a slow fire in the final boiling). Truly, the sublimation of both is better achieved from the <term>calx</term><note>The powdery metallic ash or oxide left after heating.</note> of that metal which we shall indicate. Although both sulfur and arsenic are the perfecting "medicine" of our Mercury, nevertheless the <term>elixir</term> cannot be made without it—that is, without mixing it with the metallic bodies. When the sulfur, after washing, has become scaly, reduced into a body, and finally brittle, it is prepared.

**DEMOCRITUS:** When sulfur, arsenic, and Mercury are made fixed, they do not melt, nor can they penetrate bodies, because they have already become earthy. If, however, they are not made fixed, the spirits separate from the fire; therefore, an elixir cannot be made from these.

**GEBER:** This is one of the arguments of certain men who are little practiced in the exercise of this art. I mentioned these men in Book 1, Chapter 10, rebuking them because they have little knowledge of this matter; they are unable to complete this operation at all, but by stubbornly insisting on your reasons, they fall into the vice of a "false conclusion" due to their insufficiency.

**DEMOCRITUS:** I do not follow that logical discourse.

**GEBER:** A vice of the consequence is committed...

# DIALOGUE

When we argue from a higher principle to a lower one in the affirmative, the conclusion is not necessarily correct. For instance, you cannot rightly conclude: "it is fixed, therefore it is earthy." This method of reasoning is flawed due to insufficiency. We are, in fact, able to make spirits <note>In alchemy, "spirits" usually refer to volatile substances like mercury or sulfur that can evaporate.</note> fixed so that they are by no means converted into an earthy nature, yet they can still melt and penetrate into other bodies. 

Regarding the role of sulfur, this is clear from Book 1, chapters 10 and 13; likewise from Book 2, chapters 12 and 18; and Book 3, chapters 1 and 6. Concerning the treatment of Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio"</note>, I have also stated in Book 3, chapter 6, that this can be made fixed without being converted into earthiness at all. However, through a certain type of transmutation, it *can* be fixed into an earthy state—specifically through a hasty and brief fixation performed by precipitation or distillation. In that case, it is fixed and transmuted into earth within the burnt dregs remaining at the bottom of the vessel or the glass flask <note>original: "bocciæ," a round-bodied alchemical flask</note>. This is the rapid transmutation of the primary mercurial form, which is completed within the space of six hours. 

Once the earthiness is removed through washing <note>original: "lotionem"</note>, it joins and unites itself with the other prepared spirits. When placed in its vessel over a slow fire, it gradually and successively releases vapors. Through this long and slow confirmation and fixation, it does not lose its metallic fluidity <note>original: "fusionem," the ability to melt like a metal</note>, nor is it converted into earth. 

In Book 2, chapter 6, we said that this Mercury possesses two types of moisture. One can be removed from it by the violence of fire—that is, through distillation or the pressure of heat—and what remains at the bottom of the vessel will not melt, for it has become fixed and earthy. But once the earthiness is removed by washing, the other moisture remaining within it is removed by its own proper fire—namely, a slow fire, which is suitable for the final fixation of spirits. 

As for what pertains to the preparation and fixation of other things of an earthy nature: metallic bodies are fixed through calcination <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to oxidize it or reduce it to powder.</note>, so that...

<meta>The catchword "manif" indicates the next page begins with "manifestum" or a similar word. This page establishes the distinction between "fixed" substances that remain metallic/fluid and those that become "earthy" or dead powders.</meta>

<summary>The author explains that alchemical "fixation" does not always result in a dry, earthy powder; if done slowly, a substance can be fire-resistant while retaining its metallic ability to melt. He distinguishes between rapid fixation, which ruins the material, and slow fixation, which preserves its "mercurial" virtues.</summary>
