# BOOK ONE

We clearly demonstrated in Book 1, Chapter 18, by stating that through **calcination** <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to cause oxidation or dehydration; essentially reducing a metal to a powder or "calx."</note> imperfect bodies are fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixation" refers to making a volatile substance stable in fire so it does not evaporate.</note> without the admixture of any other thing, whatever it may be, once reduced into a calx by its own combustible or burning sulfur alone. For everything that possesses a burning sulfurous quality within itself is burned and calcined by fire, as we have shown clearly enough in Book 2, Chapter 13, and Book 3, Chapter 6. Therefore, when your metal has endured in the fire for so long that it cannot be reduced back into its body as it was before, and it neither diminishes nor changes from one color to another, know that it is now **fixed**. That is, it has been converted into a certain earthy nature which easily endures the fire—not by metallic melting, but by **vitrification** <note>The process of turning into glass or a glass-like substance.</note> alone.

And then all the **spirits** <note>Volatile substances or essences within a material.</note> existing in that calx are fixed; and unless they are separated and made volatile, and afterwards fixed again, they cannot be prepared. Therefore, you shall consider how they are fixed through conversion into an earthy nature, and from that it will be manifest how they may be prepared. However, since by this fixation not all are rendered fixed at the same time, nor in the same manner, we must therefore discuss these things distinctly.

Regarding **Mercury**, enough has been said so far; now we shall speak of **arsenic** and **sulfur**. We said previously that through calcination, the Mercury of metals is converted into a **salt**. Since this salt has much earthiness, it has thereby acquired a certain fixation. We say the same of arsenic, which is a most subtle sulfur, naturally enclosed very deeply in that earth. Therefore, in a certain fixation, they are so firmly bound together that they cannot be separated from that earth except by **distillation** with a strong force of fire. I said, therefore, in Book 2, Chapter 7, when mention was made of this Mercury converted into salt: "When you see it most white, and separated from sulfurousness and from a kind of extrinsic redness, you shall repeat the **sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification.</note> without dregs"—that is, distill...

This is easily understood from the difficulty of distillation, just as I warned in Book 2, Chapter 9. For its fixed part, unless it has been corrupted and distilled—namely the aforementioned arsenic—will adhere firmly to the dregs, that is, to the earthiness, so much so that it cannot be separated otherwise. We have, however, frequently made mention of this earthiness, calling it "salt" in many places, as you will easily gather from our writings.

**DEMOCRITUS.** Show me now, in what manner sulfur is made fixed <note>To "fix" a substance in alchemy means to make it resistant to fire so that it no longer evaporates or changes when heated.</note>.

**GEBER.** Sulfur is not made fixed unless it is first reduced into a lime <note>original: "calcem"; a powder produced by heating a substance to remove its volatile components, a process called calcination.</note>. Moreover, when salt is mixed in, it is calcined more easily and becomes perfect, as I showed in Chapter 13 of Book 1. And Albertus <note>Referring to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a famous philosopher and scientist who wrote extensively on minerals.</note> confirms this in Book 3 of *On Minerals*, Chapter 2. For he says that we see in the works of Alchemy—in which art imitates nature most closely among all other arts—that the yellow elixir <note>original: "elixir citrinum"; a preparation intended to transmute metals into gold, often associated with the color yellow.</note> cannot be made in any other way except from sulfur. 

Indeed, sulfur itself partakes of so much oiliness and burning power that it blackens and burns all metals upon which it is smeared; finally, a method of washing with sharp waters <note>original: "aquis acutis"; likely strong acids or caustic solutions used for purification.</note> was discovered, and a method of boiling it for so long until all the combustible oiliness leaves it, and only the subtle oily part remains which can endure the fire. These waters are made "sharp" by washing with their own salt and distilled vinegar. For these wash and purify our sulfur, and are then removed from it. Therefore, I said in the preface of Book 2 that those things which purify bodies without sticking to them have the nature of salt, alum, nitre, and borax; by which I wished to designate salt itself, just as by "bodies" I wished sulfur itself, or several species of sulfur, to be understood. We said in addition to this in Book 3, Chapter 13: Since all salts, alums, and glass (that is, the above...

Likewise, in Book 2, Chapter 13, we wrote to this effect concerning the preparation and calcination of that sulfur: namely, that a fire is kindled in the furnace under the vessel of calcination of such power that the body itself—that is, the sulfur—is melted so that it can be calcined. As soon as the body itself, melted in vinegar and salt with the heat of fire, has created a black film upon itself—which is sulfur already reduced to lime—it must be removed from the surface and pushed to the bottom of the vessel with an iron or stone spatula. You will note, however, that this black film of calcined sulfur is openly called "iron," since that sulfur is said to be iron which [has] already [turned] into an earthy substance.

This drawing off and sinking of the films continues until the entire body of the sulfur is reduced to powder. I say this because one film is created every day. These films are, in fact, the very oil of sulfur.

If, however, Saturn <note>The alchemical name for Lead, here used to refer to a specific white salt</note> (that is, the white salt separated from the sulfur) has been prepared, it requires a greater intensity of fire—that is, it must be distilled through ashes with the forceful expression of heat; sulfur, however, is prepared in a water bath <note>term: "balneo" (Bain-marie); a gentle heating method using hot water</note>. In Book 3, Chapter 14, we called this iron shovel an "iron rod."

I have delivered the entirety of this operation and its brief practice in Book 1, Chapter 8, describing the sublimation of our lime under the name of Marcasite <note>term: "Marchasitæ"; a mineral (iron pyrite) often used in alchemical experiments for its sulfur content</note>. I set down two methods for its sublimation: the first, which is done without ignition—that is, first in dung <note>A method of providing steady, low heat through fermentation</note> and then in a water bath; the second, which is done with ignition—that is, by distilling the salt itself.

The reason for this double sublimation is the double nature of its substance. For the first substance is the pure substance of sulfur in its root; the second substance is useful as "mortified" <note>term: "mortificatus"; a substance that has been "killed" or changed in its physical state, usually by losing its volatility</note> Mercury—namely, the aforementioned salt. The first substance is useful as "living" mortified arsenic—that is, moderately prepared. For by this we are excused from having to take common Mercury, which requires much labor and industry for its "mortification" <note>The process of "killing" or fixing mercury to strip its fluidity</note>. Moreover, the entire method of the sublimation of **Marchasite** <note>term: Marchasite; a term used historically for various metallic sulfides, often pyrite or bismuth</note> consists in this: that it be placed, finely ground, into an **aludel** <note>term: aludel; a pear-shaped subliming pot used in laboratory work</note> with distilled vinegar, and the sulfur be sublimed from it without reaching the point of ignition, using only the heat of horse manure <note>original: "fimo"; a common method for maintaining steady, gentle warmth</note>. After putrefaction in a water bath, the sulfur—which often rises in the form of a black film—must always be removed.

When, however, the sulfur and vinegar have been removed from the salt, and the salt itself has been placed upon a mat smeared with clay, the fire should be increased until the **aludel** vessel glows. The first sublimation of the Marchasite ought to be done in a sulfur-sublimation vessel until the sulfur, separated from the salt, is in the water bath. The vessel for the putrefaction of the lime <note>original: "calcis"; the powdered residue after heating</note>, the calcination, and the washing of the sulfur shall be the same. I described the form of this vessel in Book 2, Chapter 10, in these words:

->In the bottom of the **aludel** vessel, let there not be a great quantity of the body to be sublimed. This vessel should likewise be flat at the bottom and of shallow concavity, so that the body of the Marchasite may be spread evenly and thinly across the bottom of the vessel, so that the matter of the sulfur and salt may be sublimed by the vinegar equally and copiously from every part, and may ascend freely into the heights.<-

Furthermore, you will note that when all the sulfur (which we said is called by the name of Mars) has been sublimed through many films, its true color then appears—that is, the purest white. # DIALOGUE

It is burned away, like common sulfur. However, that substance which is sublimated <note>Sublimation is the process of heating a solid until it turns into a vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the material.</note> after washing does not ignite (as we stated in Book 2, Chapter 2), nor does it display any property of sulfur, but rather that of quicksilver, or "mortified" Mercury <note>original: "Mercurij mortificati"; this refers to mercury that has been "killed" or fixed into a solid state through chemical reaction.</note>. For this reason, in Book 3, Chapter 2 near the end, and in Chapter 18, when speaking of the washing of Mercury, I intended the washing of this sulfur to be understood as well.

The ancients hid this preparation of sulfur under the fable of Hercules and Antaeus. For as Macrobius <note>A 5th-century Roman writer often cited by alchemists for his allegorical interpretations of myths.</note> relates, the ancients signified the Sun by Hercules—that is, our arsenic <note>In this context, "arsenic" often refers to a specific volatile crystalline substance used in the "whitening" of metals rather than the modern poison.</note>. By Antaeus, the son of the Earth, our sulfur is understood, which is the "fatness of the earth." These two are imagined to wrestle when, mixed together in a vessel, they boil in vinegar; for they are joined together after the process of putrefaction <note>The stage of chemical breakdown where the matter turns black and decomposes.</note>.

Furthermore, since sulfur itself (called by the name of Mars <note>Mars is the traditional alchemical name for Iron.</note>) is prepared with salt, as I said above, arsenic can also be prepared because it is mixed with salt in its depths. This is what I wished to demonstrate in Book 3, Chapter 16: that Mars (that is, sulfur) is prepared through the sublimation of arsenic. 

When the arsenic, mixed with salt, becomes stronger and thus reduces the sulfur (signified by the name Antaeus) into lime <note>A powdered calx or oxide.</note> and converts it into an earthy nature, it was fabled that Antaeus was thrown down to the earth. Yet, because the sulfur becomes more perfect in this way—being more "fixed" <note>Resistant to change by fire.</note> and rendered more bright—they feigned that Antaeus, having recovered and increased his strength by being thrown down, always rose up stronger. 

Hercules, however—that is, our salt—when distilled with Mercurial water (which is signified by Juno and the element of air), dissolves, attracts, and holds the said prepared sulfur suspended in that very water. In the decoction, it stains the mixture with a black color, which can also be understood as a "manifestation," as will be clear from what follows when we discuss the elevation of bodies. Concerning this [preparation] of sulfur...

# BOOK ONE

Regarding preparation, Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and influential philosopher who wrote extensively on alchemy and minerals.</note> also spoke in the third book of *On Minerals*, chapters 1 and 2, and in the second treatise, chapter 5. Furthermore, it is written in the *Book of Vapors* that arsenic, or orpiment <note>original: "auripigmentum"; a deep yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment and in alchemy.</note>, possesses two oily qualities <note>original: "unctuositates"; referring to the greasy or combustible nature of the substance.</note>, just as sulfur does. One of these two oily qualities is removed from it by washing in urine, lye, vinegar, and goat's milk. For these washes are sharp, and they carry away the oiliness from it. By these "sharp washes," you should understand vinegar with salt.

Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose medical and philosophical works were foundational to Western science.</note>, in his letter to Hasen the Philosopher, writes that he contended with great diligence to remove from sulfur and arsenic everything that blackens silver. Since sulfur is indeed better than all other minerals, he finally found the way to this: namely, that they should be cooked over a slow fire, yet in such a way that the fiery power within it is not burned away, but rather extracted; and that nothing be taken away from the fiery power and the substance of the sulfur by the fire, but only the oiliness be consumed. Sulfur excels other metallic spirits because it is more fixed <note>term: "fixed" (fixum); a substance that remains stable and does not evaporate when exposed to heat.</note> and possesses the tincture of redness. By the "silver" which is cooked by the oiliness of the sulfur, one should understand the whiteness <note>original: "albedo"</note> hidden beneath the redness <note>original: "rubedo"</note> of the sulfur.

Rhazes <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and alchemist known for his laboratory manuals.</note>, in his *Book of Divinity*, making mention of this preparation, commands one to remove the oil that floats on the surface with a sponge, for as long as it takes until no more blackness appears and a round little cloud <note>original: "nubecula"; likely referring to a film or scum forming on the liquid.</note> rises. This should be left until its sublimation <note>term: "sublimation"; the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification.</note> is established; then one cloud after another should be taken and submerged in boiling water until all the clouds have been consumed—for know that these are the oil of sulfur. The true sense of this discourse is that we should understand that as soon as the films have risen above the vinegar and become firm, then the vessel must be moved gently, or the films must be pushed to the bottom by any other means, and this in...

It emphasizes the alchemical transition between the "whiteness" and "redness" of these substances.</summary>

# DIALOGUE

It is called "animal" because, when projected upon metals, it makes them like itself. It is called "mineral," however, because it is made from minerals or metals, and furthermore, because generating gold and silver is the singular property of metals. Yet both our elixir and our metallic stone can be called "vegetable" because they are generated from salt, which is called vegetable. For this is generated by the power of heat, and is said to "vegetate" <note>To enliven or cause growth</note> all things; or because that salt first makes things green, like a herb or plant: and for this reason we have called it both vitriol <note>term: vitriol (a sulfate mineral, often green, used as a powerful solvent)</note> and living water <note>Latin: *aquam uiuam*</note>. The sulfurous and, as it were, fixed substance is the body. The spirit, however, is called the volatile nature before it is fixed and made firm. These are called minerals because they are metallic, or because they contain within themselves that sulfur in which the metallic virtue consists. Aristotle, in his letter to King Alexander, commands that we take the vegetable, animal, and mineral stone. We see, moreover, the practitioners of Alchemy make medicines from metals that are more or less perfect, by which they have learned to transmute and dye certain metals into the color of gold and silver. Since, indeed, in every genus in which these degrees of perfection are found, it is necessary to arrive at one singular thing which holds the highest and most perfect degree of perfection in that genus—as Aristotle affirms in the tenth book of the *Metaphysics*—it will also be necessary in this art that we find one most perfect metallic medicine. This medicine must be able to transmute imperfect metals into true gold and silver, because those things which have an affinity in substance can be transmuted one into another through mutual corruption <note>In Aristotelian physics, "corruption" refers to the breaking down of one form so that a new one may arise</note>, as we see in the mixing of the elements. Since, truly, all metals (as we have shown above) are generated from one and the same substance, and there is no difference between them except for accidents <note>non-essential qualities like color or hardness</note> and a greater or lesser digestion <note>the process of "ripening" or refining within the earth</note>: therefore, by removing the accidents and sufficiently digesting the undigested parts, one can be transmuted into another which is the most perfect above the rest. And in this, art overcomes the operation of nature. For art can inwardly purify metals, which nature cannot achieve. This is the same opinion held by Saint Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas</note>. For he, at the beginning of the fourth book of the *Meteorologica*, says that metals can be transmuted one into another, since natural things...

For this reason, Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical texts are attributed.</note> established a "circulation" <note>original: "circulationem"; a process of continuous distillation or the cyclical transformation of matter.</note> among the metals. Furthermore, the Elder Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Senior Zadith (Ibn Umail).</note> left it written that the thing which the Philosophers seek is one thing, which they have called by the name of all red things, and of all bodies and species that are handled by the hands of men. <summary>Geber argues that the alchemical Elixir is derived from a single, self-contained "stone" rather than a mixture of many substances, using the biological metaphor of male sulfur and female mercury. ->**The Golden Breeze**<- <note>original: "aura"; likely a poetic reference to the breath of life or the subtle spirit in alchemy</note>

Furthermore, Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note> says in his letter to the King of Naples that the **Philosopher's Stone** <note>term: Lapis Philosophorum; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting long life</note> is one single thing and of one single nature; within it is contained everything necessary for itself, and it also possesses within itself that by which it is improved. Overcome by these most weighty opinions of the Philosophers, you undoubtedly believe that the **elixir** must be made from only one radical thing—from one single metal, which is called a "stone" for the reason that it has been reduced into the form of a stone. Moreover, from this stone are made gems and medicines, by which the first fathers <note>The Biblical patriarchs</note> prolonged their lives to such a great age; that is, the **quintessence** <note>term: quinta essentia; the "fifth element" or purest essence of a substance</note> and **potable gold** <note>term: aurum potabile; a liquid gold supplement believed to be a universal cure</note>. Unless both of these are joined together, it cannot be made from any other thing, nor can it exist by itself. Since, therefore, one is so firmly embedded in the other, it will be manifest that the elixir is not made from many things, but from one single metal, since all things are composed of Sulfur and Mercury. However, the elixir is said to be made from "many things" because it is composed of sulfur, arsenic, and Mercury; because many things have a certain similarity to these—as well as to the various colors appearing during their preparation—we cannot deny that in the "Shorter Work," where there are several paths, different metals can concur. But in the "Greater Work," only one thing has the near virtue and power to be converted into the elixir. For this reason, I said in Book 2, Chapter 2: if you have sublimated <note>term: sublimation; the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note> spirits from the **calx** <note>term: calx; the powdery metallic oxide remaining after a metal has been burned or roasted</note> of any metal, the sublimation...

You also affirm in Book 1, Chapter 17, that gold is a tincture of redness <note>original: "tincturam rubedinis"; in alchemy, "redness" refers to the final, most perfect stage of the Great Work</note>, and that gold is able to transform and dye a body; for this reason, many Philosophers call gold the "ferment of redness." Furthermore, you yourself affirmed at the end of the *Book of Investigation* that perfect bodies can be made more perfect, and can be reduced to a state of subtlety and spirituality.

**GEBER:** You philosophize acutely and ingeniously enough, but you have not sufficiently considered the true nature of gold. For (as I taught in Book 3, Chapter 4), since gold is generated from the most subtle and clear substance of Mercury (or quicksilver), having very little sulfur and a pure redness, it is therefore fixed and clear by its own nature, being dyed by itself. For this reason, sulfur can by no means preserve this quality unless it has reached its ultimate form and permanent tincture, such as the Elixir <note>The "Elixir" or Philosopher's Stone is the goal of the alchemist, capable of transmuting base metals into gold</note>. Therefore, gold will be changed neither by nature nor by art, nor will it be properly improved in any way by itself without the mixing of another tincture. 

And if by chance gold is increased in color by some preparation, this happens because the tincture itself, which was hidden within the gold, is drawn outward. And if you were to separate this tincture from its own Mercury, you would dye only as much Mercury with it [as was there before], and no more. Agrippa <note>likely referring to Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, a famous 16th-century occultist</note> affirms this same thing: because gold does not have more tincture than is needed for the dyeing of its own Mercury, as Arnold [of Villanova] teaches in the *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosario"; a famous medieval alchemical text</note>. Therefore, it would be vain to seek this tincture in gold, since there is such a small quantity in it, and it has no utility of its own. 

For this reason, Albertus [Magnus] said in the third book of *On Minerals*, chapter 7, that gold is not transformed by alchemical operation. And this is the reasoning of the ancient Philosophers and certain old Alchemists: that the species of gold is merely the [perfect] form of metals, while all other metals are incomplete; they are truly on the path so that they might approach the species of gold, like an incomplete thing which is on the way to reaching perfection...

<note>The text follows the scholastic tradition of using Aristotle's physics to explain alchemical processes.</note>

...come. Therefore, they said that imperfect things can be aided by art <note>The term "art" here refers to alchemy, the "Great Art."</note> so that they might finally reach perfection, just as seeds are aided by the cultivation of the earth. Gold, however, since it is more determined and complete, is not convertible into another metal. For as Aristotle and his commentator <note>Likely referring to Averroes, the "Great Commentator" on Aristotle.</note> affirm in Book 7 of the *First Philosophy* <note>original: "primæ Philoſophiæ," referring to Aristotle's Metaphysics.</note>, no thing moves toward a form or perfection unless it first possesses some imperfect part of that form within itself toward which it may be moved. For we know that the seed would not move itself to acquire the form of a man unless it first contained "man" within itself potentially; for if it were already a complete man, it would not be the beginning of motion, but rather of rest. Therefore, Aristotle says that when the species exists in the matter, motion ceases. For motion occurs through the deprivation of the species which is in the matter itself. Since it is so arranged by nature that the imperfect, insofar as it is imperfect, naturally desires to become perfect: therefore, the deprivation within it leaves a natural desire to become perfect. This desire, moreover, is the principle of transmutation, as Roger Bacon <note>original: "Rogerius Bacho," the 13th-century English friar and philosopher associated with early experimental science.</note> handed down. Therefore, since gold is perfect in the metallic species, it will not have this deprivation and desire within itself toward the metallic form and species.

**DEMOCRITUS.** I grant that common gold is perfect in its species; however, compared to the elixir <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" or the agent of transformation.</note>, it will be imperfect, for art overcomes the work of nature.

**GEBER.** I say that gold is a more perfect metal than the elixir: for however firmly this [elixir] retains the metallic substance within itself, it is nevertheless not a metal, since it is fragile and composed of spirits alone, and its tincture <note>The coloring or transforming power.</note> exceeds the common metallic tincture.

**DEMOCRITUS.** Since for the composition of the elixir a single metal is required, alone and most necessary...

# BOOK ONE

It is necessary that I receive the spirits of gold, which are clean, pure, and fixed, while the spirits of other metals are unclean and volatile. **GEBER** <note>Referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most influential authority in medieval alchemy.</note> And this is the most certain proof that these spirits of gold are not those about which I have written in all my books on <term>Alchemy</term> <note>The art of transmuting base metals into noble ones and creating medicinal elixirs.</note>. For we have said that <term>Mercury</term> <note>Quicksilver, regarded as the "mother" of metals.</note>, <term>sulfur</term>, and <term>arsenic</term> are, before preparation, unclean, combustible, and volatile spirits; furthermore, we have handed down instructions on how they can be purged of earthiness, wateriness, and oily, burning sulfurousness—but these impurities you will not find in gold. Furthermore, **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century philosopher and scientist.</note> writes in Book 3 of *On Minerals*, Chapter 6, that a metal cannot be generated from another metal—that is, the species will remain in its primary metallic substance. Why then, in the chapter of the aforementioned book, is the author not cited as proceeding in this way? He says, namely, that by corrupting and removing something in its own species, with the help of those things which are in the metallic nature, they may induce the species of another metal. For this reason, among all Alchemical operations, that one is both the better and the principal one which proceeds through the same principles by which nature itself proceeds. Just as through the preparation of sulfur, and through the decoction <note>The process of boiling or heating a substance to extract its essence.</note>, sublimation, and purging of <term>quicksilver</term> or <term>Mercury</term>. But those who whiten with white metals, and stain with a lemon-yellow color using yellow substances while the first species of the metal remains, are without doubt impostors and deceivers; they can make neither true gold nor silver. Furthermore, **Aristotle** says that a true <term>transmutation</term> <note>The total change of one substance into another.</note> of metals cannot happen unless they are first reduced into "prime matter," that is, to the nature of salt or <term>vitriol</term> <note>A term for various sulfate minerals, often used as a starting point for chemical reactions.</note>, which can by no means happen without <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or ash.</note>, as we have taught clearly enough in the book of the *Testament*. However, since silver cannot be properly calcined, as we said in Book 3, Chapter 9, since true calcination happens without mixing—

<summary>This page argues that true alchemy must mimic nature's own processes by reducing metals to their primary matter through calcination, rather than merely changing their surface color. It cites authorities like Geber, Albertus Magnus, and Aristotle to distinguish between genuine transmutation and the deceptive practices of impostors.</summary>

# DIALOGUE

...tion of any thing, except with its own proper and innate combustible sulfur <note>original: "sulphure combustibili"</note>, as we have stated in Book 1, Chapter 13, and Book 3, Chapter 6. Since this combustible sulfur is truly not present in gold, as we have sufficiently shown in Book 4, Chapter 15, it follows that gold cannot be properly calcined <note>The process of reducing a metal to a powder through heat.</note>. We described this reasoning in Book 3, Chapter 6, saying that a thing in which the three causes of corruption (explained in the place cited above) meet, is most easily corrupted. However, in a thing where these causes do not meet at all, the speed of corruption is diminished in proportion to the absence of those causes. In gold, truly none of these causes exist, which is why it is not corrupted as others are. For this reason, we said in Book 3, Chapter 4, that gold has a fixed substance <note>Stable and resistant to fire.</note> and is without burning sulfurousness, as we easily recognize through every operation in the fire: for it is neither diminished nor set aflame. Therefore, one would attempt in vain to calcine gold without some external substance. We said, therefore, in Book 1, Chapter 17, that gold is calcined and dissolved without any benefit.

**DE M.** Arnaldus <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> says in his *Book of the Perfect Magistery* that the reduction of metals into their first matter <note>The "materia prima," the fundamental substance from which all things are made.</note> is easy; therefore, transmutation will also be easy. 

**GEB.** <note>Geber, the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the legendary father of chemistry.</note> Arnaldus is not speaking of common gold, for this is destroyed only with great difficulty because of its strong composition, as we showed in Book 1, Chapter 8, and Book 2, Chapter 7. We showed how and from which metals our Mercury <term>Philosophical Mercury; not the common liquid metal, but a purified essence used in alchemy</term> ought to be extracted; and although in that place gold is by no means openly excluded by us, we nevertheless concede that the sublimation of Mercury is more convenient from metals with which it has the least affinity. It is most certain that Mercury has less affinity with all other metals than it has with gold, as we showed in Book 3, Chapters 2 and 4. Therefore, Mercury is much more conveniently extracted from the other metals besides gold. For in what way could the elixir <note>The Philosopher's Stone or the medicine of metals.</note> be made from gold?

# BOOK ONE

it can be done, since by the consensus of all Philosophers this is made from the most base matter: as *Morienus the Philosopher* <note>Original: "Morigenus Philofophus." Likely referring to Morienus Romanus, a legendary 7th-century alchemist.</note> openly shows with these words, when he says that the necessities of this work are to be obtained at the lowest price, especially in the operation of gold. **DEMOCRITUS.** What do you respond to my inquiry? **GEBER.** I say that the radical moisture <note>The "humidum radicale" or radical moisture was believed to be the life-sustaining fluid within all things that resisted destruction by fire.</note> of the remaining metals is just as virtuous, incorruptible, and incombustible as that of gold. *Albertus* <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant figure in medieval science.</note> affirms this with us in Book 3, Chapter 2 of *On Minerals*, when he says: "We have seen all metals retain their radical moisture even in the most vehement fire." We confirmed the same ourselves near the end of Book 1. Our physicians, mixing gold into their medicines, do this without reason; for since even the most vehement fire can by no means destroy it, much less will it be digested in the stomach. Moreover, the ancient Philosophers and Poets, when extolling gold and holding it in high esteem, were not speaking of our common gold, but of the gold of the philosophers <note>Original: "auro philofophorum." A symbolic substance, not the physical metal.</note>. For common gold cannot be distilled or dissolved in any proper way. Therefore, it cannot be transmuted into salt in the required manner, as we showed above. Furthermore, when you say it can be demonstrated by certain reasons that the elixir <term>Elixir: A preparation able to change base metals into gold or prolong life.</term> must be made from common gold, I respond to your opinion thus: In the "Short Work," in which neither the solution nor coagulation of gold is required, gold is to be preferred over other metals; but in the "Great Work," since metals must be reduced to their first matter <note>The "materia prima," the formless primitive substance from which all things originate.</note>—in which there is no difference, as we showed above—gold ought not be preferred to other metals. Nor do we concede that all metals are equally suited for the composition of the elixir, since only one single metal has acquired a singular quality in its very ore, so that it might become more fit in its substance than the others, and closer to the Art.

...should approach our [substance] more than all other metals. Regarding your second argument, I believe it must be answered that Aristotle <note>The author refers to Aristotle’s theories on biology and spontaneous generation to explain alchemical creation.</note> spoke of the proper and uniform generation of animals; however, in generation which occurs through the corruption of the generator, it is sufficient for the generator to be similar in power and potential, as we said above regarding human seed. From this we conclude that our Stone, from which the elixir must be made—namely potable gold <note>original: "aurum potabile," a medicinal form of gold believed to grant longevity</note>, the fifth essence <note>original: "quintam essentiam," the purest element beyond the four earthly ones</note>, gold, and silver—is these things in its near power and potential. Arnold <note>Likely Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> also affirms this in a certain letter to the King of Naples; for he says that in the said composition or our Stone, the Sun and Moon <note>The Sun (Sol) and Moon (Luna) are the standard alchemical terms for Gold and Silver.</note> exist in potential and power. Unless the Stone contained these within itself, neither gold nor silver could be made from it. However, he adds that this Sun and Moon are by no means to be compared to common gold and silver, since they surpass them in power by a great distance. For the Sun and Moon contained in this composition are "living," that is, vegetable <note>In alchemy, "vegetable" refers to the ability of a substance to grow or multiply, like a plant.</note>; whereas common metals are dead and their powers are extinguished. Those, however, are perfected, for which purpose the ancient Philosophers named our Stone "the Sun and the Moon," since these are contained in the Stone itself potentially rather than visibly. And when the Philosophers say that gold is the tincture of redness and the ferment of redness, these things must be understood as referring to the "gold of the philosophers," which is a sulfur <note>original: "sulphur"</note> that is both boiling and cooking by its own heat, digesting and tinting its own Mercury <note>original: "Mercurium"</note>, just as our elixir tints and transforms our Mercury during the process of multiplication <note>term: multiplication (the act of increasing the quantity and potency of the alchemical medicine)</note>, and is more truly a ferment. Furthermore, what I said regarding the fact that perfect bodies can be improved and made more perfect: this is not to be understood as referring to common gold or silver, but to the elixir itself, which can be improved and refined through multiplication, solely by joining Mercury to it.

<meta>catchword: joining.</meta>

<summary>The author distinguishes between "dead" common metals and the "living" philosophical Sun and Moon (gold and silver) contained within the Philosopher's Stone. He explains that the Stone acts as a ferment or sulfur that can transform and multiply its power when joined with Mercury.</summary>

# BOOK ONE

By joining them. By "perfect bodies" we also intended to indicate the prepared species of **sulfur**, which have obtained the name of **gold** from their redness and **silver** from their whiteness; these, however, must be prepared with their own salt and vinegar, then they are refined and dissolved with **sal ammoniac** <note>original: "sale armoniaco"; a salt of ammonia used as a flux and solvent</note>, that is, with the water of its own salt. For we said in Book 2, Chapter 15, that the reason for the discovery of refined and sharp water <note>likely a reference to a mineral acid or strong solvent</note> was the necessity of refining those spirits, which before preparation had neither the ability to melt nor to penetrate, and deprived us of great utility—that is, the utility of **fixed spirits** and those things which belong to their natures. **Arsenic** is of the nature of sulfur, as we said in Book 1, Chapter 14, and Book 2, Chapter 12. Truly, the reason for the discovery of pure water was the soaking of spirits and clean medicines, that is, of prepared sulfur.

**DEMOCRITUS:** <note>The text uses "DEM." and "GEB." to denote a dialogue between Democritus and Geber</note> You would do a truly most pleasing thing for me if you would declare what this "gold of the philosophers" and "potable gold" <note>term: aurum potabile; a legendary liquid medicine made of gold, believed to grant eternal youth</note> actually is.

Furthermore, **Rhazes** <note>original: "Rasis"; a celebrated Persian physician and alchemist (c. 865–925 AD)</note> says in the *Book of the Light of Lights* that the red oil assuming color is sulfur and copper, and is compared to the sun and to gold. The **Senior Philosopher** <note>likely referring to the alchemist Zadith ben Hamuel</note> is of the same opinion, for he says the gold of this stone is the "gold of the Philosophers," and a **tincture** <note>a potent medicinal or transformative extract</note> having the soul within itself, with which the spirits may ascend. In another place he says the same: that the divine water is spiritual, in which the soul called "gold" is contained. The same says in another place that **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of the alchemical tradition</note> named the white water "gold" because it has a staining soul born from that white water; and in another place he says that the ancients called the water "soul," which Hermes had called "gold" when he said that he sowed gold in

->D I A L O G U E<-

the foliated earth <note>original: "terram foliatam"; a term for the purified matter at a specific stage of the alchemical process, often appearing flaky or leaf-like</note>. DEMOCRITUS: What did Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> wish to signify by this foliated earth? GEBER: The prepared Quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum uiuum"; Mercury</note> of the Philosophers, to which this gold ought to be joined: or rather, we understand this foliated earth to be the sulfur, which, having been sublimated from the leaves of gold and silver in various colors, is why we said that sulfur makes a tincture <note>a medicinal or transformative extract</note> with its own likeness. For by "wind" is signified the white smoke, which carries within itself the red smoke, which is called the soul, and gold, copper <note>original: "æs"</note>, Venus, coin, arsenic, glass, vitriol, fire, oil, sulfur, green water, the Green Lion, verdigris <note>original: "uiride æris"</note>, wine, human blood, the blood of a demon, orpiment <note>original: "auripigmentum"; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, permanent water, and by various other names. For this reason, the elder Philosopher said that this divine water is called by the name of all moist things, such as vinegar, wine, milk, fat, blood, and seed; so also it is named by the name of all tinctures and flowers likewise. And by this "soul of the stone," they understand that which they have exalted—that is, sublimated—in that water. Therefore, it is said elsewhere by the already cited Philosopher, that the discourse of all Philosophers agrees on this tincture alone, which they have extracted from its own soul, which they have called the soul, the offspring, and the king; and this offspring is the fatness, which is why they called it the soul and gold, since it has a red soul hidden in whiteness—

In the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophorum turbæ," referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note> it is written that our red coin is called gold. However, the Philosopher Mirois says it must be cooked until its humidity is raised into the still <note>original: "alembicum," the cap of a distilling apparatus</note>, since it is a sweat similar to gold. And in the *Heaven of the Philosophers* these words are read: "The fire must indeed be increased to the fourth degree for the space of one hour, and a water of golden color will go forth." This golden water is the golden rain into which Jupiter transformed himself <note>A mythological reference to Zeus/Jupiter visiting Danaë as a shower of gold, here used as a metaphor for a chemical process</note>, and it must be kept separately, for in it **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile: a legendary liquid medicine made from gold, believed to have life-prolonging properties</term> is contained. When I said in Book 1, Chapter 17, that everything which is able to radically purify and to tinge a metal with a lemon-yellow color <note>original: "colore citrino"</note> is itself able to make gold, I wished to signify that the root of the metal is the distilled gold of the philosophers.

Avicenna <note>A famous Persian polymath whose medical and alchemical works were foundational in the West</note> says in the *Book of Minerals* that sal ammoniac <term>sal armoniacum: ammonium chloride, used as a flux in alchemy</term> is entirely sublimated, and is a water with which a hot, very subtle, and highly humid smoke is mixed. And in Book 3, Chapter 9, making mention of our **Saturn** <note>In alchemy, Saturn usually represents Lead</note>, we said that nothing is resolved from it into smoke—as far as can be noticed—that is not of a very bright lemon-yellow color. And in the aforementioned place we said that when the smoke ascends, it necessarily ascends together with a non-burning sulfur; its property is to retain that yellow color. Furthermore, in Chapter 8, speaking of our **Jupiter** <note>In alchemy, Jupiter usually represents Tin</note>, we said that those who have diligently watched the smoke raised from it by a great expression of fire have observed this smoke to approach a yellow color, since this is the property of calcined sulfur; they judge by a most certain and infallible conjecture that this smoke retains much of the nature of **fixed sulfur**.

But when the gold of the philosophers, which is red, is whitened, it is said to have been converted into silver, and it is named silver. The same is to be understood regarding sulfur: when it is converted into whiteness <note>original: "albedinem," the second stage of the Great Work</note>, and afterwards becomes reddish through long decoction <term>decoction: the process of heating a substance in a liquid</term>, it is then said that silver has been converted into gold.

Both of these are called quintessences. Furthermore, beyond the names mentioned above, the red vapor is also called the "soul of wine," as John of Rupescissa <note>A 14th-century Franciscan alchemist</note> handed down in these words: "We call the quintessence the soul of wine, but our wine is the gold of the philosophers." But the white vapor, which carries the red wine in its belly during distillation, is properly and truly called the quintessence of which the philosophers have spoken. # DIALOGUE

...a certain thing which is called the spirit of the stone. This, however, is neither an element, nor does it possess an elemental quality within itself in actuality, but rather by virtue: for in actuality it is neither hot nor dry like fire—for it cools and heals cold diseases—nor is it hot and moist like air, which is easily corrupted, as we see in times of plague and in the generation of frogs and similar creatures which are generated from the corruption of the air. For the <term>fifth essence</term> <note>Latin: *quinta essentia*; the "quintessence" or a pure, celestial substance distinct from the four earthly elements</note> prepared from metals can be preserved for ten thousand years without corruption, provided it is kept in a glass vessel most diligently sealed so that it cannot breathe. For this reason, Hermes, Plato, Socrates, and many philosophers who knew of this substance attributed perpetuity to it, claiming those who used it would not die; and yet, they themselves died just like other mortals, as God willed it.

**DEM.** Therefore, it is useless to use this medicine. 

**GEB.** It would be useless, and impossible, for a man to labor to transcend the natural limit of life; yet this limit could be extended to the five-hundredth year, as was established for the first ancient fathers <note>A reference to the long-lived Biblical patriarchs like Methuselah</note>. Just as a man, beset by diseases in many ways, is often unable to reach his established term of life and may shorten his lifespan, in the same way, by removing these impediments, he will be able to prolong his life so that he finally reaches the term established by his own nature. Among all other remedies by which we prolong life, however, this prepared <term>fifth essence</term> excels, as has been demonstrated clearly enough in our dialogue entitled <term>The Wood of Life</term> <note>Latin: *Lignum vitae*; likely a reference to the Tree of Life</note>, for it gives life to man. Rhazes <note>Latin: *Rasis*; Abu Bakr al-Razi, a celebrated Persian physician and alchemist</note> says in the book <term>The Light of Lights</term> <note>Latin: *Luminis luminum*; a famous medieval alchemical treatise</note> that the water descending from the greater rock is named by the philosophers the <term>water of life</term> <note>Latin: *aqua vitae*</note> and "heaven," for the <term>fifth essence</term> is simple and incorruptible and separated from elementary matter; it is neither cold nor moist like

elemental water, for it burns before it is coagulated: therefore it is called **burning water** <note>original: "aqua ardens," likely referring to a highly rectified spirit or a potent chemical solvent</note> by the philosophers. Furthermore, it is neither cold nor dry like the earth, as we clearly perceive; for it warms greatly and cures melancholic infirmities, which are cold and dry, and it has the form of the clearest and most subtle water. It is called by various names by the Philosophers, assumed from some property or similarity. It is also called **air** because of its subtlety, and **sal Ammoniac** <note>A nitrogenous salt used in alchemy to dissolve metals</note>, because it is a distilled salt, and because of its harmony and homogeneity. The ancient Philosophers also signified this by the names of pernicious and ferocious animals, such as the **serpent**, the **Dragon**, the **Lion**, the **Bear**, the **Basilisk**, and the like, because of its horrible and poisonous nature before proper digestion. For this reason it is also called **fetid water**, for it is bitter and stinking before coagulation.

If, therefore, sweet things corrupted by heat are converted into bitter salt, this our **quintessence** <note>The "fifth essence," the purest and most potent part of a substance</note> distilled from the salt of metals will receive a much greater bitterness, being thus cooked and burnt up.

DEMOGORGON: In what way will this bitterness be removed by sulfur so that it becomes sweet, since sulfur itself is also bitter, as Rasis <note>Rhazes (c. 865–925 AD), a Persian physician and alchemist highly influential in the Middle Ages</note> testifies, who says that sulfur, which is called by the name of Mars <note>original: "Martis nomine"; likely referring to iron-based sulfur or a specific volatile state</note>, is manifestly bitter and sharp on the outside? The other sulfur, however, which is called gold <note>original: "aurum"; here referring to the "sulfur of the philosophers" or the solar principle</note>, which is also received into this composition of the medicine, is sanguine, sweet, and fragrant, as Rasis says. Alphidius <note>An Arabic alchemist known through Latin translations</note> writes these words: 

> Before you undertake this work, I shall prescribe for you a good composition of the elixir. First, it will be necessary that it be moist, and when it is boiled, that it be coagulated and thickened like snow or hail, and a salt of sweet flavor, with a black belly and a white color.

DEMOGORGON: What is signified by the "black belly"? GEBER: Alphidius's meaning is that however much our medicine may be white at the beginning of the boiling, it nevertheless has a hidden blackness proceeding from a certain oily and combustible moisture. This blackness is most clearly manifested in the coagulation of the matter; but it must be boiled so much that this moisture is consumed and it becomes white. For then this operation is completed; and just as the heaven above us has an influence on these lower bodies not only by itself, so that the species of things may be preserved, but more strongly by the power of the Sun and the stars—

# BOOK ONE

is signified, so also this heaven of ours ought to be adorned by our splendid and incorruptible sun—that is, by our aforementioned gold. For it is generated in the earth by the power of the Sun, from which it receives its nature, color, power, property, and incorruptible substance. Therefore, our <term>fifth essence</term> <note>The "Quinta Essentia," a highly purified substance believed to hold the life-force of matter.</note> adorned by this marvelous Sun, will infuse into us the power of heaven and of the celestial Sun; that is, the preservation of life, the restoration of natural moisture, and the renewal of youth. Regarding this Sun, Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar whose encyclopedias were standard references in the Middle Ages.</note> spoke in his 16th book, saying that <term>Chalcantum</term> <note>original: "Chalcantum," likely copper sulfate or blue vitriol.</note>—which is the flower of copper—is made in medicine from salt by a very hot Sun, and has such great astringent power that if sprinkled into the mouth of a Lion or a Bear, it binds their mouths with such strength that they cannot bite.

**GEBER.** <note>The dialogue features "Democritus" and "Geber," traditional personas used in alchemical texts to represent the student and the master.</note> By Chalcantum and the flower of copper, Isidore understands our copper, which we call "ink," and the vitriol hidden in salt before distillation; it is made through calcination together with salt. Indeed, this copper, when united with our fifth essence (which is understood by the names of the Bear and the Lion), so entangles and coagulates it that it takes away its sharpness so that it cannot "bite"—that is, it cannot dissolve the sulfur. Just as the celestial influence is increased by the influence of the stars, in the same way our "heaven" must be joined not only to the Sun, but also to the other stars and planets, so that its influence and power may be increased, and so that it may be more easily coagulated and led more quickly to perfection.

**DEMOCRITUS.** What are these stars and planets that must be joined to our fifth essence, or applied to our heaven?

**GEBER.** All the prepared metals of the philosophers signified by the names of the planets; these are also received into the composition of the <term>elixir</term>. For this reason, it is said that for this composition, one single common thing exists and is sufficient. Nevertheless, it is true that in this art there are principally two substances that are more suitable for coagulation

that there are types of sulfur, Venus <note>Copper</note> and Mars <note>Iron</note>. And this was the opinion of Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and philosopher who wrote extensively on minerals.</note> in the first book *On Minerals*, chapter 9 of the first treatise, when he wrote that the Alchemists, with singular zeal and diligence, create waters which possess the qualities of different elements in virtue and action—that is, so that they may fix and coagulate that which they desire to transmute. For since glass is dry, it is able to draw out much moisture from the meat through boiling, so that the meat is cooked much faster. Since this glass from the ashes of hay and fern is much drier than other common glass, the cooking will be completed much sooner by adding it; for common glass still possesses some moisture which it received from the lead that was first in its composition. **DEM.** Although I have not yet made a trial <note>original: "periculum" - meaning a test or experiment</note> of this matter, I hardly believe that meat is cooked faster by adding glass. **GEB.** This is the reason many ignorant people, unskilled in natural things, have accused Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman author of Natural History.</note> and Albertus of lying, since they interpreted what the Philosophers spoke through similarities and allegory according to the bare letter, and thus thought themselves deceived and mocked. **DEM.** What did they wish to signify by this thing? **GEB.** By the name of "glass" in this art, sulfur is frequently signified; for just as common glass receives all colors, in the same way our sulfur demonstrates all colors during its preparation. By the "meats to be cooked," however, our Mercury is understood, which must be coagulated and made firm or fixed in Mercurial water by the mediation of sulfur. ->BOOK ONE<-

It is also dry, though less so as Rasis <note>Rhazes, the 9th-century Persian physician and alchemist</note> says, for it shares in the humidity of Mercury, which it received from that salt called Saturn <note>Lead or lead-based salts</note>, from which the aforementioned sulfur—named for Venus <note>Copper</note>—was distilled. Furthermore, you shall note that there is a difference between the coagulation of the fifth essence <term>quintessence: the purest, fifth element extracted from a substance</term> and the fixation of the elixir, since they ought to produce different effects. For the elixir must transmute imperfect metals into true gold and true silver; it is necessary, therefore, that it be fixed and converted into a more-than-perfect substance of gold and silver. In this case, the Mercurial water will not remain, as it hinders fixation, although the red fifth essence remains there, called such while staying in its own form. 

However, since the Mercurial fifth essence is to be taken by mouth as a medicine that penetrates through all the limbs and preserves them from corruption, it ought not to be so fixed that it is converted into a golden or silver substance. For it will suffice only to be so decocted and digested that its vaporous, foul-smelling, and sharp humidity is consumed and becomes sweet. Thus, when taken by mouth, it will suddenly penetrate through the whole body as the most subtle thing of all and one conformed to the human body. By its powerful and divine virtue, it will induce such incorruptibility in every place it penetrates that those who have taken only three drops, either by itself or mixed with another suitable thing, shall live healthily for a long span of time, like the first ancient fathers who ate from the Tree of Life <note>original: "lignum uitae"</note>. All these things were hidden in the fables of the poets by the ancient wise men, especially those of Orpheus. D. E. M. You will have made for me...

# BOOK ONE

whatever [knowledge] he may have, and experience, he will immediately understand—upon hearing any name—what it might signify in this art. Let us take for example the name of Iron or Mars <note>In alchemy, Mars is the planetary symbol and secret name for Iron.</note>. If, therefore, a Philosopher says that this is manifesty hot and dry, and a ready tincture of red, the skilled craftsman of this art will immediately recognize that he has said these things concerning sulfur, since this quality does not suit common iron at all in its manifest <note>original: "in manifesto"; referring to the outward, visible properties of a substance.</note> state. Rasis <note>Rhazes (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and foundational alchemist.</note> says in his book on divinity that it must be observed that things are bound together by nature with such subtle artifice that in every thing, all things exist in potentiality, even if they are not perceived by sight; this is better detected in balanced things than in others. In this way, the interiors of gold are silver, and the interiors of silver are gold: for there is found in the Sun <note>Gold</note> something of the Moon <note>Silver</note>, and in the Moon something of the Sun. Indeed, in copper, gold and silver exist in potentiality, but not visibly; and in these are iron, lead, and tin, in which you may also find gold and silver in turn. Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant medieval scholar and theologian.</note> writes the same in Book 3 of *On Minerals*, chapter 8, saying that the Philosophers and renowned Alchemists Hermes, Gilgil, and Empedocles, along with the entire number of all other Alchemists, affirm that in every metal there are many species and natures of metals—some hidden, some manifest, some appearing on the inside, some on the outside, some in the depths, and others on the surface. This is just like those who disputed concerning the "latency of forms" <note>A philosophical theory that all possible forms are already present in matter, waiting to be drawn out.</note> and that "every thing is in every thing," as was the pleasure of Anaxagoras <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher who taught that all things contain a portion of everything else.</note>. They said, therefore, that lead is gold on the inside, but lead on the outside; whereas gold is gold on the outside surface, but lead on the inside and in its depths. In this same way, copper and silver and every metal contain within themselves every other individual metal. Furthermore, they say that the exteriors are not named...

whether they are called "interior metals" or other names based on their parts within the whole, they are named according to the property and nature of the dominant element, since the dominant part holds enclosed within itself that over which it rules. In this way, these men seem to entirely affirm the opinion of Anaxagoras <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 500–428 BC) who taught that "everything is in everything," a concept the alchemists applied to the presence of all metals within one another.</note>, who declared that individual metals exist within other individual metals, and that the naming of a metal comes from the dominant part. **DEMOGORGON.** These things seem impossible to me, and if I judge correctly, their opinion is rejected by Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant medieval authority on alchemy and natural philosophy.</note>. **GEBER.** You are laboring under a similar error as certain philosophers who used to say that there was no other substance in the world except that which could be seen. However, if you had diligently examined, corrupted, and transmuted metals at any time, and had considered their intrinsic qualities and the diversity of their substance compared to their apparent color, these things would not seem impossible to you. Furthermore, if it seems to you that Albert rejected this opinion, I tell you that he rejected only the bare literal meaning, but by no means the discovery of the ancients and their allegorical sense. **DEMOGORGON.** Therefore, declare to me distinctly and openly the intention of the ancients and this allegorical sense. **GEBER.** Through calcination <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder.</note>, putrefaction, and washing, we reduce our metal into a white salt like common lime, and in many places we have called this "lime." However, when it has the property of common lead, it finds the name of "lead." For this reason, Rhazes <note>original: "Rafis"; referring to the Persian alchemist and physician Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925).</note> said in the place already cited, that lead in its manifest state—*that is* on its surface—is cold and dry; this is lead and silver, black, soft, earthy, melancholic, bitter, foul-smelling, feminine in action, and lead in potential is silver. In its depth, however, it is hot and moist—that is gold, airy, sanguine, saffron-colored, sweet, fragrant, and masculine.

# BOOK ONE

On one side it is cold and moist—that is, tin and quicksilver or Mercury <note>original: "Mercurius"</note>, which is watery, phlegmatic, white, tasteless, relaxed, and feminine. On the other side it is hot and dry—that is, iron and copper, which is fiery, choleric, red, bitter, sharp, and masculine. You understand now, surely, that in one common metal and one metal of the philosophers, each of the philosophers' metals is contained; the same must be understood regarding the others.

**THE DISCIPLE.** <note>original: "DE M." likely standing for Discipulus or Demogorgon in some dialogues</note> These things seem to me to be difficult and very obscure.

**GEBER.** In the preface to the second book, I said that the consideration of those things by which the completion of this work is reached must begin with a diligent investigation of the principles of bodies according to their depth, their hidden nature, and their manifest nature. You should notice, therefore, according to the opinion of Rhazes <note>original: "Rasis," referring to the 9th-century Persian alchemist and physician</note> in the book on divinity, that in every body there are three dimensions: height, depth, and breadth. Height is that which is manifest <note>visible or external</note>, depth is truly hidden, and breadth holds the middle ground between the manifest and the hidden—that is, it is partly manifest and partly hidden. 

Truly, that salt which we call lead, and quicksilver or Mercury not yet prepared, and the "dry water" and the "small cake" <note>original: "pastillum," a medicinal or alchemical pellet</note> about which I spoke more extensively in the chapter on descension—that is, distillation—since it is white in its manifest appearance, it is therefore said to be cold. For whiteness is a sign of coldness, or rather it is called cold because it participates in the moisture of water, of which I spoke in Book 2, chapters 2 and 7. Since, however, it has much "earthiness" intrinsically, as we have said above in many places when speaking of Mercury and lead, it is therefore said to be of a dry nature. This is the reason we said in Book 1, chapter 15, that this Mercury does not easily adhere internally to its sulfur, which we name Mars <note>The alchemical name for Iron</note>...

# BOOK THE FIRST.

It is written in a book of three words that it is necessary for the manifest to be hidden, and the hidden to be made manifest <note>A common alchemical axiom: the physical appearance of a substance must be broken down to reveal its inner essence</note>. This hidden thing is truly of the nature of the Sun <note>original: "Solis"; referring to Gold</note> and of fire, and it is the most precious oil of all hidden things, a living tincture, and a permanent water. 

In this state, Saturn <note>Lead</note> is still manifest on one side—namely in the dimension of breadth—and hidden on the other. It is of a cold and moist nature; that is, it is Jupiter <note>Tin</note> and Mercury <note>Quicksilver</note> in a prepared state. This occurs after the washing of the burnt dross. For this reason, I said in Book 1, Chapter 19, that lead possesses a great deal of earthy substance, and because of this, it must be washed and converted into tin. 

Morienus <note>A legendary 7th-century alchemist</note> says that among the philosophers, the "unclean body" is called lead, while the "clean body" is called tin. It is said to be of a phlegmatic nature because of its viscous moisture. It is called tasteless and weak because of the separation of much sharp water during distillation. It is called feminine because it is cold and moist, and in the generation of metals and the elixir, it is similar to the female menstruation. 

On the other side, which partakes of both the manifest and the hidden simultaneously, Saturn is hot and dry; that is, iron <note>Mars</note> and copper <note>Venus</note>. However, as Rasis <note>Rhazes, the 9th-century Persian physician and alchemist</note> teaches, the dryness of Venus exceeds and is greater than the dryness of Mars, because it partakes of quicksilver or Mercury, which tempers its dryness. 

It must be noted, however, as I advised in Book 1, Chapter 12, and Book 3, Chapter 7, that within metallic bodies there are two species of sulfur necessary for our art. In the manifest state, these depend upon redness, but in the hidden state, they are white, black, and reddish-brown. One of these is intrinsic to Saturn itself. Therefore, it is dissolved like the others mentioned above, and is called by the name of Venus. We stated in Book 1, Chapter 15, that all the aforementioned things exist together before distillation, and all possess the nature of salt; and that Jupiter, Saturn, the Moon <note>Silver</note>, and Venus are dissolved in this way.

# DIALOGUE

Through Mercury—whom we designate by the name of Saturn <note>In alchemy, Saturn often represents lead or the process of "mortification," but here it refers to a specific power or virtue of Mercury</note>, that is, through his own power—all these things are dissolved, since they all simultaneously possess the nature of salt. However, only those things are dissolved which have the nature of salt, or of alum and gall, as we have often advised.

**THE DISCIPLE:** You have already mentioned "dissolution" <note>Latin: *solutio*; the process of turning a solid into a liquid state</note> many times; therefore, I ask that you be willing to declare to me the meaning of this little verse:

> Dissolve the bodies into water, I say to all you who seek to make gold and silver.

**GEBER:** <note>The text refers to Geber, the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most famous figure in early alchemy</note> In Book 2, chapter 17, I said that all our sulfurous bodies are sulfur and arsenic. Indeed, the arsenic—called by the name of Venus <note>Copper</note>—is dissolved by distillation (as we said above). However, the sulfur called by the name of Mars <note>Iron</note> is not dissolved by distillation, since it does not have salty parts within itself (as we said in Book 1, chapter 3); yet it is refined and dissolved with its own Mercurial water, which we joined with the other sharp waters <note>Acids or corrosive solvents</note> in the chapter on dissolutions. 

From these two sulfurous waters, take whichever one you wish—namely, this one which is called Venus and gold—and give this to your "enemy" to drink; that is, join it to the sulfur which is called iron. For we have said in the place already cited that the tincture <note>The substance capable of transmuting metals</note> is made from sulfur with its own likeness or companion, which is arsenic. 

Furthermore, by the "Ancient Lion" or the "Dragon," we understand Mercury converted into salt; they call it "ancient" because it is incorruptible. From this sentiment, you will also understand the remaining verses: namely, he who cannot dissolve (our arsenic and our Mercury being understood here) nor refine the sulfur, let him not touch the sulfurous body nor our quicksilver <note>Mercury</note>. For the fixed sulfur, which is called by the name of Mars, will not be able to hold the volatile arsenic unless they have been very well mixed together and a single substance has been made from the two. This can be done with the Mercurial water, which we call "living water" <note>Latin: *aqua uiua*; often referring to a powerful solvent or quicksilver</note>, which holds the middle place in...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of metallic transmutations and the chemical properties of sulfur and salts, specifically focusing on the relationship between Iron (Mars) and Lead (Saturn).</meta>

# BOOK THE FIRST

This occurs in the mixing of tinctures <note>original: "tincturarū"; refers to the coloring agents or essences used in alchemy</note>, that is, between the sulfur and the aforementioned prepared salt, which is called the dissolving salt. Rhazes <note>original: "Rasis"; a famous 9th-century Persian physician and alchemist</note> makes mention of these sulfurs in his writings, and says that the water of copper <note>original: "ęris"</note> dissolved into redness is both better and stronger than the water of iron; that is, when iron has been sublimated <note>vaporized and solidified</note>, it is converted into redness, and when vinegar is added, it acquires a red color like blood. Since, therefore, the sulfur called by the name of Mars <note>the alchemical name for Iron</note> is, as it were, external in the aforementioned Saturn <note>the alchemical name for Lead</note>, it is for that reason more easily separated from the mixture during preparation. For this cause, we said in Book 4, Chapter 14, that those metals which have a small quantity of Mercury are more easily separated from the mixture of salt. And in that same place, concerning this sulfur called by the name of Mars, I said that Mars does not have fusion—that is, it does not dissolve. Therefore, it is not mixed with that salt, because it is characterized by the deprivation of much Mercurial moisture.

For this reason, in Chapter 10 of the same book, we said that bodies are not separated from one another by those two examinations (that is, in the calcination <note>reduction to powder by heat</note> of sulfur with vinegar and salt, and in the distillation from salt and separation) except through the diversity of the composition of their substance. For from that diversity is born a diversity of fusion—that is, of solution, density, and rarity—which are the principal causes of the separation of our metals. Speaking of Mars alone, however, I say that it is fiery—that is, a red sulfur, hot and dry, and easily burned before preparation because of its great oiliness. It is "choleric" <note>referring to the humor of yellow bile</note> because of its vehement heat, bitter and sharp; since it is choleric, it is like food that is bitter and sharp due to indigestion. Furthermore, it is called masculine because it is hot, and it is the principal seed of metals and the elixir. Thus, truly, you will comprehend all things in one common metal.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the chemical principles of the "Great Work," specifically focusing on the relationship between natural mineral formation and laboratory practice.</meta>

# DIALOGUE

the metals of the philosophers <note>original: "metalla philosophorum"; refers to the essential substances used by alchemists rather than common metals</note> are comprised, and that gold and silver exist there in a near potential state, since their seed is within it, partially digested and fixed by nature; yet it is nothing other than sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum"; mercury</note>. If you truly wish to be certain of this matter, you will diligently consider those things which we wrote in Book 2, chapters 7 and 16, and Book 3, chapter 4, near the end.

**DEMOCRITUS:** Unless I see the practice of this operation, I do not feel capable enough to understand these things, even though I perceive that art imitates nature in many ways.

**GEBER:** Nature, through decoction <note>the process of heating a substance in a liquid</note>, burns earth and water mixed together and generates vitriol <note>a sulfate mineral, often associated with the "green lion" in alchemy</note>; art, however, burns its metals and reduces them into a lime <note>original: "calcem"; a powder produced by roasting or calcination</note> in which vitriol is hidden—the seed of nature and art. Through putrefaction <note>the breaking down of matter through rot or fermentation</note> and separation, the vitriol and sulfur are extracted. Nature resolves its vitriol and exhales a double smoke; art does the same with its vitriol. Nature, through sublimation <note>the process of a solid turning into gas and back to solid</note>, leads that double smoke into mineral places and joins it with fixed sulfur; art similarly joins its double smoke with fixed sulfur. Since that white smoke would be a great hindrance to us in the process of fixation, we therefore remove it, and in its place, we take a viscous, coagulated Mercury which we have extracted from the burnt dregs. For this reason, we said in Book 2, chapter 6, that the substance which is closer to the body in the head of the aludel <note>term: aludel; a pear-shaped earthenware pot used as a condenser in sublimation</note> is always separated—just as in a powder raised upward, that is, dissolved into water—from that which remains prepared at the bottom of the vessel. Nature also washes that fixed sulfur, and art does the same. In the washing of this red sulfur, which is the color of red wine, it is first calcined and becomes black because of its oiliness; by Raymond <note>likely referring to the famous alchemist Ramon Llull</note>, it is called "blacker than black" in relation to his vitriol. Therefore, we said in Book 4, chapter 20, Mar—

# BOOK ONE

<note>original: "PRIMVS"</note>

In every kind of combustion, a blackness and dark color is created, appearing more or less depending on how much the metal has been calcined <note>Calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state, often reducing it to a powder.</note>. For through strong calcination, the combustible sulfurous quality is consumed, as we showed in Book 3, Chapter 7. For this reason, all the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the historical lineage of alchemists.</note> largely agree in thinking that these substances ought to be black; perhaps so that the lime <note>original: "calx" - the powdery residue left after heating.</note> does not turn into glass due to an abundance of sulfur. From this blackness all the way to whiteness, all other colors appear: for whiteness and blackness are external and completely opposite colors, and all other colors are clearly intermediate between them. Therefore, in this preparation, whenever something of the blackness is lost step by step, the color always appears less black, until finally the ultimate and perfect color of whiteness is reached.

**DEMOCRITUS.** I believed this diversity of colors appeared after the coagulation of the elixir: for you said in Book 2, Chapter 16, that it is the property of sulfur when combined with quicksilver <note>original: "argento uiuo" - Mercury.</note> to create a red or lemon-yellow color according to the quantity of the mixture; but that it is the property of the removal of sulfur to give whiteness to the Mercury through fire. This, therefore, is the cause of the diversity of colors in the substance itself after its coagulation into a rock or stone. However, I do not understand this way of speaking: for when you put sulfur and Mercury together so that they might coagulate, I thought they were no longer to be removed or separated; how then will sulfur be the cause of the variety of colors after the coagulation of the elixir?

**GEBER.** In sulfur, as Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a famous medieval scholar and theologian who wrote extensively on minerals.</note> handed down in his work *On Minerals*, there are two combustible oilinesses <note>original: "unctuositates" - oily or fatty substances believed to be the source of flammability.</note>: one greater and, as it were, external; the other smaller and internal. The first is red and is removed by washing; but the internal one is consumed after the coagulation of the elixir,

Gradually, that coagulated matter remains and increases until this dry matter stands out like a globe of earth in the midst of the water at the first creation of the world, like an island in the middle of the sea surrounded by water.
