These things were hidden by the ancients under the veil of poetic fables. **DEMOCRITUS.** It is truly a wonder that the ancient Philosophers concealed this art with such obscure and chimeric fictions, which are explained and twisted by our grammarians toward a merely moral sense. **GEBER.** He who has no knowledge of this science will not grasp the reasoning or intention of the ancient Philosophers; he will not understand why they fashioned such a great number of gods and goddesses, or what they wished to designate through their births, debaucheries, loves, and various transformations. For it would be foolish to believe these were invented merely to hide moral discipline, which they otherwise set forth so strenuously with such praise, both publicly and privately, in words and deeds across every kind of virtue. **DEMOCRITUS.** If you would make me a participant in so great a secret, beyond your other benefits bestowed upon me, you would bind me to you most closely by this one act alone. **GEBER.** Lest you believe me to be alone in this opinion, read Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar and bishop who wrote extensively on nature and alchemy</note> in Book 1 of *On Minerals*, chapter 4. He says that Empedocles <note>A Greek pre-Socratic philosopher</note>, who long preceded Hermes, confirmed that stones were created from burning heat; the origin of this affirmation regarding the body comes from the ancient fable of Pyrrha and Deucalion <note>In Greek mythology, these survivors of a great flood repopulated the earth by throwing stones (the "bones of the mother") behind them</note>, in which fable stones are called the bones of the Great Mother. Indeed, according to Empedocles, these bones are composed in part from Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire; here used as a term for fire or heat</note>. **DEMOCRITUS.** It seems to me that these words were by no means spoken by Albertus with this meaning in mind. **GEBER.** The "stones generated by burning heat" are the colors of our metals generated by the vehemence of fire; they are called "bones" because of their hardness, and they are "partly composed of Vulcan"—that is, of sulfur <note>original: "fulphure" (sulfur); in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle</note>, which is called by the name of fire, because fire predominates within them, that is, sul-

These are called the "stones of the great mother," <note>original: "lapides magnæ matris"</note> that is, of the earth, because their generation arises from metal which contains a great quantity of earth within itself; for their entire substance is fixed and earthy. The Poets tell a fable of Pyrrha and Deucalion, <note>In Greek myth, these were the only survivors of a great flood, who repopulated the earth by throwing stones behind them.</note> that they alone escaped and were saved during the time of the flood in a small boat which they had boarded; by the flood, the poets intended to signify our "white waters." Moreover, the "gaze" of the vapors of metallic bodies toward this mineral power occurs when the fumes have already ascended into the long neck of the vessel or flask; they look back and lead themselves back toward this mineral power, which hardens and converts those vapors into the most noble Stone of the Philosophers. This sulfur is the parent of another sulfur, which is remarkably subtle and easily melted, which is understood under the name of Icarus, the son of Daedalus; for it is a more subtle arsenic...

the more useful and fusible part of Mars <note>Iron</note>. By the "Labyrinth," the poets wish to signify a vessel or a glass flask, or rather the humble "Philosophers' Stone" in which the aforementioned sulphurs are enclosed. Moreover, the wings with which they attempt to fly and are carried into the heights are those substances I mentioned in Book 2, Chapter 10, when I said: the bodies that require the administration of things that lift or exalt them are Venus <note>Copper</note> and Mars, because of their slow fusion—that is, their dissolution. This Icarus flies high together with that water—that is, he is raised up into the head <note>the alembic or cap</note> of the vessel—for the salt is dissolved by the heat. But Icarus falls through the beak of the vessel's head into the receiver, into the "bitter water," and is suffocated there; for he turns black through the process of decoction <note>boiling/digestion</note>.

The wings, however, with which Mars flies away from the said stone, designate arsenic, as we have stated clearly enough in Book 3, Chapter 16. This is because the humidity and sharpness of the vinegar, acting by means of the heat of the smoke upon the calcined and refined body, dissolves and attracts the substance of the salt hidden in that heat. In doing so, it attracts the subtle thing we call the "Sulphur of Venus," which is enclosed in the depths of this salt. Because of the binding together of the sulphurs in this putrefaction, the arsenic attracts Mars and causes it to be carried upward and sublimated. Furthermore, the "wax" with which the wings were fitted and fastened is the aforementioned viscous, whitened salt—referred to above by the name of Jupiter—which resembles white Tyrrhenian wax. I have handed down these matters more extensively in Book 3, Chapter 7.

# BOOK ONE

near the end, speaking of the elevations of bodies with spirits. Furthermore, Virgil <note>The Roman poet Virgil, whose *Aeneid* was often interpreted by alchemists as a coded manual for the Great Work.</note> mentions the golden bough, which, when plucked, is immediately replaced by another similar one; by this tree he wished to signify the **vegetable salt**, from which, through the expression of fire, that **sulfur** which we call **gold** is distilled. Since all of this sulfur is not expressed from the matter all at once, but gradually, drop following drop, they fashioned the fiction that when one branch is plucked another grows in its place—that is, by the strong expression of fire. But the elder Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Hermes Trismegistus or another foundational alchemical authority.</note> demonstrates this more clearly by saying that this **soul** cannot be expressed from the stone all at once, but in many turns and little by little. For this reason, the ancients called it "soul" and "souls," "flower" and "flowers," "tincture" and "tinctures," "blood" and "bloods," "fatness" and "fatnesses," because of its slow emergence from one stage to the next. 

They also fashioned the story that Jupiter, angry with his father Saturn, cut off his genitals with a very sharp sickle, and these fell into the sea, and from the mixing of their blood with the foam of the sea, Venus was procreated. By **Saturn**, they understand the aforementioned salt, who is called the father of **Jupiter**—that is, of the prepared salt itself which is generated from him. When this salt is placed in the fire within a vessel or glass bulb <note>original: "boccia," a round-bottomed flask used in distillation.</note>, it is called "angry Jupiter" and "altered by fire," and it is dissolved into a subtle and sharp water. This is signified by the sharp sickle with which the angry Jupiter cut off his father's genitals—that is, he separates and draws with him the masculine parts or virility, which is the **intrinsic sulfur** within the salt itself. Together they fall into the receiving vessel, which is signified by the fall into the sea (namely, into the water of the salt). From this salt and sulfur, **Venus** is generated, as we have said above. This water is the chariot of **Phaëthon**, which the Poets call **Eridanus** <note>The mythical river into which Phaëthon fell; here representing the liquid distillate.</note>, because by it our **Sun** and the "stinking spirits" (which we call copper <note>original: "aes," meaning bronze or copper.</note>) are carried. # DIALOGUE

striking his own head, Minerva leaped forth armed from his very brain. <note>The author is interpreting the myth of Athena/Minerva's birth from the head of Zeus/Jupiter as a chemical process.</note> I judge that this fable must be understood in this way: Jupiter, having been transformed into fire, turns his own head—that is, that salt which we call by the name of Saturn <note>Lead, or the base mineral salt used in the work</note>—in which is contained that subtle, white, soft, and moist salt, like a brain. From this, through distillation, is born the "armed Minerva"; that is, that water clothed or covered by the most subtle part of the sulfur, which we call by the name of Mars <note>Iron, or a specific red sulfurous principle</note>. 

They say, furthermore, that Vulcan, captured by love for Minerva, pursued her most tenaciously, finally grabbed her garment, and spilled his seed upon the earth, from which a small boy was immediately born; thus Minerva escaped the violence of Vulcan. By Vulcan, we understand the sulfur which we call fire. And this iron <note>original: "ferrum"</note> is moved by a great love toward the Mercurial water which we signify by the name of Minerva. For they are together in one and the same metal. Therefore, he follows her in calcination <note>The process of reducing a metal to powder by heat</note> and in putrefaction <note>The breaking down or "death" of the substance</note>. 

But since their natures are diverse and they are very easily separated from one another, they fashioned the story that Minerva fled the embrace of Vulcan, since this sulfur does not retain the seed in the same way as other subtle sulfur. Therefore, it is said that he grabbed her garment because it is united with him and joined with his salt, as if from the outside; and thus in separation, he releases his seed—that is, by scattering that subtle part of the sulfur into the earth of the salt itself. 

Through distillation, however, that sulfur is born which we said above was signified by the name of Ganymede, as well as Apollo and Phoebus <note>Names for the solar, golden principle or the perfected sulfur</note>; and thus she escaped the powers of Vulcan through this removal or separation, which had been an impediment to fixation <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note>. 

The ancients also fashioned the story that Io was loved by Jove, but she, fleeing Jove, was surrounded by a thick and dark little cloud so that she might stop in her course. By Io, they signify the aforementioned Mercurial water loved by Jove, since they are of the same substance, yet at the same time...

# BOOK ONE

->**The First Part**<-

Joined and enclosed in a glass vessel <note>original: "boccię," a cucurbit or glass flask used in distillation</note>, the water flees from the fire through distillation by way of the long neck of the vessel. Then, falling back to the bottom where it is surrounded by Jupiter <note>Jupiter here represents tin or a specific metallic state</note> by the aforementioned black skin, through which Io <note>Io represents the volatile principle or "mercury" of the philosophers</note> is coagulated and fixed; that is, she is halted in her course. These black skins are the sails with which Theseus returned by ship to Athens. Seeing these, Theseus's father, persuading himself that his son had perished, threw himself headlong into the sea out of despair and was there suffocated by the waters. By Theseus, they wish to signify that subtle sulfur which is contained in that skin—or rather in that oil—signified by the name of Theseus. But the father of Theseus, Aegeus (that is, the thicker or more crude sulfur), casts himself headlong into the sea, which is the salt-water; there he is dissolved and dies, that is, he is suffocated, for in the second stage he is turned black.

Among many other such poetic fictions, the ancients handed down that after the flood and the drying of the earth from the inundation of waters, various species of animals were produced by the earth. Among other animals, they say it produced a certain serpent, which was slain by an arrow from Apollo, or Phoebus. They have interpreted this fable in this way: through distillation, which we also call sublimation. First, by a slow fire, the "flood" comes—that is, the watery humidity appears within. Once this is removed and the fire strengthened, the earth (that is, our matter, which has much earthiness in itself) produces "animals," which is that subtle sulfur we call "animal." 

But among the other animals, it also gives birth to that serpent which they call Python <note>original: "Phitonem"</note>; that is, the fetid water, which Phoebus kills. This means it is coagulated by our salt and turned black to become a medicine for the human body. Furthermore, they imagine Mars to be the son of Jupiter and Juno; yet there are those who wish Mars to be procreated by Juno alone without a father. By Juno, although they often understand the element of air, nevertheless when they say Mars is the son of Juno, born without a father...

# DIALOGUE

By Juno, they understand the element of earth. Moreover, Jupiter is found with Mars and Juno after putrefaction <note>The stage of chemical decay or fermentation in the alchemical process</note>. However, because Mars is separated from her belly through washing <note>original: "lotionem"</note>, he is called her son. Since a son ought to be similar to his father and mother at least in some part, and yet this sulfur—which we understand by the name of Mars—bears absolutely no resemblance to Jupiter (for it is hot and dry, reddish, hard, without viscosity or solubility, and as if deprived of quicksilver <note>mercury</note>), while Jupiter is cold and moist, white, soft, viscous, easily melted and dissolved, like mortified quicksilver; for this reason, they feigned that Mars was the son of Juno—that is, of the earth—for he is the fatness of the earth.

**THE MASTER:** Show me then, in what place do they understand the element of earth by the name of Juno?

**GEBER:** They feigned that Juno was the daughter of Saturn and Ops, the sister and wife of Jupiter, and brought forth into the light in the same birth before Jupiter; that she is the queen of gods and goddesses, and of riches, presiding over childbirth and marriage. Truly, the Mercurial water <note>A purified liquid state of mercury used as a solvent</note> signified by Juno is called the daughter of Saturn, for it is distilled from him and his earth, which is signified by Ops. This water bestows riches—that is, our gold—and in that distillation, Juno is generated (that is, that Mercurial water signified by the element of air) and Jupiter (that is, the salt which remains at the bottom of the vessel or flask among the dregs). Since the Mercurial water is distilled first, and first exits the flask or vessel of distillation, the ancients said that Juno was born in the same birth before Jupiter. I wished to signify the same thing in Book 1, Chapter 11, when discussing the preparation of Jupiter, saying that Saturn is prepared in the same way (mocking those who are not sons of this art <note>A common term for true alchemists or initiates</note>), sometimes only that which cannot break bodies—that is, the earthiness which does not break, that is, does not dissolve, sulfurous bodies,

# BOOK ONE

Furthermore, one must add the Mercurial water <note>original: "aquam Mercurialem"; likely a reference to a specialized solvent or "philosophical mercury"</note>, which comes before Jupiter returns into the body from his lime—that is, from the white salt, which is the true lime. Moreover, they say that Juno presides over childbirth because, through distillation, she brings forth Phoebus <note>The Sun, representing Gold</note> into the light. For this reason, she is also called Lucina <note>The Roman goddess of childbirth and light</note>. She is also said to preside over marriage because she acts as a certain intermediary in the joining of the sulfurous tinctures—that is, of Venus <note>Copper</note> and Mars <note>Iron</note>. This is signified by the very fine net fashioned from steel. Morienus <note>A legendary 7th-century alchemist</note> used to say that the soul—that is, Venus—is not joined with the body—that is, with the sulfur signified by the name of Mars—except by the mediation of the spirit, which is this spiritual water. She is also called the wife of Jupiter because these two together beget our gold, or because they are joined and united together before distillation. They ascribe to her a kingdom over all the Gods and goddesses of the heathens, whom we signify by our metals. For she rules these; through her they are born and dissolved, refined and separated, joined, killed, brought to life, and bear fruit. For this reason, they even fashioned her as the goddess of riches.

**DEMOGORGON.** Do not wonder that I have not interrupted such a long discourse until now; for to tell the truth, I have been greatly delighted by this interpretation of such obscure and plainly chimerical—or rather, monstrous—fables and fictions. And (to say what I feel) I am plainly persuaded that you are entirely twisting such fables to suit your own opinion, even if the ancients were never of the intention toward which you strive to twist them.

For unless you deny this opinion of the ancients which I told you—namely, that every metal in every...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the hidden meanings within classical mythology, specifically interpreting the stories of Latona and Vulcan as alchemical processes.</meta>

->DIALOGUE<-

If you have fully understood the explanation I gave you regarding this saying, you will never deny that this was the true intention of the ancient poets in their fables and fictions. For they labored in every way to deeply obscure and hide this science <note>The "science" refers to alchemy, often called the "Great Art" by practitioners.</note> within their poetic inventions, so that they might conceal the secrets of nature from the ignorant and the unworthy. Indeed, many wise men skilled in these matters have not hesitated to affirm this along with me.

For instance, they wrote that Latona was seduced by Jupiter, and because of this, Juno descended from heaven and found her on the island of Delos, where she gave birth to Phoebus <note>Apollo, representing the Sun or gold</note> and Diana <note>Representing the Moon or silver</note>. By Latona, they understand that **sulfur** which we previously said was signified by the name of Venus. Since it is impure and an altered composite of Jupiter and Juno—that is, of that "aerial water"—it descends from "heaven" (meaning the head of the **alembic** <note>The cooling cap of a still used in distillation</note>) and finds Latona in the vessel or flask, where she there gives birth to the **white and red elixirs** <note>The white elixir transmutes metals into silver; the red into gold.</note>.

They also feigned that Vulcan was the son of Jupiter and Juno, just as we also said above regarding Mars. However, because he was deformed and ugly of face, he was suddenly cast down onto the Island of Lemnos, and there nourished and raised by apes. By Vulcan, they understood the **sulfur** which we have already frequently warned is signified by the names of Mars and Fire. This sulfur, after **putrefaction** <note>The stage of decomposition or "blackness" in the alchemical work</note>, is joined and united to Jupiter and Juno, but remains different in nature regarding its melting point, thickness, and thinness. It is separated from them because—as we said in Book 3, Chapter 4—**quicksilver** <note>Mercury</note> does not receive any other sulfur except that which is of its own nature, namely **arsenic** <note>In this context, a specific "philosophical" arsenic, not necessarily the common poison</note> which partakes of the nature of salt. Therefore, they say Vulcan is their son because he is separated from their "womb." Yet, because he is foul and deformed due to his scorching oiliness, he is cast away to the island of Lemnos—that is, into the vessel...

To these things they added the fiction of Atalanta, the swiftest runner of all men, who was nevertheless overcome and defeated by three golden apples. By the Minotaur placed in the Labyrinth is signified our *quintessence* <term>Quinta Essentia: the "fifth essence" or purest spiritual core of matter</term>, which is ensnared and coagulated in the vessel or flask along with the aforementioned sulfurs.

**DE M.** I give you great thanks that you wished me to become a participant in such a great secret; that, setting aside all envy, you would open and explain to me through your singular kindness those things which were hidden by the ancients with the greatest zeal. For from this, I hope that I shall finally find the true sense of the fables and poetic fictions. Since I am satisfied in this part, let us leave these inquiries so that our begun discourse may return to where we departed when speaking of metals—namely, from which metal the aforementioned things ought to be made. For since they must be made from one alone, and yet not from common gold, I am persuaded they must be made from silver. This is not without reason, since you command that that gold must be accepted which is hidden in the depths of metals. Since the Moon <note>Silver</note> is gold in its depths—as you openly demonstrated in the *Testament*—it is without doubt that we must take silver. You also wrote in Book 1, Chapter 18, that the Moon is the "tincture of whiteness."

# DIALOGUE

And many Philosophers call the Moon <note>original: "Lunam"; in alchemy, the Moon represents Silver</note> the ferment of whiteness, and Physicians receive silver into their mixtures of medicines. Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and influential philosopher/scientist</note> further says at the end of the third book *On Minerals*, that we know for certain that for those things having a shared symbol in their matter, virtues, and natural powers, the transmutation into one another is easy. And a little further down, he says: "Let it not be hidden from you that in all things which have circular generations from one another, the transition of those which have agreement in more characteristics is easier; because of this, gold is also more easily made from silver than from any other metal." For in silver, it is not necessary to change anything except the color and the weight, and these things are easily done; for once the substance is compacted, the weight certainly adheres more closely when the watery part is diminished and the good, lemon-colored sulfur is increased, and consequently the color will be changed; and this is the method in other things as well.

<term>GEBER</term> <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of Islamic and Western alchemy</note>: I tell you for certain, if the whole substance of gold or silver were to be prepared by us without reducing it to its first matter, these would far excel the others, but in this way they are of little use. It is true that according to the "short path" mentioned above, gold can be made more easily from silver than from any other metal. This is because once the watery humidity is consumed through calcination <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile matter</note> and the substance is reduced, it becomes more fixed. Therefore, I said in Book 2, Chapter 18, that the weight increases, and through the smallest admixture of our good and prepared sulfur—which is white in its manifest appearance, but in its hidden nature is lemon-colored and reddish—after being cooked together for many days, it is finally changed in color. This is because the color of the tincture hidden in the sulfur will be made manifest through the power of heat, and this method is common in every metal. However, speaking of the preparation of the <term>elixir</term> <note>the "Philosopher's Stone" or agent of transmutation</note> according to the longer path of nine months—that is, by reducing the metals to their first matter—it cannot happen that gold or silver...

In responding to the arguments you brought forward, I say that even if the philosophers very often mean Jupiter <note>tin</note>, Venus <note>copper</note>, and Mercury <note>quicksilver</note> by the term "Moon" <note>silver</note>, nevertheless, by the word "Moon" one must also understand the Salt of Saturn <note>lead</note> signified by that name, which in its depths is the very "Gold of the Philosophers," as we have said above. We can also understand the "Moon" to mean the white elixir, for this is the tincture of whiteness, and the ferment of whiteness in multiplication; in secret, it is gold, and is most easily converted into gold because by continuing or strengthening the fire, the matter is more fixed, the weight increases, and the reddish sulfurous tincture, which lies hidden in the depths, is manifested. If, however, you wish to make any medicine from the Moon <note>silver</note> for the human body, it is made in the same way as we described above regarding gold.

**THE DISCIPLE:** If the aforementioned things must be made entirely from metals, yet cannot be made from perfect metals <note>gold and silver</note>, it is necessary to make them from imperfect metals, but only after they have been prepared.

-># DIALOGUE<-

There is no doubt when we speak of common gold: for this science treats imperfect bodies <note>The "imperfect bodies" refer to base metals like lead, tin, copper, and iron.</note> insofar as it is fitting to make them perfect, as I showed in the preface of the book *The Investigation of Perfection*. And Callisthenes <note>Likely referring to a pseudo-epigraphical alchemical author rather than the historical nephew of Aristotle.</note>, a most eminent man in this science, says that Alchemy is the science which grants the excellence and nobility of the superior metals to the inferior ones. And the Blessed Thomas <note>St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), to whom several alchemical treatises were falsely attributed in the Middle Ages.</note> says at the beginning of the fourth book of *Meteorology* that the primary goal of Alchemists is to transmute metals (namely the imperfect ones) according to truth and not by sophisticated <note>original: "sophisticè" — meaning deceptive or merely appearing to be gold without having its true essence.</note> means.

**DEM.** I think that through the thorough cooking of the **elixir**, imperfect metals are made perfect.

**GEB.** I say this: if the perfect elixir were prepared without the corruption of foreign things—and I demonstrated this clearly and openly enough in the prologue of the book *The Investigation*, and in the chapter on the preparation of the sharpest vinegar, and again in Book 1, Chapter 8—by saying that in the process of purification, imperfect metals may be reduced by ingenuity and industry through artifice into a pure and more noble substance in a short time, which cannot be done by nature. And in the book of the *Testament*, I said that every metal (understand this as the imperfect kind) can be converted through **calcination** <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state, often reducing it to a powder.</note> not only into salt, but even into true **Sun** <note>original: "Solem" — the alchemical term for Gold.</note> and true **Moon** <note>original: "Lunam" — the alchemical term for Silver.</note>. Nor can you object to me at this point that I meant perfect metals, since these *are* the true Sun and true Moon; nor is this done through projection <note>The final stage of the Great Work where the Philosopher's Stone is cast upon base metal to transmute it.</note>, but through calcination. Furthermore, we said in the book *The Investigation*, in the chapter on **vitriol**, that various vitriols <note>Sulfate minerals, often used as reagents in alchemical processes.</note> are extracted from imperfect bodies which are necessary and useful to us. We also said of various vitriols that in this art, not only alums but also sulfurs of various kinds are called vitriols. In Book 4, Chapter 9, speaking of the **tinting medicine** <note>Another name for the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone that "colors" or transforms the metal.</note> that changes Silver into Gold, we said this is done following a known and certain administration of the body...

# BOOK ONE

of the Imperfect <note>The text refers to "imperfect bodies," meaning base metals like lead or copper, as opposed to "perfect" metals like gold or silver.</note>. And if you have diligently studied my written works, you have surely noticed that in my book *On Roots* I put forward this opinion: namely, that when we see imperfect bodies—prepared by our experience and industry and cleansed of all superfluous corruption—acquire a greater clarity and splendor than bodies that are naturally perfect, we have finally, through that consideration, reached the goal of this work and science. In the book *The Mirror of Alchemy* it is written: if we find a material in which there is quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum"</note>, clean, <margin>*</margin>clear, white or reddish, congealed into a mass, but not yet brought to completion in such a way that we can reach its intimate and perfect cleansing and complete purity through our industry and natural talent—so that after the completion of the work it is a thousand times stronger and more perfect than other bodies cooked by simple natural heat—then we shall taste this delight, upon which every intention of the Philosophers is founded. Furthermore, Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant medieval scholar and theologian interested in natural science.</note> says in Book 3 of *On Minerals*, chapter 1, that it is characteristic of all metals to appear incomplete in their species, and therefore they are convertible into any other. However, the "medium" <term>medium: a substance acting as an intermediary or middle state</term> properly speaking has a formless nature, while the "extremes" retain a distinct, formed nature; because of this, the extremes are reduced from the mediums through talent and nature when the nature of one extreme is united upon the other. We must, however, highly note the nature of the mediums for the transmutation of metals, because in them consists the greatest knowledge of those things which strive to convert one into another.

These extremes are the white and red elixir <note>The white elixir transforms metals into silver; the red elixir transforms them into gold.</note>. **DEMOGORGON.** If I remember correctly, you said in that same place that the "perfect" is made perfect by this very mastery <term>magistery: the alchemical process of transformation</term>, and also that the perfect brings the imperfect to perfection, and finally you testify that these are altered and made perfect together.

**GEBER.** In that passage, by "perfect" I do not mean common gold or common silver, but the aforementioned prepared sulfurs which are partially fixed <note>Fixed substances are those that can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating.</note>. By "imperfect," however, I mean the aforementioned prepared salt signified by the name of quicksilver, and Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for the metal Tin.</note>. From these, one is brought to perfection by the other, since the sulfur thickens, fixes, and tints the Mercury <note>original: "Mercurium"</note>; Mercury, however, thins the sulfur and makes it fusible, softens it like wax <note>original: "incerat"</note>, and preserves the sulfur from combustion. In this way, one is altered by the other, and you can find this answer in Book 1, Chapter 11, in the middle of the chapter.

**DEMOGORGON.** In my opinion, you disapproved of this method in that place.

**GEBER.** I did indeed disapprove of that method in that place, as far as the pure and naked literal meaning is concerned; for I was not speaking of common bodies, but of the metals of the Philosophers. require a far different diligence in unrolling <note>original: "evoluendo," referring to the physical act of unrolling a scroll or opening a book</note> and a greater zeal in understanding. **DEMOCRITUS.** The matter itself will easily judge with how much zeal, diligence, and industry I have hitherto labored over your writings. Tell me, therefore, what the cause is that I have achieved less than I had hoped to achieve with such great effort. **GEBER.** Because you paid too little attention to what I said in Book 3, Chapter 12: that common lead and tin are unclean in their own proper root and in their first creation. For they possess an unclean substance, both of sulfur and of quicksilver <note>term: "argentum vivum" (quicksilver/mercury), considered by alchemists to be a primary component of all metals</note>. Since, indeed, the pure and the impure have been made into one substance and one essence, it is therefore not possible that by any industry through a medicine of the first order <note>term: "medicina primi ordinis," a basic alchemical preparation used for surface purification rather than total transformation</note>—that is, through some simple preparation—this earthiness can be removed from such unclean and impure quicksilver. For this reason, I had long ago utterly despaired, since I understood that this body could not be prepared so that it might finally reach the completion of its bright splendor. But returning to myself, I realized that these bodies are clean in the depths of their nature; therefore I said in Book 2, Chapter 7: if you sublimate <note>term: "sublimaveris," the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then solidifies again</note>—that is, if you extract—the quicksilver from common lead or tin, they appear black after sublimation. And further, in Book 4, Chapter 8, I said that in the softer bodies, by no wit or industry in this art can that rapid melting be removed, nor the impurity in the root of their beginning. We can also add to these things those which we advised in Book 2, Chapter 2: namely, since our sulfur and arsenic are also naturally joined with dregs <note>original: "fecibus," referring to the sediment or impurities</note> in no small measure, it is necessary that

He notes that while their inner nature is pure, their external forms remain black and "earthy" even after initial sublimation.</summary>

# DIALOGUE

they ascend during sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify materials</note>, and are carried upward along with their unpurified substance. Since, therefore, neither lead nor tin possesses a fixed substance—that is, one that is hard, earthy, and permanent in a violent fire—but rather one that flees from it, the spirits extracted from them through sublimation cannot ascend without these kinds of impurities. This is because they lack a fixed earth that remains stable in intense heat, which could hold back those impure parts and prevent them from rising with the spirits during the sublimation process. For this reason, I stated in the final chapter of Book 1 that those metals which, at their very root, participate in a greater infection of "lividity" <note>original: "infecione liuiditatis"; likely referring to the leaden, bluish-gray tarnish or internal corruption associated with base metals</note> require more labor and possess less perfection; these metals are lead and common tin.

**DEMOGORGON.** Yet you said, if I am not mistaken, in Book 3, Chapter 8, that you had observed the corruptions in tin were not joined to it at its very root, but were merely accidental. Since, therefore, they were not joined to it in the first mixing, they can surely be easily removed and separated from it. You also said in Chapter 10 that Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for Tin</note> is indeed clean, while Saturn <note>The alchemical name for Lead</note> is unclean. And in the *Book of Investigation*, you write that all supervening accidents <note>External or non-essential qualities that have been added to a substance</note> can be easily removed, and the bodies themselves can be fully and sufficiently prepared.

**GEBER.** Truly, if you had correctly understood those things which we pursued at length in our previous discourse, you would by no means have raised these objections to our response. # BOOK ONE

GEBER: You perhaps suspect that I have handed down deceptions and imperfect medicines. You certainly should have known long ago that our arsenic and the other materials we use are not those common substances known to everyone. Furthermore, we exhorted the practitioners of this art in Book 1, Chapter 5, to flee from deceptive works. I say, however, that the medicines of the first order are imperfect for this reason: because sulfur is the medicine of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti uiui," literally living silver or mercury</note>, which we have called Jupiter, Saturn, and the Moon <note>The planets represent Tin, Lead, and Silver.</note>. Quicksilver, moreover, is the medicine of sulfur, which we have called the Sun, Venus, and Mars <note>The planets represent Gold, Copper, and Iron.</note>. And these are in the first order—that is, in the first preparation, through which they are cleansed of superficial accidents—and in the second order—that is, in the second preparation, which is decoction <term>decoctio: a process of heating or boiling to extract essence</term> and fixation <term>fixio: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</term>, through which they are cleansed of intrinsic, combustible oiliness. They remain imperfect until they are perfectly cleansed and fixed. Truly, just as a man alone cannot live forever, it was necessary for him to be joined with a woman, so that through this union the human race might grow and multiply; in the same way, since our elixir would be consumed and finished in a short time when it is cast through many and frequent projections <note>The act of transforming base metal into gold by "throwing" the elixir upon it.</note>, and since repeating its preparation from the first principle requires much labor and a great expense of time, it was necessary—in order to shorten both the preparation and the fixation—to mix some part...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of alchemical transmutation, specifically focusing on the stages of fixation and the extraction of essences from "dregs" or ashes.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

The demonstration of the stone consists in this: that we make the volatile fixed, and this is **multiplication** <note>The process of increasing the quantity or potency of the alchemical medicine</note>. Behold, therefore, the first step is preparation, the second is fixation, and the third is multiplication. And just as our **elixir**, which becomes gold and silver, fixes and transmutes our **Mercury** <note>Quicksilver</note> in a few days, so also our **sulfur**, when it is fixed in part and possesses a metallic nature and splendor, likewise fixes our Mercury or quicksilver in a short time; for the volatile can never be fixed into a metal without the fixed.

Furthermore, the aforementioned quicksilver and **Jupiter** <note>The alchemical name for Tin</note> are extracted from the burnt dregs of salt through washing. **Rhazes** <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi, a famous Persian physician and alchemist</note> says in the book *The Light of Lights* <note>original: "Luminis luminum"</note>: when the dregs are whitened, we call them **magnesia**, and tin, and the "froth of Jupiter."

Moreover, the Philosopher **Mireris** says that the ashes must be taken, of which the Philosophers have spoken, saying: do not despise the ashes remaining in the lower part of the vessel, because in them is placed the **diadem of the heart**. He says the same: that what you seek is in the dregs.

**Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note>, therefore, speaking of the distillation of **Saturn** <note>The alchemical name for Lead</note>, says: Take it and exalt it in the tabernacle until it is entirely consumed into vapor and nothing more ascends, and preserve the sublimated water for the opportune time. Afterward, take the dregs remaining in the bottom of the flask or vessel and keep them diligently, for they themselves are the **crown of the heart**.

It is written in the book *The Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum," a famous early Latin alchemical text</note>: I command that you burn the body and convert it into ashes, for this burnt body is the **Phoenix**, which burns in the fire and is renewed by burning; for by burning this body, Jupiter is extracted from the ash, who, when joined with one part of the elixir, is always multiplied and increased. **Hermes** says: when you see the body converted into ashes, you have governed it perfectly. **Rhazes** likewise in the book of Light...

<summary>This page outlines the three stages of the Great Work—preparation, fixation, and multiplication—emphasizing that the "crown" or "diadem" of the work is found in the discarded ashes and dregs of the vessel. It cites authorities like Hermes, Rhazes, and the Turba Philosophorum to explain the symbolic death and rebirth of metals, likened to the Phoenix.</summary>

For the declaration of the second cause, you ought to know that by "weak bodies" I understood water and oil, which in distillation are preserved from combustion by descending into the receiving vessel after being reduced into a body from lime—that is, from their salts. Since in distilling we cannot distill all parts at one and the same time, that part which was first reduced into the **head** <note>original: "capitellum," the top part of a still or alembic</note> of the vessel does not wait for the part following it. If, however, the head receives a stronger fire, the greater part would be lost; therefore, it was necessary by skill and industry to lead out and take away that which has already ascended into the head through the beak of the head, and to collect it little by little in the receiving vessel, where it will find a cooling of the heat.

# BOOK ONE

By the salt, or rather the alum designated by the name of **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, etc., when combined with glass—that is, with the sulfur that is hidden within that salt—the entire earthy substance of the salt itself remains behind. But if by "body" we understand **arsenic**, that is, that subtle sulfur which is hidden in the salt itself, I say that it descends into the receiver through distillation. After this, with the glass (that is, in the *boccia* <note>A round-bottomed glass flask or cucurbit</note>) and with the salts or alums, all the earthy substance will remain at the bottom of the vessel. This prepared salt is called **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>.

Returning indeed to our discourse on the imperfect metals, I say that from the reasons written above, it is finally concluded that common **Venus** <note>Copper</note> and **Mars** <note>Iron</note>—although they possess great earthiness and accidental sulfurousness which may be easily removed—are nevertheless clean in their roots. Their sulfur and **quicksilver** <note>Mercury; original: "argentum uivum"</note> can be distilled or sublimated in a pure and clean state, since they have fixed and connatural parts joined to them, as we said in Book 2, Chapter 2. For this reason, we also said in Book 4, Chapter 3, that the **whitening** <note>dealbatio</note> of the substance of Venus and Mars is pure, and similarly the **reddening** <note>rubificatio</note> of the **Moon** <note>Silver; original: "Luna"</note>.

**DEMOCRITUS.** These things do not seem to me to have been uttered with this meaning.

**GEBER.** They can be understood in two ways. In one way, if we understand these things to be about common metals, I call the "whitening" the quicksilver, which is the white medicine of its own sulfur. By "reddening," however, one must understand the sulfur of Venus and Mars, which is the tincture of redness for that same quicksilver which we call the Moon; and these are pure in their root. But if by Venus and Mars we understand the aforementioned sulfurs extracted from one of them, I say that after **putrefaction** <note>The process of breaking down a substance through gentle heat, often resulting in a black color</note> they become clean, on account of the burning oiliness. And although they are reddish, they do not yet acquire that redness with the medicine of the first order—that is, through the first pre-

Having been changed from this first nature, and through long decoction <note>Latin: *decoctionem*; heating a substance in a liquid to extract its essence</note> they become fixed; this is the pure reddening of the Moon <note>The Moon is the alchemical symbol for Silver</note>, that is, the elixir of the white or of its own quicksilver <note>Latin: *argenti vivi*; mercury</note>.

In that place, you say that Venus <note>Venus is the alchemical symbol for Copper</note> shows the color and essence of Gold in the depths of its substance: for it is malleable and glows like silver and gold. Therefore, this secret is to be taken from it, since it is a certain medium between the Sun <note>The Sun is the alchemical symbol for Gold</note> and the Moon, and is easily converted from one tincture into another, being of good conversion and requiring little labor. It should therefore be taken before all other imperfect bodies in the minor and middle work, but less so in the greater work. 

And in Chapter 17 of the same book, you say further that you have known from the operation of nature that bronze or copper can be transmuted into gold. For in the mines of copper or bronze-pits, we see flowing water carrying with it scales of the finest bronze, which are washed and cleansed by the continuous downward flow of the water. When the flow of water ceases, I have seen those scales with dry sand after three years so "cooked" by the heat of the Sun that most true gold is found among them. Therefore, imitating nature as much as we are able, we shall alter the same. 

**GEBER.** Here indeed is the stumbling block <note>Latin: *scrupus*; literally a sharp stone or grit, metaphorically a difficulty</note> against which even those who thought themselves to know much in these arts have heavily struck, and were not a little offended.

...they are, nor do you remember that I told you that our subtle sulfur <note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible principle of a substance.</note> is the bronze or copper which outwardly displays a reddish color, but inwardly is black and white, and later red, malleable, and "fire-bearing"—that is, it melts and dissolves by fire like gold and silver, just as those things composed of sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argento uiuo"; mercury</note> do. Because an easy and superficial dye <note>original: "tinctura"; the power to change the color/nature of a metal</note> is neither useful nor permanent, and since it is an intermediate between the Sun and the Moon <note>The Sun (Sol) represents Gold; the Moon (Luna) represents Silver.</note>—that is, sulfur and quicksilver—it is easily converted from one dye into the other; that is, it will easily receive a lemon-yellow color. However, this easy dye is not useful.

->BOOK ONE<-

it does not possess the nature of salt: for this reason, before its preparation, it is more imperfect than the other metals, and its preparation is the longest of all, on account of its great oiliness <note>original: "unctuositatem," referring to the alchemical principle of Sulfur which was thought to give metals their combustible or oily quality</note>, its earthiness, and the difficulty of its dissolution. **DISCIPLE:** In your writings, you have warned us in many places not to take that stone which you revealed in many chapters. Therefore, if you now say this stone is common Mars <note>Mars is the alchemical name for Iron</note>, or rather its lime <note>original: "calcem," referring to the powdered oxide produced by heating a metal</note>, and by Mars you understand its sulfur, and you command it to be taken—if I then take common Mars, I shall err; and if I take sulfur, I shall likewise err, if this secret lies hidden within common Mars. Tell me then, I ask you, will you describe the practice <note>original: "praxin"</note> of this art?

**GEBER:** Most certainly. **DISCIPLE:** It will therefore be necessary to speak of the preparation of common iron, if the secret lies hidden within it. **GEBER:** I do indeed speak of it, but occultly <note>meaning "hidden" or "secretly"</note>, namely in the chapter on general matters, where I discuss the preparation of all metals, of Marcasite <note>a term often used for various metallic sulfides or base minerals</note>, of salt, sulfur, arsenic, quicksilver <note>original: "argenti uiui," or Mercury</note>, and the like. **DISCIPLE:** In what way did you reveal this secret in different chapters? **GEBER:** I said in many places that a greater secret must be sought in Mars: I did this with deliberate effort, so that no malicious person could reproach me or accuse me of insufficiency; and it is indeed true that I have handed this down in a certain specific way of speaking. I did this so that those who are ignorant might understand something other than what I intended, and believe my speech was uttered in another sense, by clinging too closely to our literal words. However, the wise and prudent, who know our manner of speaking, perceive these things easily. The poet Virgil also used this manner of speaking when he said that golden gold cannot be cut down by iron, except by those who point the way. <note>original: "ferro aurum aureum non detruncari posse, nisi indicantibus." This is a cryptic alchemical interpretation of Virgil's poetry.</note> **DISCIPLE:** Come then, since you have revealed this secret to me, show me—since this is the conclusion of all those things which, through a long...

<summary>The master Geber explains to his disciple that alchemical secrets regarding the preparation of Iron (Mars) are intentionally hidden behind metaphorical language to protect them from the unworthy. He emphasizes that while the literal text might mislead the ignorant, the "wise" will understand the hidden practice within his general descriptions of minerals.</summary>

these things were handed down to me by your discourse. **GEBER:** I said in Book 1, Chapter 15, that quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note> easily adheres to individual metals, but to Mars <note>Iron</note> in no way, except through craftsmanship. From these words, shake out the highest secret: namely, from Mars. Furthermore, I said in Book 9, Chapter 9, that Jupiter <note>Tin</note> approaches closer to perfection, Saturn <note>Lead</note> less so, and Venus <note>Copper</note> still less, but Mars least of all—in which all perfection is situated, and upon which the elixir itself depends (although in certain of our copies this text is read in a corrupt form). In the same Book 1, Chapter 17, I said that Mars is the last, and this is the greatest secret of nature. To these things I said in Book 3, Chapter 9, that the cause of the impediment of any fusion is fixed sulfur; from these words you will be able most certainly to track down the greatest secret. By that "fixed sulfur" in this place, I understand common iron, for the reason that the greater part of it is fixed and earthy sulfur, as we said in Book 3, Chapter 6; for a designation is made from the predominant element. In Book 4, Chapter 14, speaking differently of Mars, I said that through this, the industry of the craftsman is most certainly extended to the true rectification <note>The process of refining or purifying a substance</note> of any body. Through this—that is, through common iron, or if it pleases you to understand sulfur by "Mars"—it is the same; the truth will be perceived by either part through a short path. It is extracted, however, from common iron also; therefore, concluding in Book 3, Chapter 6, speaking of the essence of Mars, I exclaimed, returning immense thanks to God, the Best and Greatest, who created this, and gave to it a substance, and to that substance a property, which it would not be fitting for any other natural thing to possess: that such perfection can be found in it through craftsmanship. For in this we have found a "near power," because in its lime <note>original: "calce"; the powdery residue left after heating a metal</note> it overcomes fire, nor is it overcome by it, but marvelously rests in it, refreshing itself from the fire.

# BOOK ONE. 167

because iron is restored in its strength by fire, and in fire it is always made more beautiful, brighter, and better. Therefore, Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note> says in the *Rosary*, it is manifest that those bodies are more perfect which contain more Mercury <term>Mercurius: the philosophical principle of fluidity and transformation</term> within themselves, while those that contain less are of lesser perfection. Therefore, let the glorious and blessed God, the craftsman of all things, be praised and blessed—the best and greatest cause of all things—who created a most precious thing within a common thing. **DEM.** I do not grasp in what way a most precious elixir <term>Elixir: the "philosopher's stone" or medicine capable of transmuting metals</term> can be created from such a common metal, for it is more likely that something more precious would be procreated from something already precious. **GEB.** <note>Geber: the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational authority of medieval alchemy</note> However much common iron may be cheaper in itself than all other metals, nevertheless, once its impurity has been separated from it, it turns out much more precious than the others, because it possesses a near potential for converting itself into the elixir. Wherefore Rudigenus the philosopher says: it must be known that every metal contains the Sun <note>original: "Solem" - representing Gold</note> and Mercury in its hidden part, and we have extracted quicksilver from iron, and from that iron Alchemy was afterwards made, and from its color we have wrought a good work at the limit of the fire. The same philosopher says further: We say the greater stone is Mercury, and this is that very thing which was extracted from that stone which we have shown. Let us therefore explain what that stone is, and the method of extracting it from the quicksilver within it. It is found in every place, encountered by everyone, and every man uses it, and its name is Mars <note>The planetary and alchemical name for Iron</note>. Take this, therefore, and grind it most finely, and wash it. Raymond <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher to whom many alchemical works were attributed</note> says that without iron, men cannot sustain their lives. Moreover, the Elder Philosopher <note>likely referring to Senior Zadith, an 10th-century Arabic alchemist</note>, speaking of iron, says: "I am iron—hard, strong, compact, beaten; every good thing is through me, and the light itself and the secret are generated through me." Furthermore, Avicebron <note>Solomon ibn Gabirol, an 11th-century Sephardic philosopher</note> and Rhazes <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi, a Persian physician and alchemist</note> say that the body of iron is stronger than other bodies, and the stone is from it, and the wise have chosen this.

It cites various authorities like Arnold of Villanova, Geber, and Raymond Lull to prove that iron's strength and endurance in fire make it the ideal base for alchemical transformation.</summary>

**DEMOGORGON.** Let this opinion rest for a moment, and if you have any certain reasons, bring them forward; for I will be more easily drawn to your view by definite reasons than by long, winding speeches.

**GEBER.** You should know that I have by no means spoken without certain reasons. For in the *Book of the Testament* <note>referring to the *Liber Testamenti*, a foundational alchemical text attributed to Geber</note> I said that **Mars** <note>original: "Martem"; the alchemical name for Iron</note>, since it possesses a fixed substance, is better than the others. And although the name of Mars can also be understood as **sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and "masculine" principle of metals</note> in respect to other internal metals, it can nevertheless be understood by these words that **common iron** is better than the other metals because of its fixed substance—that is, because it possesses more fixed sulfur than the other metals.

**DEMOGORGON.** I believed that the sulfur of **gold** <note>original: "auri"</note> was more fixed.

**GEBER.** You speak the truth, but it stands at a higher price; nor is labor upon it useful, nor can it be improved, since it has already reached the highest perfection. However, the sulfur of iron is already on the way toward the ultimate perfection and **tincture** <note>a substance capable of transmuting or coloring metals</note>, and it can be assisted by our **mastery** <note>original: "magisterio"; the alchemical process or "Great Work"</note> so that it may finally reach the perfect redness. Furthermore, an abundance of it is easily supplied to us, and without a great expense of price. By "fixed substance" I still mean the earthy part, because in Book 3, Chapter 6, it was said by us: "If fixed earthy sulfur is mixed with earthy **quicksilver** <note>original: "argento uiuo"; Mercury</note>, the best common iron is made from that mixture."

**DEMOGORGON.** This exceeds the grasp of my wit, nor can I easily be led to believe or be persuaded that because it is more "earthy," it stands far above the rest. I had long ago determined for myself, and am still of the opinion, that our **medicine** <note>original: "medicina"; the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone</note> ought to be made from a purer material, separated from all earthiness.

**GEBER.** I do not say that the earthiness of iron is good in that work, but rather that the **spirits** innate to it, and nourished within the earthiness of the iron, are better and more virtuous than the spirits nourished...

<summary>Geber argues that iron is a superior base for alchemical work because its "fixed sulfur" is already progressing toward perfection, unlike gold which is already complete and costly. Demogorgon expresses skepticism, questioning how a seemingly "earthy" and impure metal could be the best source for the Elixir.</summary>

# BOOK ONE

ground into a small amount of earth and other elements of the remaining metals; and this has been proven by Raymond <note>Likely referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and alchemist to whom the work "On the Secrets of Nature" was attributed.</note> in his book *On the Secrets of Nature*, while discussing iron under the name of the **terrestrial element**. For he says that the earth, through the power of rays, produces three principal parts—namely the animal, vegetable, and mineral—and that it produces things so terrible <note>In the Latin sense of *terribilis*, meaning "awe-inspiring" or "formidable."</note> they cannot be described, which happens very little with the other elements. Indeed, the more a thing descends toward the terrestrial element, the more the **celestial power** increases within it.

Therefore, water and spirit immersed in the terrestrial element, through a likeness in natural matter, imitate and draw out the property and power from the matter of that place. Because iron participates in the earth more than all other metals, its waters and spirits will be more powerful than the spirits of other metals. Just as a grain of wheat falling into the earth bears no fruit unless it dies <note>A common alchemical metaphor for the process of "mortification" or breaking down a substance to release its essence.</note>, in the same way, the spirit and living seed of gold falling into the "earth" of iron produces no fruit unless it dies through **putrefaction** and **decoction** <term>decoction: the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence</term>.

Another reason can also be brought forward as to why iron is better and more excellent in this work, even if it possesses much "earthiness" <note>original: "terrestreitatis"</note>. This is because this earthiness is more easily and better separated from iron than in other metals, since they are not so strongly mixed and have no affinity with the radical part. Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant figure in medieval science and alchemy.</note> confirms this in Book 4 of *Meteorology*, treatise 4, saying: iron has a great deal of earth, and is marvelous in its earthiness; and although nothing is as easily damaged by fire as water, the opposite occurs in iron. For the earth is burned by fire much more quickly than the watery substance itself. The cause of this matter is such: because the earthy part mixed into the composition of iron is muddy, not well purified, nor well blended. The most certain proof of this...

The clearest evidence for us is that it is easily covered with rust <note>original: "ferrugine"</note>, and when it is scorched in the fire, many small scales of burnt earthiness fall from it. For its earthiness is porous, and the fire can scorch it from every side, just as we have confirmed more extensively in Book 3, Chapter 6. We also indicated this reasoning in Book 4, Chapter 14, when discussing calcination <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to cause oxidation or powdering.</note> under the name of "ash-testing" <note>original: "cineritij," referring to cupellation or refining in a furnace.</note>, with these words: Those imperfect bodies which possess more earthiness are less able to endure and last in that trial of calcination and the separation of earth, because they are calcined more quickly, and their earthiness is easily separated. However, those metals which have less earthiness—since they are much better mixed and purified—endure longer in the fire and in the trial of their earthly separation. 

This reasoning is further confirmed by what we said in Book 2, Chapter 7, when speaking of the sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then cools back into a solid, used here for purification.</note> of Mercury: for there we said that the ingenuity or industry of separating the superfluous earth from Mercury itself consists of mixing it with those things with which it has no affinity, and by frequently repeating its sublimation over the dregs <note>original: "feces," the solid remains after distillation or sublimation.</note>. By these words, I wished to indicate the Mercury of iron, which is a white salt naturally joined with some white earthiness, having a certain resemblance to the whiteness of gypsum, or the lime of eggshells and white marble, and similar things. 

Since, however, this earthiness is not well mixed, nor has it any affinity or any suitability with that Mercury, they are therefore easily separated, even in calcination and the washing of sulfur, although I said that sublimation must be repeated more often. In conclusion, therefore, I said that the sublimation of Mercury is better when performed from those metals with which it in no way agrees, than with those with which it does agree. We know for certain that it agrees least of all with Mars <note>The alchemical name for Iron.</note> and with its earthiness, than with

In Book 2, Chapter 15, I said this is the property of salt in the fire: not to be burned before fusion, and to preserve the mixed sulphurous spirits from the scorching of the fire. Therefore, the Mercurial spirit, which after equal preparation will better preserve the bodily nature of its salt, will better defend the sulphurous spirits from the combustion which the Mercurial spirits dissolved in water are likely to undergo. For the more subtle and dissolved they are, the more they will approach the nature of fire, and the more quickly they are burned, as we said in Book 2, Chapter 2. It is certain, however, that if all the Mercurial spirits of the metals could be equally prepared, the spirit of Mars would better preserve its good bodily nature; for which reason it is also much less scorched and will better preserve the sulfur from vitrification <note>the process of turning into glass, often seen as a failure in the "fixation" of alchemical substances</note>, that is, from combustion. Since, however, all metals are by their nature more subtle than iron, they become even more refined when prepared equally; in their <term>distillation</term> <note>The process of purifying a liquid by heating and cooling</note>, almost their entire substance is dissolved, which is why they also burn more quickly. By "filings of the body," we understand prepared sulfur, similar to the thinnest filings of silver. **Albert** <note>Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century philosopher and scientist</note> also brings forward another reason which applies to common iron and to his sulfur called by the name of Mars in Book 4 of *On Minerals*, chapter 1. <meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the chemical properties of metals and their suitability for alchemical transformation, specifically focusing on the role of iron versus gold.</meta>

# DIALOGUE

Its odor indicates a terrestrial substance within it that is highly flammable and combustible, and its stench shows it to be largely undigested <note>In alchemy, "digestion" refers to the process of refining or maturing a substance through heat</note> and unfinished. Rather, by a corrupting heat, it belongs to an undigested body rather than one perfected through complete digestion. Because of its specific temperament <note>original: "complexione"</note>, it is able to serve as the body or universal matter of all metals. If it were perfected to one fixed temperament, it would undoubtedly be impossible to convert it into other things unless that state were first removed. But now, because of its temperament, it is convertible into all things—just as the female and other sources from which the things of nature are generated. Therefore, wise Nature provides an abundance of sulfur wherever there is a place for the generation of metals. Since iron also abounds in sulfur, it is for this reason a proper site for the metals of the philosophers.

By these reasons, you will certainly perceive that iron is closer to our art than gold. For iron is more [readily] digested and undetermined; gold, however, since it is already digested and fixed, cannot have its determined temperament removed without great and heavy labor to render it incomplete and undetermined again. Otherwise, it cannot be converted into anything else. This is the principal reason why transmutation does not occur in metals unless they are first reduced to their **prime matter** <term>materia prima: the original, formless base substance from which all things are made</term>.

Albertus <note>Referring to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar and patron saint of scientists</note> proves the same thing in another way and by other reasons in *On Minerals*, Book 3, Chapter 1. He clearly demonstrates that iron itself is a unique mineral. Since it is the specific property of a stone not to be melted by fire, while the property of metals is to be dissolved and melted by the hot and dry, for this reason stones are said to be of the "dry and earthy" genus, while metals are of the "moist" genus. Since both of these properties meet in iron, we shall not doubt that it is a true middle mineral.

<summary>The text argues that iron is a better starting material for alchemy than gold because it is "undetermined" and "undigested," allowing it to be more easily transformed into the philosopher's stone. It cites Albertus Magnus to define iron as a "middle mineral" that shares properties of both stone and metal.</summary>

<keywords>iron, gold, sulfur, Albertus Magnus, prime matter, transmutation, digestion, mineralogy, alchemy</keywords>

to affirm: for it participates in much earthiness and a stony substance which prevent it from being melted like other metals. It was even called by the name "stone" by the philosophers <note>The "philosophers" usually refers to ancient alchemists or natural historians.</note> because of its excellence. But since it also melts in a strong fire, as Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant medieval scholar of natural sciences.</note> affirms in Book 4 of *Meteorology*, tract 4, chapter 5, and possesses the weight and luster of a metal, it therefore participates in the metallic nature.

**DEM.** Albertus attributes this property to Marcasite <note>original: "Marchasitę"; in early chemistry, this referred to various metallic sulfides like pyrite.</note> in the following chapter.

**GEB.** The philosophers, because of the agreement in matter and name, have spoken of Mars <note>The alchemical name for Iron.</note> and its lime <note>original: "calce"; the powdery residue left after heating a metal.</note> under the name of Marcasite. Indeed, Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), author of the Etymologies.</note> says in Book 15 that Quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum uiuum"; Mercury.</note> is specifically found in metals, and is of such strength that if you place a stone of one hundred pounds weight upon it, it suddenly resists such a great weight; but if you cast in a single scruple of gold, it suddenly receives the gold into its bosom.

**DEM.** I do not understand how it is possible for quicksilver to resist such a great weight of a stone placed upon it.

**GEB.** Do you not notice that Isidore spoke in this part concerning the quicksilver which is in the iron itself? For this sustains a great substance of earthiness or stone; however, if you distill this Mercury <note>original: "Mercurium"; used here interchangeably with quicksilver.</note>, its own sulfur—called gold—is submerged in that distillate, as I said in Book 1, chapter 15. The ancients extolled iron with many praises, but among the other things they attribute to this praise, the chief point is that iron is the proper mine of quicksilver. The reason for this thing is this: since according to the mind of Rhazes <note>original: "Rafis"; Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and influential alchemist.</note> in his *Book of Divinity*, the manifest <note>The outward, observable qualities.</note> is always contrary to the hidden; and the manifest quality of iron is hot, dry, and hard. Therefore, by the reasoning of Rhazes already mentioned, its hidden quality is cold, moist, and soft—that is, quicksilver. For this reason, the same Rhazes says in the place already cited, that the hidden...

You set this down as a certain sign that they are themselves dry in their manifest form. Therefore, Rhazes <note>original: "Rasis"</note> understands in this passage the sulfur which we call Mars, which after sublimation is whitened like silver; surely, in the same way, when you speak of this sulfur and demonstrate its washing, you are speaking of the washing of mercury.

This is because, after putrefaction <note>the process of breaking down a substance through rot or fermentation</note>, the entire substance of common Mars is reddish, like blood; and beneath this redness is the mercury itself in the form of a white salt, like lime. Indeed, Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> says that every secret consists in nitre and salt. **DEMOCRITUS:** I am unable to understand who this "paschal virgin" might be. 

**GEBER:** The ancients hid the secrets of nature not only in writings, but also under various pictures, characters, ciphers, monsters, and animals, depicted and transformed in diverse ways. These were seen carved before the doors of temples and palaces, but were understood by no one, except those who possessed some knowledge of this secret.

# BOOK ONE

<page-num>117</page-num>
-> FIRST <-

...brought forth knowledge. The "Paschal Virgin" <note>original: "Virgo paschalis"; likely a coded term for a specific purified mineral or plant extract used in the Great Work</note> is a natural herb, possessing the property and likeness of Mars. The ancients, however, delighting in similarities and allegories, named iron the "Paschal Virgin." They used to depict her in the form of a woman with her hair spread out and scattered; upon her crown was placed the character of Mercury, in the middle the character of the half-moon <note>representing Silver</note>, and at the lowest part the character of the Sun <note>representing Gold</note>. By this figure they wished to signify that, just as hair is indigestible even if it is soft and fine, so also the most subtle radical parts of iron are incorruptible and incombustible; and within them, Gold, Silver, and the Mercury of the Philosophers <term>Mercury of the Philosophers: a sophisticated, "living" essence of mercury sought by alchemists, distinct from common quicksilver</term> are contained. For Mercury is lighter than silver, and silver is lighter than gold. Therefore, the philosopher Rudienus says that the beginning of this work consists in the entry of the Sun into Aries <note>the spring equinox</note>. Likewise, Rasis <note>Rhazes, the 9th-century Persian physician and alchemist</note> says in his *Book of Divinity*: "Take the stone after the entry of the Sun into Aries."

**DEMOCRITUS:** I was persuaded that they spoke of the first degree of fire, which ought to be light—that is, warm and moist like the air in springtime—as Aristotle says in the *Book of Secrets* to Alexander: "Spring begins with the Sun entering the sign of Aries."

**GEBER:** This opinion of yours does not displease me. However, it was the intention of the philosophers to show by these words that the natural principles of this work are Iron, or Mars, for Aries is the "house" of Mars <note>in astrology, Mars is the planetary ruler of the zodiac sign Aries</note>. Indeed, when the Sun enters Aries, it begins to ascend through many degrees; but upon entering Leo, it ascends no further, but now begins to descend. This signifies that the Sun <note>Gold</note>, which is imperfect within Mars <note>Iron</note>, can be brought to perfection by the Art; whereas Gold itself cannot ascend any higher, and thus through calcination or solution it descends and becomes imperfect. Rasis also says in the *Book of Divinity* that this matter is taken from animals that are warmer and better than all others...

...to be carried, that is, by a man of a choleric temperament <note>original: "complexionis cholericæ"; in medieval medicine, a choleric person was thought to be dominated by yellow bile, making them hot and dry in nature</note>.

As Razi <note>Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and alchemist</note> says, Mars is externally hot and dry, fiery and choleric; for this reason, men born under the influence of Mars are also warlike and choleric. Furthermore, iron is called a "man" because it possesses a soul, a body, and a spirit (as we said above) that are healthy; for it is pure in its root, young and strong, because it is hard and of great strength. It is also called the **microcosm** <note>original: "minor mundus" or "lesser world"; the idea that a single object or human contains the blueprint of the entire universe</note> for the same reason that it is called a man, or rather because it can be divided into the four elements. This most virtuous metal, although generated by the power and influence of all the stars and planets as Roger Bacon <note>13th-century English philosopher and friar</note> affirms, was specifically generated in the earth by the most powerful and virtuous strength of the Pole Star, which the Italians call *Tramontana* and the Greeks call *Arctos*. A certain proof of this (as recorded by Raymond Lully and John of Rupescissa) is the needle of the nautical compass, which always looks toward the pole and turns itself as if toward its own kind. And we spoke above of the "Net of Vulcan" being made of steel, and that the Golden Fleece, the bulls, and the Dragons were all dedicated to Mars. **DE M.** I remember it well. **GE B.** I wished to signify by those words that Mercurial water, the Mercury and the Gold of the Philosophers, are all made from iron, and that iron is that stone which is sold at a cheap price compared to other metals. For this stone was called by various names by the ancient philosophers so that the foolish would not recognize it; for if they recognized it, they would not sell it at such a low price. And in the *Book of the Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note>, it is said: "That stone which you seek is sold publicly and at a cheap price everywhere." Arnold <note>likely Arnold of Villanova, a 13th-century alchemist</note> calls it the "vegetable stone nourished in the mountains"; many who wander in error do not recognize...
