...they [the ancients] have stated; it is sold everywhere and at a cheap price. **DEMOGORGON.** You said above that our medicine could not be made from a vegetable substance. **GEBER.** "Vegetable iron" <note>original: "Ferrum uegetabile"; likely referring to green vitriol or ferrous sulfate, which "grows" like a plant in crystalline form</note> is so called not only because it is composed of green vitriol and produces black ink from it, but because it is transmuted by means of fire and has a near potential to be converted into the elixir. **DEMOGORGON.** You said before that nothing should be spent to acquire the necessities of this art for ourselves, since it is a most vile thing of no price. All the philosophers affirm the same. For Alphidius <note>An Arab alchemist often cited in medieval Latin texts</note> says this secret is not acquired for a price, but is found cast away in the middle of the road, so that it can be possessed even by the poor. It is written, moreover, in the book *The Lily of Alchemy*, that the foundation of this art is one single thing, more deeply tinged, stronger, and more sublime than all other things. Likewise, in the book *The Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba philosophorum," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note> we read: "It is nothing else than what the philosophers have indicated to us; that is, it is encountered by everyone—rich and poor, generous and greedy, those walking and those sitting—when it is cast in the road and buried in dung-heaps." **GEBER.** I concede that iron is reduced into the form of a most vile stone from which the elixir is made. Aristotle speaks of this stone to Alexander <note>Referring to the "Secretum Secretorum," a popular medieval text attributed to Aristotle writing to Alexander the Great</note> and says: "Take the vegetable, animal, and mineral stone, which is not a stone, nor has the nature of a stone; and if it shall have the water of air, and the air of fire, and the fire of earth, you shall possess the whole art." **DEMOGORGON.** These things seem impossible to me. **GEBER.** The philosophers, speaking metaphorically or through similarities, have not lied; for Aristotle, in the words just recited, teaches you the distillation and separation of the elements. Arnold <note>Likely referring to Arnaldus de Villa Nova, a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note> speaking of this stone, says: "the stone is such in appearance and touch, but not in its inner nature." GEBER. <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the legendary father of alchemy</note> Certainly I tell you that the matter from which this medicine is made has the likeness of a very cheap stone at the beginning of the work; and we clearly stated in Book 1, Chapter 5, that the Stone is a single, unique material in which the whole mastery <term>magisterium: the complete alchemical process or "Great Work"</term> consists, and that in its preparation we remove the superfluities. Therefore, we cannot understand these things as referring to the finished elixir, since it has no superfluities; nor is it true that all things which can be divided into the four elements, or are not consumed by fire, are stones or have the likeness of a stone. 

But the philosophers wished to signify that their matter has the likeness of a cheap stone, and that it is not consumed in the fire and can be divided into the four elements, and that this is the foundation of the entire art. The philosopher Hali says that this stone is found at all times, in every place, and around every person. For that reason, its discovery is neither troublesome nor difficult for anyone. It is a cheap stone, black, hard, and foul-smelling, which is obtained at no price. Furthermore, we read in the *Book of the Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, translated from Arabic</note> that this thing is a stone and not a stone, found in every place, a thing both cheap and precious, obscure...

And however much that lime appears arid, dry, and deprived of moisture, nevertheless if you perform the experiment, you will find the things spoken of above. This calx has no combustible or oily sulfurousness because we have separated it. From the first calx, however, we do not only distill weak bodies, but also the fixed sulfur called by the name of Mars <note>Alchemical code for the metal Iron</note>.

Furthermore, we said in Book 1, Chapter 14, that arsenic and sulfur are fixed, and the sublimation of both is better achieved from the calxes of metals. And in Book 2, Chapter 2, I said that experience teaches this to be necessary; for those who have put this matter to the test have realized they labored in vain by sublimating things foreign and extraneous to the nature of arsenic and sulfur. This is because no purification could be found after their ascent <note>The rising of vapors during heating</note>. Yet, when sublimated with the calx of some metallic body, they were finally able to sublimate well and purify perfectly with ease.

Our intention regarding the dregs <note>original: "feces"</note> administered there from the metallic calxes is that sublimation becomes easier and more expeditious because of them; therefore, no other thing can be substituted in their place. We do not say, however, that sublimation is impossible without the calxes of bodies, but it is far more difficult, of the slowest labor, and a prolongation leading even unto despair. It was of these calxes that John of Rupescissa <note>A 14th-century Franciscan alchemist known for his work on the "quintessence"</note> spoke...

# BOOK ONE

...by speaking of divided things, he says he will hand over the method of enchantment <note>original: "incantationis"; here likely referring to a chemical process or "binding" of the metal</note> and the reduction of gold and silver into earth and lime <note>calx: a powder produced by heating a metal or mineral</note>, so that they may be carried publicly in the hand during times of war without fear of thieves.

**DE M.** As soon as I have obtained some leisure, I shall try this operation, since it costs such a small price.

**GEB.** Pay attention diligently lest you err.

**DE M.** If an error is made anywhere, I will repeat the work from the beginning.

**GEB.** I am not speaking of the practice, but note the material.

**DE M.** I shall take that lime of iron which you indicated to me.

**GEB.** In that, I say, an error can be committed, perhaps when you take a lime which will be little suited to this business.

**DE M.** Therefore show me a better one.

**GEB.** You will find a certain kind of this lime that is brittle, and this is of iron, yet still little fit for this operation. You will find also another kind, brittle indeed, but white in its fracture; nor is this good, because it still retains something of the nature of iron, and has much oily and combustible sulfurousness <note>original: "sulphuritatis unctuosae"; refers to the alchemical principle of Sulfur, thought to cause flammability and impurity</note>. But when you see it converted into the nature of glass by the greatest violence of fire, of a color almost green or dark sapphire or dark sky-blue, this very thing is the true and most virtuous <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and achieving immortality</note> which is generated in the fire; and the longer it has been in the fire, the more it will be improved.

**DE M.** I give you the greatest thanks for such great liberality; nevertheless, I will say with your permission what I feel. You said in Book 2, Chapter 13: *Everything that is deprived of moisture is not melted, except for glass-making substances.* Therefore, iron converted into the substance of glass by the most vehement heat of the fire is deprived of moisture, and consequently it will also be deprived of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> and other spirits.

**GEB.** According to the opinion of Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant medieval authority on natural philosophy and alchemy</note> in Book 3 of *On Minerals*, Chapters 2 and 5: There are in every metal two oilinesses <note>unctuositates</note>, of which one is as it were extrinsic, subtle, and flammable; but the other is in-

<meta>catchword: trin (internal/intrinsic)</meta>

<summary>This page features a dialogue on alchemical practice, specifically describing how to refine iron into a glass-like state to create the Philosophers' Stone. The speakers debate the role of moisture and "oiliness" in metals, citing the authority of Albertus Magnus.</summary>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the chemical transformation of metals, specifically focusing on the nature of glass-like substances derived from iron.</meta>

# DIALOGUE

<margin>The internal moisture of metal</margin>
...retained internally within the root of the metal so that it would not be exhausted or dissipated by the fire; and this substance is neither capable of being cremated nor combustible. It is certainly true that this glass is the **radical moisture** <note>The "radical moisture" was a medieval concept referring to the essential, life-giving fluid or fundamental humidity within a substance that preserves its form.</note> of iron, and for that reason, it is incombustible and not entirely deprived of moisture. For if it were in some way deprived of its external moisture, it would be flammable.

The same **Albertus** <note>Referring to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant medieval philosopher and alchemist.</note> in the book already cited, chapter 4, says this concerning glass: he denies that the material which enters into the substance of glass is merely ashes. Rather, it is the purest radical moisture that was retained intrinsically within the incinerated matter itself. This moisture could not be extracted by the power of an ordinary fire, but it flows under the strongest fire; and this is that moisture which has experienced the greatest dryness. Therefore, this matter is liquefiable and is the **prime and remote matter**, one and common—that is, the very moisture of this genus.

The same Albertus, in Book 4 of the *Meteorologica*, treatise 2, chapter 9, says that vehement heat gathers together homogeneous parts and separates heterogeneous ones, because it dissolves the moisture and separates it from the earth. However, the moisture received from the earth cannot be entirely separated; therefore, it begins to be distilled with the subtle earthy part, and by a very strong fire, it is melted into glass. The coarse earthy part, however, which held more firmly, is burned into ash.

And we said in Book 2, chapter 13, that fire by its greatest force reduces matter into glass. Therefore, it is manifest that glass is a part of the radical moisture of iron, combined with coarse earthiness. When those parts which hindered fusion in the iron are burned into ash, the glass becomes fusible because it has now returned to its liquefiable nature; and because its pores are closed, the fire cannot penetrate and destroy it.

**Vincent the Monk** <note>Vincent of Beauvais (c. 1184–1264), author of the massive encyclopedia *Speculum Maius*.</note> writes in Book 7 of his *Natural History* that it is called "a stone and not a stone." For it is called a **stone** because it can be ground down, and **not a stone** because it may be melted and flow in the fire without evaporation.

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical theory of glass formation from iron, arguing that glass represents the "radical moisture" or essential essence of the metal that survives extreme heat. It cites authorities like Albertus Magnus and Vincent of Beauvais to explain how intense fire separates pure, fusible matter from combustible waste.</summary>

<keywords>radical moisture, Albertus Magnus, Vincent of Beauvais, alchemy, iron, glassmaking, metallurgy, fusion, prime matter</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a dialogue regarding the properties of the Philosopher's Stone, specifically its relationship with fire. # BOOK ONE

as gold, nor does this property belong to any other thing.
**DEMOCRITUS:** When the Philosophers say that their Stone is not consumed in the fire, they undoubtedly mean to speak of the finished medicine <note>The "finished medicine" refers to the Elixir or Stone in its final, perfected state.</note>.
**GEBER:** That is a most wretched gloss <note>A "gloss" is an interpretation or commentary on a text.</note> which corrupts and perverts the original text; for I tell you that by those words they understood the very principle and foundation of this work. This is also confirmed for us by George Bacco the Englishman <note>original: "Georgius Bacco Anglicus"; likely referring to Roger Bacon, the 13th-century Franciscan friar and scientist</note> in these words: "I say of every quality that it ought to be fixed upon the fire—namely, so that they do not flee from it, nor are they consumed or corrupted by the fire, and finally that they do not change color in the fire; for these are the things that provide the beginning of this work."
Furthermore, Alphidius <note>An Arabic alchemical author often cited in medieval Latin texts</note> says that fire undergoes and enters all bodies, spirits, substances, and stones, but it cannot enter this Stone nor have dominion over it, nor can it corrupt the sulfurs within it. Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova, a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note> says to the King of Naples that the longer this Stone endures in the fire, the more it increases and grows in goodness and virtue. This can suit no other thing, because all other things are consumed by fire, but the said Stone alone is improved in the fire and grows in goodness; for fire is the very nourishment of this Stone, and this is the most certain sign for recognizing the Stone.
Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina, the Persian polymath</note> says in his *Book on the Soul* that some are found who do not grant the mastery of Alchemy, saying that from those things which cannot endure the fire, nothing can be procreated that might sustain it. To these we respond by the reasoning of nitre <note>term: "nitrum" (nitre/saltpeter); here used metaphorically or chemically to describe a substance resistant to fire</note>; for earth is entirely removed by fire, yet by contact with fire it emerges stronger, because fire has no power over nitre.
**DEMOCRITUS:** And yet salt-nitre is very combustible.
**GEBER:** Avicenna understands the aforementioned nitre through a figure of speech which we call Antithesis <note>original: "Anthitesin"</note>, by changing the first letter, as one might say "illi" for "olli" <note>A linguistic example of archaic Latin substitution to show how terms can be encoded or hidden</note>. The eastern region where precious stones are found is closest to the Sun; likewise, soda <note>original: "nitrum"; here referring to natron or mineral alkali used in glassmaking</note> becomes stronger the more directly it is touched by the Sun. For this reason, this stone will be all the better the more it endures the heat of the fire and the other labors of this operation.
*Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century scholar and patron saint of scientists</note> in book 4, tract 3, chapter 16* says that although certain things are corruptible in fire, they are nevertheless not combustible because their moisture is not fuel for the fire—such as stones and iron. Some of these things do not receive the fire because of the constriction of their pores, and therefore they do not burn; but these things must be understood as referring to the aforementioned glass. We also said at the end of the preface of the *Book of Investigation*, and in the preface of *Book 2*, that glass is among those things which assist in preparation: for by reducing iron to the nature of prepared glass, much earthiness is removed. And in *Book 3, chapter 7*, we said: if we say that bodies are cleansed through calcination <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile matter</note>, we certainly mean the cleansing of iron from the earthy substance that is united in its very root. I was thinking of this glass when I said in *Book 1, in the discourse on sulfur*, that some, when they saw this deeper quality in it, purified it and removed its volatility and tendency to burn; it was rendered fixed and earthy, and through fire it yields no good fusion except a glass-like one.

**DEMOGORGON.** <note>The interlocutor in this dialogue</note> Perhaps the philosophers, when speaking of glass, understood it to be common glass. **GEBER.** <note>The famous alchemist Jabir ibn Hayyan, to whom this text is attributed</note> Common glass has neither the moisture, nor the sulfur, nor the quicksilver <note>mercury</note> that are compatible with metals. Therefore *Rhazes* <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi, a Persian polymath and physician</note> says that the ancients created a "metallic glass." And we said in *Book 1, chapter 11*, that the philosophers make glass from metallic bodies. *Rhazes* also says in the *Book of Divinity* that some have handed down the teaching that the tincture <note>the transformative agent or elixir</note> comes from iron and from no other thing, and that this stone is greater, cheap to find, and famous. **GEBER.** <note>The speaker is Geber, the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most famous figure in early chemistry/alchemy</note> It is true that the philosophers very often used equivocation <note>The use of ambiguous language to conceal secrets</note>; however, when you wish to distinguish and know which thing they are speaking of, you ought to examine the property of the things diligently, and thus you will easily notice when they mean either sulfur or the aforementioned lime. Alphidius <note>An Arabic alchemical author known in the Latin West</note>, speaking of this glass, says: "Break the glass, and extract the stone, and place it in a glass vessel or a flask, and extract the oil from it, and you will find that which the philosophers have handed down to us." In this glass is quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"</note> which overcomes fire, yet is not overcome by it, as we said openly in Book 3, Chapter 6, near the end. Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville, an influential medieval encyclopedist</note> speaks in almost the same vein: "Quicksilver," he says, "is better preserved in glass vessels, for it eats through others." We also said in Book 2, Chapter 6, that only glass and things similar to it, since they have no pores, are able to contain the spirits which flee and are driven out by fire. By these words I wished to indicate that the spirits of iron flee intense fire until they are converted into a glass of sapphire or dark celestial blue color. Therefore, in Book 4, Chapter 16, I said that in prepared bodies we ought to discern a celestial color which becomes perfect on account of the goodness of the quicksilver. Likewise, Albert <note>Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century scholar and saint who wrote extensively on minerals</note> secretly confirms these things shortly before the end of his book *On Meteors* <note>original: "liber Meteororum"</note> with these words; for he says that we cannot easily know when

-># DIALOGUE<-

metals have already acquired their specific form or not: for it is not easily known when it is already a true stone or a metal, unless they are led through corruption to a great weakness, to such an extent that nothing remains there except the mere shape, and nothing of the power <note>original: "uirtute"; refers to the inherent medicinal or alchemical efficacy of a substance</note> which follows the essence. Indeed, these are compared to dead bodies; for if these have been dead for a short time, they seem to have little difference from living bodies, but when buried in a tomb for a long time, then only the shape remains in them, and if they are touched, they suddenly collapse into ashes, and then a great difference appears between living and dead bodies. The same is to be understood concerning vegetables and minerals. For minerals also die in their own way, just as other animals do, yet it is not easily recognized when they have lost their being and nature, except by a very great change. For as long as a Sapphire <note>original: "Zaphirus"</note> possesses the color, transparency, and shape of a Sapphire, it is like a living thing and one that possesses the operation <note>The active influence or "work" a gemstone was thought to exert on its surroundings</note> of a Sapphire. But if, after a long change, it becomes darkened and broken apart, we know that it is no longer a Sapphire, but a likeness of a Sapphire; the same is to be understood concerning living and dead gold, and the same concerning silver and other metals.

**DEMOCRITUS.** I ask that you do not find it burdensome to explain to me in three words those things which you have treated in such a long speech. **GEBER.** This is the sum of the whole opinion: that you should understand we wished to indicate by that speech that when iron has endured in the fire for such a long space of time that it has now been made very weak and fragile, and clearly nothing of the nature of nitre <note>term: "nitri" (nitre/saltpeter); here likely referring to the volatile "salt" or life-force believed to be within the metal</note> remains there, and it has acquired a dark Sapphire-like color, it is only then, as it were, converted into the **Philosophers' Stone**. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins of matter, specifically focusing on the mythological figure of Demogorgon as an allegory for mineral substances.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

[The primordial being is] surrounded on every side by darkness, clouds, and gloom, dwelling in the very bowels of the earth. There he was born, clothed in a certain green mantle sprinkled with moisture, breathing out a terrestrial odor, horrible and foul-smelling; not fathered by anyone, but eternal and the parent of all things. 

**DEM.** Although these things seem very obscure to me, I nevertheless desire to hear the explanation of this myth. 

**GEB.** The name Demogorgon is Greek <note>original: "Demogorgonis uocabulũ Græcum"</note>, interpreted in English as "God of the Earth," "God of the People," or "Terrible God," and it signifies **Iron** <term>ferrũ: iron, here used in an alchemical sense to represent the base metal or strength of the earth</term>. He is called the great-great-grandfather or the ancestor of all the pagan gods; for all have drawn their origin from him and are derived from him (as we have already said). He is surrounded by clouds and gloom—that is, by terrestrial substance—and dwells in the middle of the earth's bowels because he was born and nourished underground. They say he is eternal and generated by no one, for from him all things that have a likeness to any generated thing are produced; for this reason, they called him the parent of all things. He is said to be eternal either because he existed before the earth and prior to all the elements, or rather because he contains within himself a certain incorruptible essence. He is clothed in a certain vile and abominable moisture—that is, rust <note>original: "ferrugine"</note>—and a certain green mantle—that is, with terrestrial vitriol <term>uitriolo: sulfate minerals, often green in color</term> and sulfur. He breathes a terrestrial odor, horrible and foul—that is, sulfurous—when placed in the fire, and with it a stinking water. 

To him (as Boccaccio writes in his book *On the Genealogy of the Pagan Gods* <note>Giovanni Boccaccio's "Genealogia Deorum Gentilium," a famous 14th-century encyclopedia of mythology</note>) the ancients attributed **Eternity** and **Chaos**. Eternity is the "Fifth Essence" <term>quinta essentia: the quintessence or purest element</term>, which is the radical moisture or **Quicksilver** <term>argentum uiuum: literally "living silver," or mercury</term>. Pliny also relates this in his 33rd book, saying it is an eternal liquid. Claudian <note>A Roman poet</note> calls this prepared quicksilver the **Serpent**, which in its coagulation devours its own tail; this refers to the Mercurial water of the quicksilver itself, distilled in the form of the tail of a Lion...

Truly, **Chaos** is that earthy salt which we previously named **Saturn** <note>In alchemy, Saturn usually represents lead or the heavy, "dead" state of matter at the beginning of the Work</note>; for it is a confused matter, and within it all things are contained without form—that is, the Sun, Moon, and stars, which are the aforementioned planets that possess no light of their own. Likewise, the four elements and all vegetable, animal, and mineral things are held within it. For this is the vegetative part, and from it all elements are born. This sulfur, however, seeks to be separated, since it is of a different nature and lacks saltiness; therefore, it opens the belly of Chaos—that is, it is dissolved in vinegar.

As we said above, Mars is elevated with Arsenic. # THE TREE OF LIFE,
## SECOND DIALOGUE.

->DEMOGORGON, RAYMOND.<-
->*<-

**DEM.** Greetings to you, most learned Raymond. **RAYM.** May it be well with you, excellent man, but I desire to know who you are or for what purpose you have come here. **DEM.** The distinguished and famous reputation of your wisdom, and your most learned writings, clearly demonstrate that you possess a great knowledge of all sciences; since indeed every man, and especially the elderly, uniquely desire to live as long as possible, I too—though weighed down by old age—directed my journey here from Italy to this island of Majorca <note>original: "Maioricam"; the home of the famous polymath Raymond Lully</note>, so that I might come into your presence and receive from you some certain remedy by which I might be able to prolong my life for some time further, and defend myself from death without any infirmity. **RAYM.** Are you unaware that God has established for every person the limit of their life,

that no one will be able to bypass? **DEMOGORGON.** I do not seek a remedy against that final boundary which is preordained for everyone by God, but rather that I might preserve myself from the many infirmities by which the fragile human body is daily afflicted, and that I might sustain myself against the consumption of the <term>radical moisture</term> <note>The "radical moisture" (humidum radicale) was believed by medieval physicians to be the vital fluid or "oil" that fueled the lamp of life.</note> and the loss of <term>natural heat</term> <note>Natural heat (calor naturalis) was the innate warmth of a living being; its extinction resulted in death.</note>. For I have persuaded myself that the <term>ancient fathers</term> <note>Refers to the Biblical patriarchs, such as Methuselah, who lived for centuries.</note>, who extended the limit of their lives to five hundred years, used some medicine through which they produced a life of such long duration without any illness. **RAYMOND.** The ancient fathers lived for a long time by the will of God, so that through their long lives the human race might be multiplied more quickly.

**DEMOGORGON.** Why, therefore, did they not all live for such a long time? **RAYMOND.** Those who followed those ancient fathers more closely in succession lived for a longer time, as they had progressed but little from God’s first creation; for they were of a good and praiseworthy <term>complexion</term> <note>In humoral medicine, "complexion" refers to the specific balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) within an individual.</note> and were long-lived. This goodness, however, was diminished by long succession, and the closer they were to the first creation, the longer they lived. **DEMOGORGON.** Therefore all of them ought to have lived for a long time, yet sacred scripture makes mention of only a few. **RAYMOND.** In the beginning, a year according to the Egyptian custom was not of such a long duration, nor did it contain twelve months as it does today. **DEMOGORGON.** In sacred scripture, mention is made not only of years, but of months as well; therefore they were not Egyptian years, but were defined according to the custom of the Hebrews, and were as long as they are today, as **Saint Augustine** affirms in his book *The City of God* <note>original: "de ciuitate"</note>. **RAYMOND.** The fruits and all things which spring from the earth were then better and of greater virtue for sustaining and prolonging life before the <term>Deluge</term> <note>The Great Flood of Noah.</note> than they are today. **DEMOGORGON.** Therefore everyone ought to have lived for a long time, which we certainly know was by no means the case.

DEMOCRITUS: It is surely impossible for anyone to have such a good, strong, and tempered constitution that they could reach the nine-hundredth year of their life without the help of **preventative medicine** <term>medicina conservativa: a branch of alchemy and medicine focused on prolonging life and maintaining the body's youthful vigor</term>. Adam, created from the earth and adorned with all knowledge, possessed an understanding of all natural things; he knew those substances by which life could be prolonged, and he made use of them. Passing these down to those who succeeded him, others following afterward received this knowledge from their hands. For this reason, not everyone was able to live such a long age, since the knowledge of this matter was not handed down to everyone. DEMOCRITUS: I ask of you, therefore—you who are imbued with all science—to show me this medicine which Adam passed to his posterity, through which they extended their lives to nine hundred years. RAYMOND: Preventative medicines must be very durable and far removed from corruption (as we have clearly written in the book *On the Secrets of Nature* <note>A famous alchemical work attributed to Lull</note>). For if they are to preserve the human body from decay, it is necessary that they themselves be perpetually durable; otherwise, they would induce even greater corruption. Therefore, wishing to preserve and extend youthful vigor, and to restore to the elderly the **radical moisture** <term>humidum radicale: the innate life-fluid believed to be consumed by the body's heat over time</term> and **natural heat** <term>calor naturalis: the vital warmth that sustains life</term>, it is necessary that we choose a substance more incorruptible than all others that exist beneath the sphere of the Moon. We must prepare this into a medicine and a most sweet food, so that it can be taken by mouth into the body; and once taken, it may penetrate the entire human body as swiftly as possible and preserve it as if it were incorruptible. <meta>This page continues a dialogue between Democritus and Raymond (likely Raymond Lull) regarding the nature of the "incorruptible medicine" or Elixir. The previous context likely established the possibility of a medicine that prevents decay.</meta>

# DIALOGUE

(To confess what I feel) it is impossible for those things which you have already said to be true: for all bodily things were created by God for human use, either the elements themselves or things composed of the elements <note>Latin: "elementata," referring to physical matter made from the four basic elements</note>, and all these are corruptible. From where, therefore, shall we obtain this incorruptible medicine? Furthermore, we see that everything which is generated is generated by its like (as Aristotle affirms in the 7th book of his *Metaphysics*) and whatever has been lost or consumed from human flesh must necessarily be restored from another corruptible thing—namely, from corruptible foods. These are broken down by the power of natural heat and transformed in the stomach, the liver, and all the limbs through the power of the nutritive faculty, so that they may be converted into human flesh. If, however, this medicine is incorruptible, it can never be transformed into human flesh, just as the matter of the heavens <note>In medieval cosmology, the celestial spheres were made of a "fifth element" or aether, which was eternal and could not change into earthly matter</note> can never be transformed into elemental matter.

**RAYMOND:** You have brought these points forward learnedly enough. But do you think that in things composed of elements, nothing is to be found except the corruptible elements themselves?

**DEMOCRITUS:** Show me, then, that which is mixed into elemental things besides the elements.

**RAYMOND:** This is a certain subtle substance, both the radical moisture <term>Radical moisture: a fundamental life-fluid in medieval medicine believed to be the "oil" that keeps the lamp of life burning</term> and the intrinsic essence, called the **Fifth Essence** <note>Latin: "quinta essentia," the quintessence</note>. It is diffused through the elemental parts; it is simple and entirely incorruptible. This substance preserves things in their being for a long time. Wise Nature produced and ordained this because she desires to preserve individuals for a long time and to produce things in their species forever. For although in "univocal generation" <note>The biological process where a parent produces an offspring of the same species</note> of animals every animal generates its like in species, nevertheless, in "ceaseless generation"—which occurs through putrefaction and the corruption of the generator—the thing generated becomes deformed from the generator in species or genus, as we see in food, from which is generated and restored that which [is consumed] by the vigor of intrinsic and extrinsic heat...

<meta>The catchword "absum" indicates the next page begins with the word "consumed" or "taken away."</meta>

<summary>Democritus argues that an incorruptible medicine is impossible because human biology requires corruptible food to repair flesh, but Raymond counters by introducing the "Fifth Essence" and "radical moisture" as the eternal, subtle substance hidden within physical matter.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Metaphysics, elements, incorruptible medicine, quintessence, radical moisture, digestion, generation, alchemy, Raymond Lull</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
is consumed and lost. Nor do we claim that this medicine is entirely incorruptible, like the Heavens, but since it is generated from matter more incorruptible than all others, and has been made simple through the separation of corruptible elements, it will—if properly preserved—last ten thousand years without decay. Furthermore, when taken by mouth, it will for a long time preserve the human body from corruption. For this reason, Physicians diligently urge that we restore ourselves with food that is less prone to decay.

**DEM.** This opinion of yours seems to me similar to the view of certain philosophers who maintained that the Salamander lives on fire alone, the herring indeed on water alone, the mole on earth, and the Chameleon on air. This opinion is false, for no elemental thing can live on a single element, but is preserved by those things from which it is composed (as Aristotle says). Therefore, man cannot sustain his life solely by that simple and so incorruptible radical moisture <note>original: "humido radicali"; a medieval medical concept referring to the innate fluid that sustains life, like the oil in a lamp</note>.

**RAYM.** I do not say that a man can live on this medicine alone, but that by its use alongside other nourishments, he can finally attain the longevity of the first fathers <note>The biblical patriarchs, such as Methuselah, who lived for centuries</note>, who used not only the Tree of Life in the earthly Paradise, but also other fruits.

**DEM.** Did the first fathers use this medicine?

**RAYM.** They most certainly must have used it to prolong their lives.

**DEM.** It is a wonder indeed that they had nothing else that could better defend them from death.

**RAYM.** Speaking naturally, this was the best and most excellent medicine, than which nothing more excellent exists in the whole world. For since (as Aristotle teaches in the 10th book of his *Metaphysics*) in every genus there is one certain thing which holds the first rank in that category, and since this medicine is generated from matter that is more incorruptible and of more excellent virtue than any you will find under the heavens, it shall hold the first or highest rank among all other preservative medicines.

<summary>A dialogue between Democritus and Raymond Lully regarding an alchemical medicine capable of preserving the human body for centuries by mimicking the "radical moisture" of the biblical patriarchs. Raymond argues that while not making one immortal like the stars, this substance is the highest form of preventative medicine in nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a dialogue between Demogorgon and Raymond Lully regarding the nature of the "Universal Medicine" or Philosopher's Stone, transitioning from theoretical origins to its physical composition.</meta>

-># DIALOGUE<-

**DEMOGORGON.** Is this medicine, then, the very Tree of Life? <note>original: "lignum vitae"</note>
**RAYMOND.** I told you before that the first fathers were deprived of this wood from the very beginning, as we read in the writings of Moses. <note>A reference to the expulsion from Eden in Genesis.</note>
**DEMOGORGON.** Perhaps even Moses—who from his earliest youth had been uprightly instructed in the sciences of the Egyptians and possessed knowledge of this divine science (as the monk Vincent <note>Likely Vincent of Beauvais, a famous medieval encyclopedist.</note> left in his writings)—spoke of these matters with obscurity, just as the other philosophers have done.
**RAYMOND.** I wish to detract nothing from his writings, for one should believe neither more nor less of him than is proper.
**DEMOGORGON.** Perhaps it is that herb with which Medea <note>The sorceress of Greek mythology who restored youth to her father-in-law Aeson.</note> restored Aeson to youth, and with which Aesculapius <note>The god of medicine.</note> used to revive those who were almost dead.
**RAYMOND.** It is indeed that medicine itself, but it should by no means be called an herb. For the ancient philosophers hid this science under poetic fables and spoke through metaphors.
**DEMOGORGON.** What metaphor do you mean?
**RAYMOND.** This medicine varies in many ways during its preparation: for it assumes a green color like an herb, and therefore the ancients called it "vegetable" and "the herb." For this reason, Meluedus, a disciple of Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>, says when discussing these matters: "O, how easy is this thing to vegetable things because of its greenness!"
**DEMOGORGON.** Since herbs have such great medicinal virtues, how is it that this Medicine cannot be an herb?
**RAYMOND.** This Medicine can be made neither from vegetable things, nor from animals, nor from those things which are taken from them.
**DEMOGORGON.** For what reason?
**RAYMOND.** Since it is necessary that this Medicine be entirely incorruptible <note>Meaning it cannot rot, decay, or be destroyed by fire.</note> and must surpass all elements in this regard, it is necessary that it be extracted from matter which is as far removed from all corruption as possible.
**DEMOGORGON.** Yet you said in the book you titled *On the Secrets of Nature* <note>original: "De secretis naturae," a famous alchemical work attributed to Lully.</note> that this must be extracted—

<meta>catchword: extracted</meta>

<summary>In this dialogue, Raymond Lully explains to Demogorgon that the "Medicine" (the Philosopher's Stone) is not a literal plant or animal substance, despite being called an "herb" by ancient philosophers to describe its green color during preparation. He argues that because the Medicine must be incorruptible, it must be derived from a mineral or metallic source rather than organic matter.</summary>

<keywords>Alchemy, Tree of Life, Moses, Medea, Aesculapius, Hermes Trismegistus, Green Lion, Incorruptibility, Secrets of Nature, Vegetable Stone</keywords>

# BOOK SECOND

<margin>The Red Medicine</margin>
...must be drawn from the "living red." John of Rupescissa <note>Jean de Roquetaillade (c. 1310–1366), a Franciscan friar and alchemist known for his work on quintessence.</note> confirms the same, but others assert that Celandine <note>original: "Chelidonia"; a yellow-flowered herb often associated with gold-making due to its color.</note> must be used, and others still, human blood.

**RAYMOND:** You err not a little if you are persuaded that the writings of the philosophers are to be understood from the bare letter. Especially in this science, the more clearly and openly they seem to speak, the more obscurity their writings contain; for they have spoken through similarities and through riddles.

**DEMOCRITUS:** In this matter, what similarity did they use?

**RAYMOND:** An elder philosopher says that this medicine is converted and varied from color to color, from flavor to flavor, and from nature to nature, and for that reason its names have been multiplied. Moreover, the philosopher Minois says: "If it is asked why it was made red before it received whiteness, the answer is that it is blackened twice, converted into a lemon-yellow color twice, and twice receives the red color." Since it indeed receives the red color twice—namely, like red wine and human blood after putrefaction and in distillation—the ancients, speaking through similarities, called it "red wine," "human blood," and "Dragon's blood," <note>A bright red resin used in alchemy and medicine.</note> and other things like these.

**DEMOCRITUS:** I entirely believed that animals, being more noble than all other inanimate things, were also of greater virtue in medicine.

**RAYMOND:** The excellence of animals consists not in the proportion and mixture of the elements, but in the soul. However, since this medicine must be made from a corporeal and material substance, certain minerals are more excellent for perfecting this work. For their composition is harder and stronger than that of animals; the composition of the latter, as far as the body is concerned, is weak, easily corrupted, and of little virtue. For we see that animals themselves, which are occupied with high and sublime things, are deprived of those things that are base and perishable; therefore...

...those minerals, which have no active function and lack sensitivity <note>original: "sensibilitate carentia"; referring to the lack of five senses in minerals compared to animals</note>, possess a greater bodily strength than animals themselves. **DEMOCRITUS:** If this medicine cannot be made from either vegetable or animal things, perhaps it must be made from intermediate minerals, such as salt, alum, vitriol <note>original: "atramento"; literally "blacking," usually referring to ferrous sulfate or ink-stone</note>, marcasite, antimony, sulfur, and common quicksilver <note>mercury</note>. **RAYMOND:** Not from these either. **DEMOCRITUS:** Yet they are very durable. **RAYMOND:** They are neither more virtuous than other elemental things, nor more incorruptible. **DEMOCRITUS:** By what things are they destroyed? **RAYMOND:** By the violence of fire. **DEMOCRITUS:** Fire indeed destroys, seizes, and devours all things. **RAYMOND:** It cannot, however, destroy the radical moisture <term>Radical moisture: the innate, life-sustaining fluid thought to exist in all things</term> in metals, nor their radical and complexional heat, as Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemy</note> affirms in his second book, and Albert <note>Albertus Magnus, 13th-century philosopher and scientist</note> in his third book on minerals, chapter 2. **DEMOCRITUS:** For what reason? **RAYMOND:** Because of their homogeneity and their very hard and strong composition and binding, and because of their vaporous mixture and the very long and most temperate decoction <note>the "cooking" or ripening process of metals within the earth</note> in the mine or the bowels of the earth. **DEMOCRITUS:** Do you conclude, therefore, that this medicine ought to be made from the radical moisture and heat of metals? **RAYMOND:** We see metals that have lain hidden, buried in the earth for more than a thousand years in the antiquities of Rome, remaining uncorrupted and having suffered no loss; from this you may easily conjecture how long their radical moisture might last once purified, distilled, and separated from every corruptible, coarse, and elemental part. For this reason Geber says in book 2, chapter 12: "We see distilled things become purer and more easily preserved from putrefaction." **DEMOCRITUS:** From where do you think metals obtain such great power and virtue over all elemental things? **RAYMOND:** From heaven. **DEMOCRITUS:** Yet hard things receive celestial virtue the least, because they do not obey heaven; for...

->SECOND BOOK<-

We see that a seal does not impress its shape into hard stone, but rather into soft wax. <note>Raymond Lully, a 13th-century philosopher and alchemist.</note> RAYMOND: The rays of the celestial bodies are united with no element with such great virtue and power as they are in the earth; for the earth is the proper and firm receptacle of celestial virtues and the very center of the sphere. For this reason, the earth produces wonderful vegetables, **animals, and minerals.** Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> says that the earth is the parent and mother of metals, which she carries in her belly just as a pregnant woman carries a fetus in the womb. He says, moreover, that the earth is the mother while the sky is the father, and that the earth is impregnated by the sky in the mountains, the plains, the water, and in every place. However, it must be granted that while the earth remains in its hardness, it can produce nothing, since it is not obedient to motion. But when the earth is continually "cooked" by the constant celestial motion and the virtue of the sun and other planets, it becomes finer and is altered, and its more subtle parts are raised up into a smoke <note>original: "fumum"; in alchemy, this refers to "exhalations" or vapors that form minerals.</note> by the power of the sun's heat. Therefore, the celestial virtues—continually descending from heaven and penetrating into the earth—find there a material that is simple, most subtle, spiritual, incorruptible, bright, and clear. This material, prepared and made fit by the wisdom of nature to receive celestial virtues because of a certain likeness they share, infuses a most excellent power into that double metallic, radical smoke. And since this spiritual matter has already remained for a very long time in celestial obedience and spirituality within the substance of the principles of metals, it is uniform and homogeneous. As Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential 8th-century chemist/alchemist.</note> says in Book 1, Chapter 7, it is finally hardened over a very long time, even up to a thousand years. For this reason, the heavens infuse into these metals more wonderful virtues than into all other things, for nature has labored more in these than in others; and this is the cause of such great virtue in metals that all the elements...

easily overcome these things. Although indeed, after a very long time, this double smoke and spiritual vapor <note>original: "fumus duplex & uapor spiritualis," referring to the alchemical theory of sulfur and mercury vapors forming metals</note> has already reached a metallic hardness and retains within itself a heavenly virtue <term>virtue: the inherent power or medicinal efficacy of a substance</term>, it is nevertheless hindered and suffocated by earthiness and hardness. It cannot outwardly demonstrate this virtue acquired from the heavens. We, however, desiring that this power be made manifest and wishing to lead it into the light, find it necessary to remove the earthiness and density of the metal. You will be able to easily weigh for yourself how much power this medicine possesses if you consider Noah, who held this secret (as the monk Vincent writes in book 5 of his *Natural History* <note>Referring to Vincent of Beauvais, a 13th-century encyclopedist</note>), and was still fathering Shem, Ham, and Japheth at five hundred years of age.

**DEMOCRITUS:** This discourse has delighted me greatly, but I ask this: can this medicine be made from gemstones?

**RAYMOND:** Why do you ask this?

**DEMOCRITUS:** Because they are incorruptible and possess great force and medicinal, heavenly virtue, by which they even surpass the metals. They also said that this virtue descends upon natural things by flowing and influencing them more or less; for the heavens impress their power more strongly when the matter is more similar to the heavenly virtue and to the celestial bodies themselves in light and transparency. It impresses less, however, when the materials are confused and drossy, so that the heavenly virtue is overwhelmed within them. Therefore, since gemstones are more similar to the celestial bodies in substance, light, color, and transparency than the metals themselves—for which reason they are even called "elemental stars" by certain Philosophers—

Furthermore, nature coagulated gemstones with little artifice, and hardened them with that transparency and clarity which they possessed in their first smoky matter. # SECOND BOOK

How can a medicine for the human body be made from these metallic waters, since they are most foul-smelling and horrible, corrosive, and so sharp that they are considered by the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" usually refers to ancient authorities and alchemists like Aristotle or Hermes Trismegistus.</note> to be a most immediate poison? Morienus the Roman <note>A legendary 7th-century alchemist said to have taught the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>, speaking of their odor, compares their stench to the graves of dead corpses. We also see that those waters which flow through metallic veins, and are heated within them, acquire an unpleasant odor; therefore, this medicine will be much more horrible and more abominable to human nature when the metallic substance itself has been converted into water.

**RAYMOND** <note>Likely referring to Raymond Lull, though many alchemical works attributed to him are pseudepigraphal.</note>: Just as fruits at the beginning of summer are bitter and harsh because they have not yet had sufficient boiling <term>decoction: the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence</term> and ripening <term>digestion: in alchemy, a process of slow, gentle heating over many days</term>, and by continually drawing in moisture they cannot sufficiently mature and be sweetened unless they are boiled and ripened by the great heat of the entire summer so that they become sweet and fragrant; the same happens to our medicine extracted from the earth of metals. For this too, before sufficient ripening and boiling, is foul and horrible; and before the vapors have ascended from it through boiling, it cannot be sweetened. But once the vapors are coagulated and sufficiently boiled, it acquires a wonderful pleasantness and sweetness. For this reason, Arnold said in the *Rosary* <note>Arnold of Villanova, a 13th-century physician; the *Rosarium Philosophorum* is a famous alchemical treatise attributed to him.</note>: "You shall sweeten the bitter, and you shall have the entire mastery" <note>original: "dulcorabis amarum, & habebis totum magisterium." The 'mastery' refers to the completion of the Great Work.</note>.

**DEM.** <note>The student or interlocutor in the dialogue.</note>: Is there any sign by which I may know that this medicine is now finished?

**RAYMOND**: John of Rupescissa <note>A 14th-century Franciscan friar and alchemist known for his work on "quintessence."</note> handed down what we also have affirmed: namely, that there are two certain signs or indications by which it is known that this medicine is now perfected and finished. The first is this: if we place the vessel in which this medicine is enclosed in some corner of the house, then by a miracle and as if by an invisible obstacle, all who enter that house are drawn and enticed toward it. The other, truly, is if we place the aforementioned small vessel upon

the top of the tower, so that its scent may attract and gather there all the birds that perceive that sweetness of odor; these are the most certain signs that the Medicine is now finished and entirely complete. **DEMOCRITUS:** What did John of Rupescissa <note>Jean de Roquetaillade (c. 1310–1366), a Franciscan friar and alchemist known for his works on quintessence.</note> wish to signify by these words? **RAYMOND:** By the tower, we understand the furnace placed in the corner of the house, in which the glass vessel containing the matter of our Medicine is placed to be heated. When these are fixed and ascend no further, it is a sign that their watery humidity is now consumed, the matter is sweetened, and the Medicine is finished. You should not doubt this interpretation, for Rhazes <note>original: "Rafis"; refers to Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 864–925), a Persian polymath and influential figure in the history of medicine and alchemy.</note> holds the same opinion. This is also affirmed to us by Giovanni Pico della Mirandola in his book *On the Dignity of Man*, when he says that the ancients were accustomed to describe deep and sublime divine matters under the veil of many enigmas and poetic fables. **DEMOCRITUS:** Can this Medicine heal all curable diseases? <note>Original text reads "mobos", likely a typo for "morbos" (diseases).</note> **RAYMOND:** We have said above that this Medicine is established in the highest degree and the first order. # BOOK ONE

...powers, except that the more spiritual and formal it is—and the more it is separated from matter and removed from quantity—the more it extends its power to produce various effects. Our medicine, however, since it is composed of the most subtle spirits and simple matter, and is as if separated from all elemental matter, can therefore easily exert its strengths without impediment in all infirmities that are capable of receiving a cure. We likewise know that every active agent has the power of operating according to its proximity and similarity to that with which it participates, namely the First Active Principle. The first and universal corporeal and simple agents are the celestial bodies, which, as a universal cause, extend themselves over all inferior things. Since our medicine, because of its subtlety, purity, and incorruptibility, has the greatest proximity and similarity to the celestial bodies above all other bodies, it has been called "Heaven" and the "Fifth Essence" <term>Quinta Essentia: the fifth element beyond earth, water, air, and fire, believed to be the pure substance of the stars</term> by the Philosophers. It will therefore be able, above all other inferior corporeal things, to cure all curable diseases and infirmities as a universal medicine—not only those which pertain to the Physician <note>Internal medicine</note>, but even those which require the hand of the Surgeon.

Physicians also teach that the medication *Hiera Picra* <term>Hiera Picra: a traditional bitter purgative made of aloes and herbs, literally "holy bitter"</term> has a singular power in drawing out humors from the head, neck, and chest, and not from other lower members. This is because this medication is generated through the power of certain stars which have influence over the head, neck, and chest, such as the stars of Aries, Taurus, and Gemini. They assert the same regarding other medications which draw humors from the feet, hip, and shins: because these medicines have obtained a certain special power and a specific influence over these lower members, namely from the stars of Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces. Metals...

He explains that while specific drugs like Hiera Picra are tied to specific zodiac signs, this medicine transcends those limitations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the medicinal properties of metals and their astrological correspondences, following the discussion of planetary influences on the human body.</meta>

->D I A L O G U E<-

indeed receiving the essence, name, number, color, power, and unique property of the planets. Since all planets have influence and power over the entire human body, and our medicine is generated from these metals, it too will possess power over the entire human body. Furthermore, if thermal waters <note>natural hot springs</note> have the power to heal many infirmities for the reason that they carry with them a certain metallic virtue while passing through veins of metal, then surely the metallic substance itself will have even greater strength when converted into a drinkable form. 

However, according to the opinion of the ancient Philosophers, individual metals are comprised within every other metal according to likeness, power, name, color, and property—as was clearly demonstrated in the book of the *Exposition of the Books of Geber* <note>referring to the works of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of alchemy</note>. Therefore, even if our medicine has been extracted from only one of the metals, it will nonetheless possess the power of all metals and planets, and the power to heal many curable infirmities throughout the entire human body. 

Johannes de Rupescissa <note>a 14th-century Franciscan alchemist known for his work on "quintessence"</note> intended to signify the same thing when speaking of our radical moisture and the quintessence <term>Quinta Essentia: the "fifth element" or purest medicinal extract</term> under the name of the "water of life" <note>original: *aqua vitae*</note>. For he says that good "burning water" <note>distilled alcohol or strong solvent</note> contains within itself the virtue of all metals, and that it is not the water of the vine, but of life, because it grants life to men.

**DEMOCRITUS** <note>The speaker is likely the character representing the ancient philosopher in this dialogue</note>
Although these reasons are brought forward quite prudently and sharply, I am nonetheless persuaded that a single simple medicine can by no means cure all infirmities. I hold this primarily for the following reason: because contrary infirmities (as Physicians assert) are cured by contrary medicines. Since contrary qualities cannot exist in a single subject, it seems plainly impossible to me that one medicine can cure many different infirmities. Tra— <note>catchword for the next page: "tradunt" or "they hand down"</note>

<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between Demogorgon and Raymond Lully regarding the nature of the universal medicine and its diverse effects.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

Furthermore, the Philosophers <note>original: "Philosophi"; likely referring to the Scholastic authorities or Aristotelians</note> claim that from one single thing, nothing can be caused except for one single effect; therefore, neither will one single medicine cure many diseases. **RAYMOND:** A single simple thing produces many contrary effects according to the diverse nature and disposition of the recipient. For we see that the heat of the sun works a diverse effect, as it dries out mud but liquefies wax, even though these contrary effects arise from one single operation. Therefore, although our medicine is one single thing, and perhaps has one single operation, nevertheless, if we consider that great similarity which it has with all celestial things, we see that it is suited to receiving many celestial virtues <note>The "virtues" refer to the spiritual or astrological influences of the stars and planets</note>. Since it has also been extracted from all the elements, we say that it possesses the virtue and quality of all the elements; for that reason, it is of manifold power and is able to work various contrary effects. **DEMOGORGON:** Show me now the practice <note>original: "praxin"; the practical laboratory application of the theory</note> of this art. **RAYMOND:** Read our exposition of the books of Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of Arabic alchemy</note>, for there you will find both the matter and the practice described distinctly and clearly. **DEMOGORGON:** I give you the greatest thanks for such a great benefit bestowed upon me out of your singular generosity and kindness; and may you fare well in the meantime.

->THE END.<-

<summary>Raymond Lully explains how a single alchemical medicine can cure multiple ailments by comparing it to the sun's heat and concludes the dialogue by directing the student to his commentaries on Geber.</summary>
<keywords>universal medicine, celestial virtues, four elements, Geber, alchemy practice, Raymond Lully, Demogorgon</keywords>
